About this text

The King of Tantras, the Glorious Kālacakra, Issued from the Supreme Primordial Buddha, known in Sanskrit as the Paramādibuddhoddhṛta Śrī Kālacakra Nāma Tantrarāja and in Tibetan as mchog gi dang po'i sangs rgyas las phyung ba rgyud kyi rgyal po dpal dus kyi 'khor lo, is the root scripture of the Kālacakra, or "Wheel of Time," tradition. It opens with King Sucandra bowing before the Buddha, seated on a lion throne, and requesting teachings on the yoga of Kālacakra for the liberation of beings. The tantra presents its vision through interrelated outer, inner, and alternative dimensions. The outer dimension concerns the cosmos and the cycles of time, the inner concerns the subtle body of channels, winds, and drops, and the alternative concerns the practice of initiation, meditation, and the realization of empty form and immutable bliss. It is Toh 362 in the Kangyur, where it belongs to the tantra section among the highest yoga tantras, and it has long been regarded as one of the most comprehensive and influential tantric scriptures in Tibetan Buddhism.

Translation

In the language of India: parama ādi buddha uddhṛta śrī kāla cakra nāma tantra rājā. In the language of Tibet: The King of Tantras, the Glorious Kālacakra, Issued from the Supreme Primordial Buddha.

Homage to the glorious Kālacakra.

The Omniscient One, whose body is wisdom and whose body is the sun, whose eyes are like full-blown lotus petals, the Buddha seated on the lion throne, to whom the supreme gods bow: to him King Sucandra bowed deeply with his head, placed his joined lotus hands upon his crown, and made this request: "The yoga of glorious Kālacakra, which has the garland of ka and is gathered in a, for the sake of freeing human beings;

emptiness, and wisdom too; the supreme of supreme drops; the vajra holder; the buddhas; the gods and also the asuras;
the outer, the body, and the other as well; the self that is the twenty-fifth person among the natures;
the various measures within the body; the arrangement of the three existences; the enjoyments of the gods and asuras;
the maṇḍala and the empowerment: O guru of the Thirty gods and of humans, please explain these fully."

"Sucandra, I am pleased with you, for what you have asked is not known to the supreme gods,
to humans, rākṣasas, asuras, or nāgas, nor to the lines of passionless supreme sages.
All the practices, from nirvāṇa down to the earth, together with the course of words,
in the middle of the body, and the maṇḍalas and the empowerment, I shall explain. Listen, excellent lord of men!

Through time, from the voids, wind, the blazing one, water, and earth arise, the continents, mountains, and oceans,
the lunar mansions, the sun and moon, the stars, the hosts of planets, the sages, gods, bhūtas, and nāgas,
the animals of four modes of birth, the various humans on the earth and below, and the hells as well.
They arise in the midst of the void, like salt in water, and like the egg-born within the egg.

Body and wisdom, space, wind, fire, water and earth, the stable and the moving,
the gods, the seen, and the unproduced, said to be ten kinds of existence: from the vajra body
arise the birthplaces of mantra, and, lord of men, right here they are freed once more.
Whoever truly knows it thus is no beast; he is freed from the conceptions of the mind.

In the left limb is white, blazing, the portion of the nectar of beings; on the right, the color red.
Rāhu, Kālāgni, the Moon, the Sun, the son of the hare-marked one, the earth-born one, the white one, the guru,
the long-tailed one, the slow one, rain, lightning, water, and the crested one: these blazing ones are seven pairs.
They abide in the world, and a single pair without darkness is at the end of darkness.

At the back is yellow as well, the stars and the rainbow; that is by the division of two fours.
The single wind called life becomes tenfold, at the crown, the root, and in front.
Above the central vajra the seed, together with its own power and its host of qualities, abides below.
Everywhere it is wisdom and emptiness, together with the abode of peace, free of all entities.

The thirty vowels beginning with a, which are joined with ha ya ra va la, are the digits of the moon and the sun as well. The classes beginning with ka, together with the vowels, move through a day and night by the measure of six thousand fires. Those beginning with ha, and the vowels too, are the hours, and the birthplaces of asuras, hooded ones, and bhūtas, O knower of mantra. Thus from those joined with a and the rest and ka and the rest, the three realms are surely produced, lord of men.

The vowels joined with ka ca ṭa pa ta, and thka and the rest, are the birthplaces of the conjuncts. The throat, the palate, and the upper part arise from space, wind, and fire; the lips and teeth are water and earth. Those beginning with a are, in order, at the uṣṇīṣa wheel and the wheels of the heart, throat, head, and navel, together with the variegated, the black, the red, the hare-marked white, and the gold-like, with the aggregates, the elements, and the presiding deities.

From the edge of wind to the boundary of Meru, the hells and the cities of the hooded ones span two hundred thousand yojanas. The measure of Meru is one hundred thousand; from the abode of the hosts of planets, twenty-five thousand; the throat; on the face, fifty; and likewise twenty-five to the stable station of the unmoving. Outside it is emptiness alone, free of the three existences, without qualities, and devoid of the principles.

From the edge of wind to the boundary of wind and the stable earth base, the continents, mountains, and oceans
measure half of four, two hundred thousand; the fire and wind maṇḍalas measure two hundred thousand yojanas.
In the center is Meru, above which the wheel of the houses, with the stars, revolves by day and night,
two hundred thousand in each of six parts. All the three existences are born from the conjunctions of time.

The full circumference of the world sphere is three times its diameter.
The circumference of the earth is three hundred thousand, and of water, fire, and wind, six, nine, and twelve in order.
Lord of men, the one hundred thousand yojanas in the abode of the outer three existences
is, in the place above the earth, within the body, exactly one cubit by each one's own measure.

Eight extremely fine particles make one fine particle; eight fine particles make a hair tip. By these come a mustard seed, a louse, and a barley grain, in turn; barley grains make a finger width, and twelve finger widths twice over make a cubit; four cubits make a bow; and here two thousand bows make a krośa. Four krośas make a yojana; this is the measure for the realms of the gods, the earth, and the sky.

First, Good Dharma, the pair of eons, those possessed of pairs, Brahmā, and Beyond the World,
the Glorious Eon and the White Eon too; from the abode of Paranirmitavaśavartin, the desire realm is eleven.
The formless are the four beginning with a, free of ha and of sexual union; the sixteen beginning with i are form.
Those from ha up to la are the desire realm, clearly tenfold, and the single consonant is ka sa.

By the thirty-one existences, here they become the three existences; by the division of realms, the three realms. All these three existences, without remainder, become the three vajras, by the division into a and the rest and ka and the rest. From Ratnaprabhā onward: Gravel Water is declared a hell; Sand Water is the second;

Mud Water, Unbearable Smoke, Fire, Darkness, and Wailing, the seventh.

Salt, beer, water, milk, curd, ghee and honey are the seven oceans; and the mountains
are Blue Light, Mount Mandara, Niṣadha, Jewel Light, Droṇa, Cool Mountain and Vajra.
The continents are Moon, White Light, supreme Kuśa, Kiṃnara, Krauñca,
and also Fierce, lands of enjoyment; the seventh, Jambu, where humans dwell, is the land of karma.

In the east, wind is perfectly round; O lord of men, in the south, fire is triangular.
In the north, water is a half-moon; in the west, earth of supreme gold has corners numbering the oceans.
Meru, empty in form, of the nature of the supreme vajra, stands at the center of the maṇḍalas;
above the earth, its thousands of yojanas are mountains, nāgas, planets and directions.

Meru's breadth at the top and at the level of the ground, in thousands of yojanas,
is fifty and sixteen; and one is the true measure of its circumference at the supreme base of the earth.
Its five peaks, at the top and at the level of the ground, encircle it in every direction.
Outside it are the continents, the mountains and the oceans, and in every direction fire and wind.

All six oceans, too, are each thousands diminished by moon, moon and one;
the continents and the supreme mountain ranges, too, are each such thousands, twenty-five.
The breadth of Jambudvīpa and the ocean of salt water measure half a hundred thousand,
and so likewise are the measures of the fire and the wind at the outer limits that hold the three existences.

In the abode of Brahmā, Kālacakra with the supreme Victors is truly praised by the host of gods; on Meru are the hosts of the Word-Arrows, and the nāgas dwelling beneath the ground are of five families. In all directions around glorious Meru, upon the maṇḍala of the earth, are all the sacred seats (pīṭhas) and secondary seats, the fields and gathering places, the meeting places and the charnel grounds, at the limits of the fire and wind quarters.

In the east is Śakra (Indra); in the fire quarter, Agni, the god of fire; in the quarters of Yama, of Nairṛti and of the west are Yama, the rākṣasa and the Water god; in the quarters of wind, of the Wealth-Giver and of Hara are Vāyu, the yakṣa and Raudra; and above and below are Brahmā and Viṣṇu; bearing weapons, together with their retinues of beings, each guards and protects his own direction. In their center is Kālacakra, progenitor of the supreme Victors, of indestructible vajra body.

In the directions behind Meru, upon the ground, is a retinue hard for even the asuras to conquer.
Wherever the Dharma declines and the age of strife sets in, there they journey forth.
Having vanquished the barbarians (mlecchas) in battle and established them in their own Dharma, they range before him.
The Kṛta, Tretā and Dvāpara, and the other, the age of strife, come about through the conjunctions of time.

In whatever segment the powerful wheel-bearer has entered, there the Kṛta age proceeds;
behind the king is the Tretā, and the age of strife is in front; between the two is the Dvāpara.
The measure of the ages in years is twenty-one thousand together with six hundred;
the measure of each is a hundred human years multiplied by time and arrow.

Breaths, dhru, water-hours, days, dhru and the four ages are twenty-one thousand and six hundred; in order, these are the measure of a single day of the subtle body-born, of humans, bhūtas, gods and asuras, and of Power and the Lord. On the earth the four ages enter as the measure of Power. Segment by segment, the wheel-bearer of the peaceful state proceeds; in a single day there are twelve suns.

Fortnights, all the months, seasons, the four ages, and the twofold course of the fire-time;
the years of the king, all in sequence, the days, the conjunctions, the water-hours and the water-measures:
all these proceed in this way in each single day of the subtle body-born, of humans, bhūtas, gods and asuras,
and of Power and the Lord; and so it is likewise in the abode of the three existences and in the center of the body.

From this year, after six hundred years, the lord of men Yaśas will clearly appear in the place called Śambhala.
Then, after eight hundred years, the religion of the barbarians (mlecchas) will surely enter the land of Makha (Mecca).
At that time, people on earth should clearly understand this short calculation.
Across the whole earth, the astronomical systems (siddhāntas) will decline in the course of time.

To four hundred and three add the elapsed years of the cycle beginning with Prabhava; this establishes the barbarian year. Take the year of the barbarian lord, less one hundred and eighty-two, and multiply what remains by twelve. Add the months beginning with Caitra. Below, multiply by six and divide by one hundred and thirty. When the quotient is added above, O lord of men, the sum of months is purified.

Place the sum of months in three positions. Multiply whatever is in the middle heap by thirty-two.
At the root, find the sixth part. This becomes a debt, to be subtracted from the middle.
To the upper and lower add the credit of two and thirty, and make it clear by parts of sixty.
Add the quotient above. Divide by the seven supreme sages, and the remainder becomes the weekday.

From the two positions where sun and moon are combined, take all that is obtained in parts by six and by twelve, with five added. Give this to the heap above it. First multiply by two, divide by eight and two, and sum. Multiply the middle of the three positions by eleven. To the lower, take the parts obtained by nine and three. Into the middle, diminished by the two combined, what is obtained in ghaṭikās is credit. To the upper heap, multiply by two

and give it, then divide by the lunar mansions. The remainder, O lord of men, becomes the sun's lunar mansion and the rest, and its traversed portion (bhoga). To the weekday give the weekday. O lord of men, double the ghaṭikās; below, to the sums, two again. To the lunar mansion give two, and to the ghaṭikās eleven, clearly as credit. Give these to the sun's traversed portion. These become the determination of the month, and further, it is taught that month is to be given to month.

The lunar days are given, removed and given again among the weekdays, the ghaṭikās and the steps. In the parts of the sum, the meaning of the steps is giving and removing, according to whether the quotient by fourteen is even or odd. At zero the value is exactly zero. At thirteen and at the step of one, five. At two and twelve, ten. At eleven and three, fifteen. At ten and the step of four, twenty less one.

At five and nine, twenty-two. At six and eight, twenty-four. At the seventh, twenty-five. Made clear there by the division of the sun, the steps of the sun are given and removed. Five and five, five and four, three, two and one: for the former part and also for the other. In the parts of the sum, according to whether the quotient by fourteen is even or odd, the moving steps are given and removed.

Because it moves and exists, from the multiplied remainder the parts obtained by six and by twelve are likewise taken.
On that very figure, by the division of the sun, the ghaṭikās of the sun's steps are given and removed.
Whatever lunar days are multiplied by four are clearly credit, combined with a third of themselves.
Six lunar mansions with three quarters are debt, to be subtracted from the middle of the sun's traversed portion.

The procedure is as before for Mars. One hundred and sixty-six are the steps for the slow correction. Multiply the ghaṭikās by the traversed portion, take the parts obtained by one hundred and thirty-five, together with the traversed portion. According to the course of the sun's progress, these are given to or removed from the sun; there is no other purification. For the sun: six, six, eight and ten, eleven, eleven, eleven, ten, eight and six,

and three and zero as well, up to the end of the course. In the north and the south these become credit and debt.
Through the divisions of the moving months, by the division of the directions, the day-maker travels for six months.
The count of lunar days given to the sun, below multiplied by six, combined with the planet's traversed portion,
when diminished, becomes the hare-marked moon. O lord of men, sun and moon combined are the yoga.

The karaṇas are whatever remains, in parts of seven, of the lunar days multiplied by two, less one.
To the purified year add eight; multiply by four hundred; reduce by that to which eight was added.
Divide by three hundred and seventeen; multiply by sixty, and divide by seven and the rest: these are the ghaṭikās.
Here, with the fixed weekday and the ghaṭikās of two and thirty, the months shift by the year.

For Aries and the rest, the addends of weekday and ghaṭikā, arranged in order, are these twelve:
one, seven, five, four, and three, five, one, and seven, one, for the third;
four, three, five, one, two, and two, six, and four, two, for the sixth;
six, and one, five, and one, seven, six, three, and six, one, four, seven, and three,

six, and four, zero, zero, two, five, for the sun's station in the house of the Fish.
For six months they wax and wane, the days and the nights alike, as the sun travels.
Four breaths and three palas wax and wane at each day, by the portion of the course.
In the south the nights increase, while north of the Snowy Mountains the days increase.

For the months, add two and twelve, multiply by two, and add one to the lunar junctures. Divide by four hundred and sixty. From the multiplication by twenty-seven, by parts of zero and the rest, comes the lunar mansion. Whatever remainder is multiplied by sixty gives the ghaṭikās; multiplied again, it gives the palas. The circle diminished by what is obtained becomes the head, and the tail of the dark one (Rāhu) is that with half the lunar mansions added.

Multiply the months by thirty, together with the individual days, and divide by seven hundred and seven. Combined in the middle of the three positions, what is obtained by sixty-four, diminished, gives the unclear days. Added to one hundred and sixty-seven, these are the sums of clear days. Divide by six hundred and eighty-seven; multiply the remainder by twenty-seven to obtain the quotient; multiply again by sixty, divide by six hundred and eighty-seven and the rest: these are the ghaṭikās.

For Mars, subtract nine and a half. If the negative is greater, add a full circle and then subtract. If it exceeds the half, discard half the circle; the remainder of what is discarded is then in reverse order. Multiply the lunar mansions by sixty, combine them with the nāḍīs (the sixty divisions of a day), and divide by one hundred thirty-five. What is obtained is for the slow equation, and its positive and negative go in reverse and in direct order.

Twenty-five, eighteen and seven are clearly the six steps over the half circle, ascending and descending. From the nāḍīs multiplied by the portion still to be traversed, the part obtained from the half circle by one hundred thirty-five Is held to be added in the first half, according to the step; likewise, in the latter half it is to be subtracted. Having summed what has been traversed, the additions and subtractions made according to the planet's course are also applied to the nāḍīs.

Then subtract the earth-born one (Mars) from the sun; the guru (Jupiter) and the sun-born one (Saturn) likewise, for the fast equation. For the moon-born one (Mercury) and the white one (Venus), it is the sun; for the fast equation it certainly becomes positive, in order. Here the remainder of that subtraction gives the steps; multiply the remaining nāḍīs by the steps still to be traversed. What is obtained as a sixtieth part, from the steps still to be traversed, is likewise to be added or taken away.

Having summed what has been traversed, all the additions and subtractions are made according to the planet's course. For the earth-born one the steps are twenty-four and so on; all of them should be set out clearly in fourteen places. In this way, O lord of men, the complete calculation of all the planets in order should be understood. For the moon-born one and the white one, what is corrected by the day-maker, the sun, truly becomes the moon-born one and the white one.

The whole count of days, multiplied by one hundred, is diminished by seven thousand one hundred twenty-three And divided by eight thousand seven hundred ninety-seven; the remainder is again multiplied by the divisions of lunar mansions and so on. By the parts as stated, the lunar mansions and again the nāḍīs become the traversed portion of the moon-born one. Setting the moon-born one aside, from the sun's mean position, O excellent lord of men, sixteen and a half lunar mansions are removed.

The rest of the procedure, positive and negative, the slow and the fast equations, is just as for Mars. The steps of the moon-born one for the slow equation are ten, seven and three; for the fast equation, sixteen and so on. The count of days, diminished by two thousand six hundred, is divided by four thousand three hundred thirty-two. The remainder multiplied by lunar mansions gives lunar mansions, and again nāḍīs, of the guru, also for the slow equation.

Subtract twelve lunar mansions; eleven, nine and three are the steps for the slow equation. Again, for the fast equations, the steps in the fourteen places are clearly ten, four and so on. The count of days, multiplied by ten and diminished by eighty-four, is divided by two thousand two hundred forty-seven. From what is multiplied by lunar mansions comes the traversed portion in lunar mansions, and again in nāḍīs, of Venus, for the fast equation.

Six lunar mansions are subtracted from the sun; five, three and one are the steps for the slow equation. For the fast equation, by divisions beginning with twenty-five, from the fourteen places, the additions and subtractions are as for Mars. The clear count of days, diminished by four thousand eight hundred twenty, is divided by ten thousand seven hundred sixty-six, And multiplied by lunar mansions it becomes lunar mansions; and for Saturn what is to be subtracted is eighteen.

The steps are twenty-two, fifteen and six, clearly in six places; for the fast equation, six and so on.
The fast and slow motions also follow the steps traversed in order, while retrograde motion goes in reverse order.
In the first half and in the latter half, according to the sun's motion, the risings of the planets also occur.
By the months multiplied by two hundred thirty-seven, the long-tailed ones become fast and retrograde.

Whatever planets enter into the sun's range, those planets certainly set. Leaving the sun, they rise; their path too is distinguished by course and motion. Whichever stays on the left path goes on the left, according to the sun's motion; Those that stay on the right go on the right; and again, clearly, here they fight one another, because they are enemies.

Those abiding in their own field, if they become enemies there, certainly do battle. This happens in a lunar mansion; it is certain that it does not occur elsewhere, and here they are joined as equals to each other. If the moon has entered to the left of the sun and rises on the left as well, Its northern horn becomes raised; in some cases, according to its course, the right one in the south is raised.

At the junctures as well, Rāhu moves level with the face and seizes only when directly facing. For those moving on the left, he seizes on the side that is not right; according to the sun's course, for those on the right, on the right side. Thus Rāhu seizes at the intermediate quarters, and the hare-bearer, the moon, at its rising, from behind as well. From Aśvinī up to half of Citrā, in the middle where day and night are equal, lies the line of the equator.

The lunar mansions, on the right and on the left, are set firmly in the abode of existence by the division into houses.
The houses of time, shaped like a bow, from the middle of the equator, rest to the south on the fire maṇḍala,
And to the north on the cool mountains, like a fine lotus petal, and to the east at Aries, Libra and so on.
From the fire to the border of the cool mountains is seventy-five thousand yojanas.

In the middle is the line of the half circle, and the sun's course, where night and day are equal.
Thereafter it moves to the right and to the left, day and night in sequence, by the division of lunar mansions.
It passes through one hundred eighty paths, and at the end of the courses, day and night as well,
The day and night of the sun and the moon, out of sixty nāḍīs, decrease and increase by a sixth part.

Because for the people in the seventh, day and night become equal with the sun in Aries and Libra,
Therefore in the second and eighth sections it is likewise, certainly, with the sun in Taurus and in Scorpio.
In those called the ninth and the third, with the sun in Gemini and Sagittarius; in the tenth and fourth,
With the sun in Cancer and in Capricorn; and in the fifth and eleventh,

With the sun entering Leo and Aquarius; and in the twelfth and sixth, with it in Pisces and Virgo.
Here the left, northern course begins with the sun in Capricorn; with the sun in Aquarius, in the second;
With it in Pisces, in the third section; and the course of people, with the sun in Aries, in the fourth.

in the fifth, called "element," with the sun in Taurus; with the sun in Gemini, in the sixth of the ground; and in the seventh

it comes about with the sun in Cancer; with it in Leo, in the eighth segment of the ground.
With Virgo in the ninth; with the sun in Libra it is in the tenth; and with Scorpio in the eleventh segment.
With it in Sagittarius, it is in the twelfth; and the courses of humans too are on the twelve-spoked ground.
Likewise, by the force of the sun's course, it becomes the southward course in the houses beginning with Cancer.

Above, numbering eighty-six, are the sun's heat-makers at the end of the northward courses;
then on the right-hand side as well they come to an end, O lord of men, at eleven thousand.
Eighty-one thousand less five hundred, measured from the middle, is the line of the globe;
what increases and decreases from that is five thousand five hundred, to the north and to the south.

Having crossed the salt ocean, the day-makers go south as far as the fire;
they go north of Kailāśa and dwell to the north, north even of the Snowy Mountains.
In their courses through the directions they travel thousands of yojanas, five multiplied by fifteen;
what is left behind day by day is obtained by one hundred eighty-two and a half from seventy-five thousand.

With the sun dwelling in a single house, it makes all the groups of time, the months and the fortnights.
Segment by segment it becomes a month, in order upon the twelve spokes of the ground.
Whatever measure holds in a given segment by virtue of day, that same holds for night in the seventh from it.
On half the path, the three hundred thousand, in front of the sun is day and behind it is night.

The kings of mountains dwell to the north of all people, O lord of men.
The sun's rising is in the east, its courses run level with the mountain, and its setting is in the west.
The times, the four junctures, the watches, day and night, all that moves truly at all times,
divided by the divisions of the ascendants, revolve clearly on the sun's ground in the directions of Meru.

In the time of one ghaṭikā the day-maker travels six thousand yojanas and a quarter;
in sixty it circles through day and night, three hundred and seventy-five thousand.
In a day and a night it traverses thirty ghaṭikās on each half of the globe; on the globes, to the right
and to the left, the decrease and increase is five, and for each day it is three palas and a pair of breaths.

For the ascendant of Aries it is two hundred ninety palas; for Taurus, two hundred sixty;
for Gemini, two hundred; and five and six ghaṭikās for Cancer and for Leo;
for Virgo, six and a half; and so in reverse order all those beginning with Libra are likewise.
Since it sets by six ascendants, its risings too are likewise, according to the divisions of the sun's lunar mansions.

From twenty-one thousand six hundred, what is drawn out by the thirty-second part
are the surplus breaths in the body that occur each day, the number of the mountains and the seasons.
These, multiplied by thirty-six thousand, are renowned as the measure of a hundred years;
divided by three hundred sixty they become ghaṭikās, and by the sixtieth part, days as well:

the moon's fortnight, the sun's course, and the nine months of the earth-born one, together with the nights. The guru's becomes six years; Venus also has a course, and Mercury too has a course. The years called "lunar days," fifteen, belong to Saturn; likewise nine to Rāhu, and sixteen to the long-tailed slow one. All these gathered into one are forty-eight years of time and three and a half months.

From the number of days with a half, too, a hundred years result; here they fall short by the Kālacakra.
The life breath descends into sattva, in between into rajas, and into tamas, by the division of body, speech and mind.
For gods, humans and animals it then becomes the fifth itself, by months with a half and by nights as well.
These very definite numbers are renowned in the Kālacakra, O lord of men.

In the unbroken courses of the Kālacakra, all the nine debts are mixed together. For the five planets beginning with the auspicious one, in their fast motion, there are ghaṭikās of a quarter. For the earth-born one it becomes three hundred sixty-four, by the division of motion in the stations of existence. Equally in the first and latter halves, in the courses of debt and wealth, apply them definitely by the division of stations.

First come ghaṭikās numbering twenty-four, and twenty-three, arising in three ways.
In the two stations: twenty-one, eighteen, fifteen, eleven, three; and at the root, eleven,
thirty-eight and eighty; and here, in the fixed stations of existence, fifty-three.
By that the sun is purified, and debts and wealth come in reverse order and in order.

The numbers four, nine and one become the ghaṭikās for Mercury's fast motion.
In the two stations, sixteen; in the others, fifteen, fourteen, thirteen, eleven, seven, five and zero.
From the root: four, eleven, and clearly, definitely, in the twelfth, twenty;
twenty-eight and thirty-four as well stand rightly by the division of motion in the stations of Manu.

The numbers four, zero and one are the sum of the guru's ghaṭikās.
In the two stations: ten, nine, eight, six, two and one; and at the root
three, six, nine, eleven, sixteen and seven: all the motions in order.
In fast, slow and crooked motion, too, the host of planets moves definitely by the divisions of the sun.

The ghaṭikās numbering six, one and four become Venus's fast motion.
In the three stations: twenty-five, two, the number of the Victors, and likewise twenty-two in two ways,
three, fifteen and eight; at the root, six, zero and three, and a hundred less one;
at the end, the numbers three and seven: in the fixed stations of existence, the motion of Venus in order.

For the son of the sun, these sums of nāḍīs come to six and the number of the elements, O lord of men.
At the root are six and elements, elements, Vedas, ocean, eyes, pair and void;
directions, Vedas, elements, tastes, Vasus and the crested one are to be set down in order.
Thus the entire motion of the planets becomes fully certain through Kālacakra.

Thirty-six and a half months, multiplied by the Vasus, are for the Auspicious one and the others. For the slow motion of the planets they become the quarters: nāḍīs numbering eye, hole and eye. That same is eighteen, mountains and the steadfast for the later and earlier portions of the son of the earth; directions, mountain and fire are Mercury's; lords, nine and fire for the earlier portions of the guru; and for the others too:

for the white one, arrows, ocean and moon; for Saturn too, eye with eye; and these become days.
In a month the motion of the earth-born one is ninety nāḍīs, and five, and the number of worlds and moons.
That of Venus, and likewise of Mercury, combined into one, becomes sky, tastes and fire-eater.
These, by three months, are the days of the year; the debt, the nāḍīs and this motion belong to the three.

In a month there is the motion of the guru: clearly, nāḍīs numbering the Rudras, with a quarter,
and thirty palas; and clearly, nāḍīs numbering the seasons are the motion of the son of the sun.
Combined into one, what is called days, multiplied by three, that again with half a month, with the nights.
For the motion of the chief, day by day of the days, the debt is also the motion of the six planets.

Whatever single month in excess, in the slow motion following the all-pervading lord, the planets have not traversed,
from that, clearly, seven and a half nāḍīs are traversed in this very month.
Rāhu's monthly motion, together with the sun, is reduced by a quarter of the moon and half a nāḍī.
In the sun's first course, three; in the intoxicating month, for the days, five lunar nāḍīs.

Whatever half nāḍī is untraversed is the day of time, at the time a month reduced in portion;
that becomes the sun's excess, and, multiplied by the seasons, it is that day in the solar month.
Rāhu's monthly motion is multiplied by eyes and divided by the motions of the directions;
what is obtained are the long-tailed one's nāḍīs at the time of each day, from Rāhu's monthly motion

seven and a half palas being taken away, and, multiplied by the qualities, they are to be corrected into the sun's motion. The long-tailed one moves together with the sun, ahead and behind in its fast and crooked motions. In its course it travels two and a half palas, day by day, in order, from the sun's motion. By its own thirty-six months, the nāḍīs of its motion too are multiplied by the worlds into the count of lunar days.

In a month and a half, two nāḍīs; it rises on each day, ahead and behind in fast and crooked motion.
In the intervals of a month and a half its rising becomes visible in the human world.
Again it enters glorious Kālacakra, and at that time it is not seen in the yearly motions.
If the portions of its rising are known, the endless time of its rising will also be known.

The days of the year, multiplied by five and again by arrows, are much reduced by the days of the course;
combined with holes, by the power of the qualities of their own course, they become the maṇḍalas of the planets.
For the earth-born one the sum of days becomes mountain, nāga, six; mountain, hole, mountain, nāga for the moon-born.
For the guru of the gods, teeth, fire and Veda; for Venus, mountain, Victor and eye.

For the son of the sun, six, six, mountain, sky and one: this sum of days becomes the maṇḍala. For the earth-born one, mountain, seasons and one become definitely Vasus; for the son of the moon, multiplied by a hundred, reduced by fire, sun and mountain; and for Venus, multiplied by ten days and reduced by eight oceans. Thousand, sky, seasons and eyes, and sky, hand, serpent and seasons become the debt for the minister and the sun-born.

For the earth-born one, nine and a half; for Mercury, a half in excess, O lord of men; for the asterisms, the sun and the guru; for Venus, six; for the sun-born, fault becomes the debt; all are to be corrected from the mean motion. Afterwards these become corrected, O lord of men, from the motion of the asterism of birth; the low is corrected from its own limbs, and by the power of the course the high abides in the middle of the sun.

From the monthly motion multiplied by six months, and by the months of the Kālacakra year,
the portions obtained also become the debt; the half nāḍī left untraversed following the sun and the hare-marked one.
Again, whatever excess of the long-tailed one, at the time of each day, is multiplied by the lunar day;
by a sixtieth part, nāḍīs, at the time of each day, and also for the fast and crooked risings:

the moon-born and the minister become strong in fast motion; the earth-born and the long-tailed one in crooked motion; the slow one and Venus in slow motion; by the power of the qualities of their intrinsic nature, in the remaining motions they lack strength. In fast motion they come to face east; again, entering into crooked motion, they turn toward another direction; in slow motion they come to face south, and by the power of the quality of their own course, in rising too they face north.

The thirty days of the month, multiplied by the ocean, sky, eye and moon, are the moon's quarter;
the twelve houses, multiplied by the seasons, eight and Vedas too, are the sun's quarter.
Within that, decrease and increase, by the power of the course's going, are out of order and in order.
In the halves of the wheel, all the numbers of Vasus and seasons are to be known, O lord of men.

At the junctures Rāhu enters the maṇḍalas of the hare-marked one, and when the maṇḍala
is wholly pure he seizes it all; by the power of the hare-marked one, the maṇḍala is sixty nāḍīs.

If half of it is pure, Rāhu seizes half, and by the hare-marked one (the moon) the nāḍīs are as many as there are.
At the junctions of the dark and bright fortnights, by day and by night, he seizes the sun and the moon.

The hundred years of human beings are also to be converted by the years of the planets' motion. The quotient obtained in the two places by the portion of the directions becomes a debt; subtract it from the upper sums. The years that number twenty-one hundred thousand and sixty thousand belong to half of the period of time. Then, by the two sums, they surely become the number of years of the four ages.

Empty, empty, sky, nāga, hand, sage and the hare-marked one: this is the measure of the Kṛta age.
For the Tretā, sky, space, empty, taste, nine and the day-maker are renowned as the number of its years.
Empty, empty, space, veda, taste and the arm-goer: this is correct for the years of the Dvāpara.
Empty, sky, space, eye, quality and ocean become the number of years for the age of strife.

Through these years, at the end of the age, the host of moving planets dwells in the empty house.
Then Aśvinī and the rest, the year called Prabhava, and likewise the month of Caitra and the rest,
the weekday, the yoga, the lunar day and the karaṇa likewise are all at their first; and at that time
a fierce war of the gods and the asuras will break out in the places of the earth.

The six classes together with the vowels of the sun, multiplied by the qualities, are three years, for the suns. The short ones beginning with a are called days; tripled, with the hare-marked one and a half part, they are months, month by month. The nāḍīs, ha and the rest together with the vowels, which are tripled, these too are Rāhu's three nights. The glorious empty one, the unstruck and the rest, which are tripled, these too are Rāhu's three ascendants.

The number of elements, suns and moon, O lord of men, is months together with a half, and three years.
Having set aside the months with their half, what is divided by the supreme sages becomes the wheel of time.
To the months add one weekday, and, O lord of men, doubled, two to the sum of nāḍīs, and five pādas.
From the wheels, what is divided by the sixty goddesses surely becomes the weekday.

Also by thirty-two, from the goddesses multiplied by nāḍīs, sages and vasus, two to the sum;
add, subtract and add, and the first five pādas of the hare-marked one; here it is by six.
The three fortnights of the moon and the days, tripled, are renowned as the motion of the sun.
Two minutes divided by sixty, and on the first solar day the nāḍīs are serpent and lord.

This glorious Kālacakra, together with the host of planets, is undivided in the outer world and in the body.
For the sake of withdrawal and emanation, in the abode of the three existences, the sun is the form of time;
the divisions of wisdom and method, the classes of even and uneven, the swift and crooked courses and the rest:
these are not known by the dispassionate, by the lineage of supreme sages, nor by Brahmā, Rudra and the other gods.

The vowel that is first among the five beginning with a, by the count of the lunar day, is joined with the five beginning with Nandā, the Joyful. These are the risings here, O lord of men, provided that the planets, the one born of it and the rest, are not there. If the inauspicious earth-born one has risen, and if moreover it is joined with Kṛttikā, then if war and disease arise, people will die: this is for the first vowel.

If, on this weekday of Bhadrā, the Excellent, and the moon-born one, it is joined with Maghā, the second will die.
If Jayā, the Victorious, with the guru falls in Jyeṣṭhā, the third will die.
If the lunar day of Riktā, the Empty, with Venus falls in Uttaraphalgunī, the fourth will die.
If Pūrṇā, the Full, together with Saturn falls in Bharaṇī, the fifth will surely die.

On Sunday, through the risings of Nandā there will be wounds, and those tormented will be afflicted.
On Monday, through the risings of Bhadrā there will surely likewise be war and disease.
The results of the five true risings, through the conjunction of many deaths, fall on the slow one.
In disease, in war, in journeys to other lands and in brides, the results, good and bad, should be known.

Thus the lunar mansions, the weekdays, the groups of vowels and the lunar days, through the divisions of the five principles, rise here in the fortnights, the months, the half-years, the years, and the cycles of sixty years. In Caitra and the rest they rise here by twelve limbs: that is the division of the fortnight. The vowels of the days rise here by seventy-two: that is the division of the month.

The limbs of the half-year are by six months; the year is by the twelve years beginning with Prabhava. Thus the five vowels rise here, and after that they set. On the first, the vowels are children; by the power of their own lunar day and quality, on the second they are glorious youths; on the days of fire and ocean they are in their prime and old in turn, and on the fifth they set.

The five vowels beginning with a, when multiplied by the qualities, are thirty in two ways.
To the letters of the classes beginning with ka, all are to be joined according to their own nature and quality.
The short ones rise here in the bright fortnight, through the division of the five principles.
The long ones are wholly certain in the dark fortnight, through the application of emanation and withdrawal.

In the twenty-seven lunar mansions there are, clearly, the arrow nāḍīs, the solar ascendants, and the ninth parts.
The ascendant of Aries and the rest are here of three natures: fixed, moving, and wholly even.
Aries, Cancer, Libra and Capricorn are here the moving ones; the second from each of them is fixed.
The remaining others are of twofold nature; they rise constantly, at the times of day and night.

There, there are thirty muhūrtas, and in the period of a day the nāḍīs are exactly sixty.
First, the thirty vowels rise here, muhūrta by muhūrta.
The thirty letters of the classes occur in the nāḍīs, through the division into short and long.
In the middle of the month they are said to rise in the portion of the lunar day, and their setting is also in that.

Space, wind, fire, water, and earth are the classes of the five vowels beginning with a. Rāhu, Kālāgni, the moon, the sun, Mercury, Mars, Venus, the guru, the long-tailed one, and Saturn are here the presiding deities. For each ascendant (lagna) described before, one should calculate the nāḍīs according to day and night. Whatever vowel has arisen in its middle, its planet is called the powerful one, and by it the maṇḍala is to be determined.

From the left the short vowels arise here, and from the right the others. On the left are space and the other elements, and the moving host of planets, Rāhu and the rest, all of them. On the right are those same elements beginning with earth, and the host of planets is surely the Sun's son, Saturn, and the rest. Externally too, one should calculate the nāḍīs of time, matching the positions of the planets, together with their vowels.

If one asks about battle, one will die when Kālāgni has arisen in space.
When the sun is in wind, there is defeat; when the earth-born one arises in fire, there are wounds.
With the guru in water there is reconciliation; when Saturn arises in earth, there will be no fighting.
Death, affliction, wounds, ease, and an unchanged state are the answers for the sick who ask.

In battle the enemy will surely be overthrown when Rāhu arises in space. With the moon in wind there is defeat, and wounds in battle when Mercury arises in fire. With Venus in water one gains one's aim; when the long-tailed one arises in earth, even the enemy comes under one's power. Likewise for journeys and for brides, on the left path the planets bring wholesome results.

Whatever vowels the messenger speaks are surely to be multiplied by ten and mixed with the wheel of houses. Again multiply them by the elements, add the people nearby, and divide by seven; the remainder, if it falls in an odd place, moon, fire, arrow, or mountain, that is one, three, five, or seven, brings no gain to people. If it falls in an even place, eyes, Vedas, or tastes, that is two, four, or six, tasks too will be accomplished.

The times of the year, the month, the fortnight, the day, and the night are all contained within the ascendant.
One ascendant, divided by five, has distinct elements, each with its host of planets and its vowel.
One element lasts three hundred breaths and sixty more, in the nāḍīs of time.
The parts of a nāḍī are sixty palas; know that each of its parts is six breaths, O lord of men.

Within the movement of the breath, out and in, one endowed with yoga knows the results of nonvirtue and of virtue.
Astrologers calculate the sun and moon by the days that have elapsed, and so the nāḍīs of time.
Whatever results of virtue and nonvirtue they proclaim here in the world without knowing what is arising now,
that is a blind man led by a blind man, entering a thicket filled with elephants and lions.

For one who does not know subtle yoga, giving an ascendant together with the strength of the planets: in the house of birth and in the seventh house, Kālāgni, the sun, the earth-born one, and the son of the day-maker, Saturn, cause death for him, and illness, and wounds, and surely the ruin of all his wealth. Therefore they are to be avoided; by my word one should surely give a different ascendant.

In the house of birth and in the seventh house, Rāhu, the moon, Mercury, the guru, and Venus
make the enemy decline and oneself prosper; one is victorious in battle and gains wealth together with a beloved woman.
Through all the true ascendants, and further, if it arises from the left channel of one's own body,
surely all aims are accomplished, everywhere upon the earth, under pure planets and lunar days.

The lunar mansions are divided into three parts of nine, nine, and nine, counted from the first lunar mansion of one's birth. When the malefic planets stand together with the moon in odd places, the sick will surely die. By the mansions beginning with Aśvinī, the three channels are laid out as a serpent, up to Revatī itself. If the lunar mansions of a malefic, the moon, and one's birth fall on a single channel, people perish.

The good years numbering six, fifteen, eight, seventeen, ten, nineteen, twelve, and twenty-one are enjoyed in order by the sun, the moon, Mars, Mercury, Saturn the sun-born, the guru, Rāhu, and the white one, Venus. In these and in their inner sub-periods as well, then, through the eight classes of the letters, the fierce planets bring what is not peaceful, while the planets of peaceful nature bring supreme happiness.

In the month of Pauṣa, with the sun entered into Capricorn, there is ignorance; with the sun in Aquarius, in Māgha, there are formations; O lord of men, with the sun in Pisces, in Phālguna, there is consciousness; with the sun in Aries, in Caitra, name and form; entered into Taurus and Gemini, in Vaiśākha and Jyeṣṭha, the eye and the other sense bases, and contact; with the sun standing in Cancer, in the month of Āṣāḍha, there is feeling.

With the sun in Leo, in Śrāvaṇa, there is craving; entered into Virgo and Libra, in Bhādrapada
and in the month of Āśvina, there are grasping and becoming; with the sun in Scorpio, in Kārttika,
there is birth; and with the sun standing in Sagittarius, in Mārgaśīrṣa, likewise aging and death, in order.
Thus, from the divisions of sun and moon, by way of both courses, O king, are the twelve links.

At the junction of Pauṣa and Māgha, on the day when the sun stands in Capricorn, there is ignorance.
Then on the second there is death, O lord of men, and likewise birth on the third.
Likewise becoming and all the rest, up to the twelve links, turn over in order.
Then, by fire, ocean, and arrow, through the course of Capricorn, there are sometimes six links.

With the planet called the lunar day, that is, by the fortnight, through the movement of the sun, there are sometimes sixteen links. Formations are at the junction of Māgha; on the day the sun enters Aquarius there are formations alone. Then on the second there is consciousness, and on the third day name and form. Thus in the two months the links occur, through the method of expansion and contraction governed by the sun.

There, the odd limbs, ignorance and the rest, are auspicious for every undertaking, while the even ones, formations and the rest, are never auspicious for royal campaigns and for brides. Likewise, in the divisions of the directions, the twelve limbs proceed by the movement of the hare-marked one (the moon), and in the white and dark fortnights, by the number of the first lunar day, through the procedures of expansion and contraction.

When the sun, Mars, the long-tailed one, and Saturn stand to the right or behind, they bring death to the enemy. To the left and in front, all the planets of peaceful nature are just the same on the field of battle. The yoginīs and the fierce viṣṭi, together with the wind, grant well-being when on the right and behind; to the left and in front they are of the nature of death, and when they are even and odd, they crush the armies of both sides.

Brahmāṇī, Raudrī, Kaumārī, and Vaiṣṇavī, who rides the lord of sky-goers, Vārāhī, Vajrahastā, Cāmuṇḍā herself, and Lakṣmī: these fall on the eighth lunar day, and on the first and the ninth, in Śakra's quarter (Indra's), that is, then the yakṣa's, the fire corner, the asura's, Yama's, Varuṇa's, the wind's, Īśāna's, and the earth. This is done with the risings; and again, for the lunar days it varies through a division of twice eight.

The viṣṭis, in the white fortnight, rise here in Śakra's, Yama's, the water's, and the yakṣa's quarters, and again in the dark fortnight, in turn in the corners: the Crested One's (Agni's), the asura's, the wind's, and Īśāna's. The fierce one goes from the east to the other half, by the force of day and night, and also to the north, on the days of the sun's northern course; from the water it goes without fail to the fire god's quarter, and to the south, on the days of the sun's southern course.

From the east, in half-watches, it enters the wind's quarter, and likewise goes to the south, then to Īśāna's, the water's, the fire's, the yakṣa's, to the city of the asuras, and in the wind's quarter it sets like the sun. Setting aside the glorious root Rāhu, victory comes from the winds as well, in contests and rivalries; but in times of turmoil it is the root Rāhu that crushes the enemy's strength; there is no other equal to it.

A fighter whose vowel is weak is crushed in battle, even or odd, by those whose vowels are stronger.
If, upon the breath, either of the two names enters the heart, that one is victorious in conflicts.
The first name spoken by a messenger will be victorious; the name spoken later will be defeated.
Sums, colors, vowels and the like, whether even or odd, are shown to be fruitless.

Should Kāla stand on the right of kings on the field of battle, or the Dark One on the left,
in battle even the lord of the gods, together with Hari and Rudra, would be crushed by men.
Therefore the glorious root Rāhu, crooked and fierce, victor over the three worlds among the asuras,
should be known to abide in the earth maṇḍala at the time of battle; otherwise the enemy does not decline.

Having laid out the lunar mansions on the ground, the sages multiplied by the times, in Indra's, the yakṣa's, the water's and Yama's quarters, one sets its wheel turning, and all of them are to be understood by the course of the day. On whichever lunar mansion Kāla and the Dark One have come, if the gaze falls there, then for both sides, in battle the hosts of the sounds fall at once without fail; in the middle of the belly there is victory.

Those who stand at Rāhu's Kāla meet with death, and their armies fall together; those who have gone to the portion of the gaze, there by the movement of the one who goes on his chest, the troops are destroyed. Those who have avoided the portion of the gaze will be victorious, and both sides too, having entered the belly. Thus the glorious root Rāhu, abiding in the word of the guru, is to be known in Kālacakra.

The yoginīs, the fierce viṣṭi, the hosts of planets, and the vowels together with their risings:
having left all these aside, kṣatriyas should hold to the Victor over the Three Worlds alone,
who devours the moon and the sun, terrifying to the Thirty gods, of fierce form, the color of collyrium,
who circles the earth by day and by night, to the east, the west, the south and the north.

If enemies defeated in battle at once take refuge inside a fortress,
one should build machines outside, raining down stones and fire arrows without pause,
and cutting machines such as swords, and at ground level breach it with screens and frames;
reducing their fortress to powder, one should overcome that wicked one in a few days.

Four posts, each sixteen cubits long, set even and uneven, are pierced at eight places;
the separate ones are joined by struts numbering the oceans, the times and the times, and at the back by one.
At the base the machine measures ten hands, and on the upper parts half of that.
The throwing arm is two cubits shorter, and its upper part extends beyond the ten cross-pieces.

It is round, two and a half cubits, and in a single hole of seven fingers, set firm so it does not slip,
leaving one cubit, the throwing arm is inserted, and at the rear of the ten cross-pieces it is fixed with a nail.
At its head fifty ropes are to be tied to rings, and the measure of the top is half round.
At the end of the throwing arm the prong is inserted, and the round yokes are a span each.

On the prong of the throwing arm, from the sling pouch with its rope, the length is exactly three cubits.
A stone is placed in it, and when it is drawn by strong pullers
and released, it swiftly flies into the sky and at once falls without fail on upper rooms and the like and on streets,
reducing everything to powder and going beneath the ground, just as a vajra falls.

Its prong is a half moon; on the ground, on both sides of the Brahmā line,
are sixteen release positions, and the release should be made from the clenched fists of both hands.
The release is made touching the ground, the feet set even and uneven within the machine;
the stone falls where one wishes, by its threefold course, in order to destroy the fortress.

If an elephant struck by it falls at once without fail, what then of tiny creatures?
It hurls outside all the enemy troops who stay in the upper rooms of the fortress.
In fighting for a fortress on the ground, who among archers could rival it?
Wherever the machine strikes, whatever enemies have entered that fortress are in the jaws of Yama.

132.
With columns and arms, six and six, equal inside on both sides, with spaces of two cubits between them,
each core is twelve cubits, equal inside on both sides, neither more nor less.
Every one of the separate squares is covered with planks, with hides, and with tallow-soaked cloths.
With cores of six, eight, three, and three times twelve, one constructs a single machine.

133.
Between every two cores the moving parts are lashed crosswise by the arms, or all around.
At the back are six who hold the oars, and from the front the two equal arms draw the machine along;
or else it is driven by the wind, through wind-banners that flutter, fixed firmly in sockets in the columns.
Mounted on it, one's own troops go out upon the ocean to bring down fortresses on the water.

134.
On a four-cornered platform, fasten to a column a fluttering wind-banner together with a standard.
Drawn by men from behind and rising from the ground on ropes,
driven by the wind, it surely travels through the sky above a fortress on the rocks.
Then, by hurling burning oil, it sets the enemy army and the fortress entirely ablaze.

135.
At the base of the wheel insert a single-pointed canopy, and above it, on the wheels,
there are spokes at intervals of two fingers each, set all the way around the circular rim.
The wheel measures two and a half cubits, and above the canopy there is also an axle.
Above the axle a sharp sword whirls swiftly, because the base of the machine is turning.

136.
By means of a rope the wind-machine rises into the sky, in stages, from one's own side.
Having drawn the rope so that it goes without fail above the fortress and to each of the enemy's houses,
it also indicates the measure on the ground; and opening the summit of the enemies' fortress,
placed at the top, one should set fire to the foundations of the enemy's houses and to their meeting places.

137.
On the upper part of the wheels a great house is built of iron and of wood,
covered all over with planks and with buffalo hides, and round in form.
Pushed by men over level ground, it travels all the way to the moat;
having filled the moat, it at once breaks in upon the walls of the citadel and its streets.

138.
On a platform, having bound a bow with its string firmly and immovably to two stakes,
and behind it, well set upon two iron stakes, half-curved pieces like fingers on the machine beam,
at the tips of these fingers, many equal arrows or sharp iron darts, propelled by the bowstring
and shot at the time of battle, pierce right through the bodies of elephants, armor and all.

139.
Two posts are planted firmly in the ground and wound about with ropes.
In the middle of the rope a sword hilt is inserted, together with a round piece, and wound with many windings.
In its middle the sword hilt is fixed immovably with a nail.
The nail and the ropes should be covered, and the point of the sword's edge pressed down.

140.
Above it, released from a plank with a pierced line, the triggered blade springs out.
On the sword, at the middle part of a moon-shaped piece, a small one made of iron should also be placed.
Whoever sets a foot upon the moon is instantly cut down by the sword.
At the edges of the city gates and in the palace of the lord of men, this machine alone is the weapon.

141.
On the axle, by sun-hands, is the place of the base; for the measure of the wheel there are nine parts.
At the corners are posts and crossbeams, equal and unequal, measured at two and a half from the chariot's place.
On the halves is the ground of the warriors; the draft horses or elephants are equal and unequal.
Then, on the ground of half the wheel, mirrors, necklaces, banners and the like are arranged above.

142.
One, a half, and two parts: moons and hands with tips, for the place of the base and for the seats;
round, with a half, one part, the ground of the lotus chariot is equal for the divisions of the Joy type.
The measure of the wheel with its support, according to horses or elephants, is two, with halves as well.
The lotus type is for the gods, arranged by the son of the gods; the Joy type is for humans.

143.
With eight, four, axle and wheels, these three kinds here equal half of a great chariot.
The supreme chariot of the Sun has a single wheel and unequal horses, and it destroys the asuras.
The great chariots, unequal and equal, which never turn back in battle, are those of the kṣatriyas.
They destroy what is above and what faces them, equal and unequal; in battle, destroying from behind is equal.

144.
By parts of six, five, four and three, the holes at the tops of the posts are made equal.
From the bases upward the half part is according to the tiers: these are the celestial palaces of the gods.
Likewise on earth, for lords of men, with three tiers, for the spectacles and festivals of the land,
at intervals of cubit upon cubit, they become great, endowed with revolving in all directions.

145.
Above the wheel, at the tops of the posts, with three tiers, are litters on every side:
eight, two, eight, and then half; directions, seasons, seasons, hands, support, and the three worlds.
Revolving up and down, the machine beam is held by hand, and girls ride upon it,
staying inside and outside, reaching below and above, curving toward the eight directions.

146.
Above and below the tubes are curved, and with tube shapes all around, it is a machine.

at the tip of the tube is the shape of a vase, and its opening is the size of the tube's openings.
It draws up water, and the water so drawn, once released from the vase's opening,
flows to wherever it is led on the level ground of pleasure groves and gardens.

To subdue the wicked on the most excellent lands, and for a victory in keeping with the Dharma,
of all that the guru of the three worlds formerly taught Sucandra in the Ādibuddha,
I have clearly shown you here today, in this land, only a small part.
To guard your own realm, build all of these, not out of aversion or attachment, O Sūrya!

In the middle, between the oceans of liquor and of salt water, on the seventh disc of earth, lies the land of karma.
It spans three hundred thousand yojanas, and over its twelve sections the lords of men hold sway in turn.
Each section is twenty-five thousand yojanas, together with its eight petals.
At its center is Kailāsa, and on every side the directions are encircled by supreme snow mountains.

On earth, the Kailāsa section together with its snow mountains makes up a third of the whole. Outside it, each of the petals is adorned with twelve lands and with island lands. In the right half, renowned as Shambhala, is the abode of the supreme sage, where more than ten million towns are found. A country is defined as bounded by ten million villages, and a kingdom by hundreds of thousands of villages.

Divided into one, two and three, the oceans, the arrows, the tastes, the sages, five, the worlds and so on, the lands are ruled in turn by the wheel-bearing lords of men. One with understanding should investigate this. There are wheel-holders, half-holders and section-holders, lords of countries, and, other than these, those whose hand bears the seal. Outside, the divisions of time are fixed, just as they are for the Lord of the World.

Here the seven rays, the mountains, the planets and the sages are likewise beings of goodness (sattva), the birthplace of the gods. The Fish, the Tortoise, the Boar, the Man-Lion, the Dwarf, Jāmadagnya, Rāma and Kṛṣṇa, these eight, who bring terror to the race of asuras, are the birthplace of beings of passion (rajas). At the end of thirty-two reigns, the enemy of the asuras, living a hundred years, will become the one with the wheel in his hand.

Ādra, Anogha and Varāhī, of the race of asuras and serpents, are of darkness, and there are five more:
Mūsā, Īsā, Śvetavastrī, Madhumati, and the eighth, Mathanī, who is of darkness.
The seventh is clearly born in the land of Makha, in cities such as Bāgada,
where the people, of asura nature, live strong of body and merciless, in the form of barbarians (mlecchas).

Having slaughtered camels, horses and cattle, they eat their flesh with the blood, only lightly cooked;
beef, quicksilver, water, butter and spices in equal measure, mixed with rice and greens,
cooked together over the fire along with fruits of the forest, is the food of the people there.
Their drink is made from the eggs of birds. O lord of men, that is the abode of the asuras.

The tilaka, the potent one, the eye-salves, the garuḍa, the river of the gods, the man-giver and the wish-granting cow, the fortress, the lightning, excellent knowledge, the supreme immutable portion, the speech of the gods, and the bodiless one: through these forms that rightly move about, the wisdom body of the Victor enters the earth. It proclaims in full all the treatises, the Vedas, logic and the rest, of past, present and future.

O glorious king, in a few days, Kalāpa, renowned as Shambhala,
ringed by mountains in the four directions, measuring a hundred yojanas multiplied by five:
having installed Devendra, the most excellent, as lord of men there, you shall go to your own abode.
In the glorious Śākya clan there will be seven illustrious lords of men, and the eighth, glorious Yaśas,

he who is glorious Mañjuvajra, honored by the foremost of the gods, will be Kalkī through the vajra family.
Having conferred the vajra empowerment, he will make all the clans of sages into a single clan.
Mounted on the true vehicle, bringing terror to the race of asuras, glorious Yaśas, spear in hand,
will make the Kālacakra shine forth on earth for the liberation of sentient beings.

In its center, at the close of the period of twenty-five reigns counted in succession, there will arise in the Kalkī line the Kalkī Raudra, honored by the lord of the gods and the foremost of the gods. To the noble ones he is a peaceful form, called the Bliss-Giver; likewise, to the barbarian race, he is Antaka, Death itself. Riding a stone horse, the wheel-bearer, spear in hand, strikes down every enemy, with the splendor of the sun.

In the midst of the Kalkī line, when sons and grandsons have passed away over a span of time multiplied by two, at that time the religion of the barbarians will certainly gain entry into the land of Makha. However many wicked barbarian lords there are, and however many fierce ones bearing mantras, honored by the foremost of the gods, at that time these two will bring fierce turmoil upon the face of the earth.

Striking down the barbarians in battle across the whole earth, with his own fourfold army,
at that time the wheel-turning emperor (cakravartin) will come forth from the city built by the gods on Mount Kailāsa.
Rudra, Skanda, the lord of the gaṇas and Hari will also give their aid to the Kalkī,
and so too will stone horses, lordly elephants, golden chariots, kings, and heroes with weapons in hand.

The stone horses, swift as the wind, will number three times three ten-millions, of many colors;
the elephants maddened by liquor will number four hundred thousand, and the chariots five hundred thousand;
there will be six akṣauhiṇīs, and kings of crowned lineage numbering ninety-six.
The Kalkī, together with Viṣṇu and Hari, will destroy the barbarians with these troops.

The supremely fierce heroes shall strike the barbarian hordes: lordly elephants against elephants,
stone horses against horses, and in battle, equal and unequal, rulers of the earth against rulers of the earth.
Mounted on horseback, Hanumān, the son of Mahāsoma, will strike with sharp weapons.

and the fierce Kalkī strikes the fierce lord and protector of the barbarians (mlecchas), all of them, whose minds the lord of the asuras has taken over.

Having destroyed the barbarians in battle with all his armies, the Kalkī, together with Viṣṇu and Rudra,
goes to the city built by the gods on Mount Kailāsa, where the cakravartins dwell.
At that time, upon the earth, all the races of people will be complete in Dharma, desire and wealth.
Grain will grow in the wilderness, and the trees will be bowed down with steady fruit; these things will come to pass.

Having utterly cut down the host of barbarians together with their retinue of people, after fifty human years
the Kalkī will attain accomplishment, behind Kailāsa, in the lofty palace built by the gods.
The sons of the guru of gods and men, the pair Brahmā and the lord of the gods, will arise in the Dharma.
Brahmā, behind, will become lord of men in one region, and over all the earth the lord of the gods will rule.

At the juncture of the age of perfection, the gurus of the three existences cut off all the barbarians' teachings,
and having cut them off, he establishes Brahmā for eight hundred years and goes to the abode of bliss.
Then again, in the midst of that, the lineages of the sons of the lords of men will divide.
In their midst there will arise lords of the asuras, lords of men, sages, and other ordinary people.

To vanquish the barbarians, the lord of the gods will go forth together with twelve lords.
Having destroyed the barbarians' teaching region by region, the cakravartin too goes to the abode of bliss.
The sons of the guru of the Thirty gods and of men are Brahmā and the lord of the gods, behind and in front;
behind, the lineages of Brahmā and the others, of many kinds, will become many divisions upon the earth.

In the time of Brahmā and the others, human beings have eighteen hundred years; this is the lifespan of men.
Half of that is in the time of Kāśyapa; on the supreme earth, half of that again in the time of the glorious Narasiṃha.
Less by sixty, likewise, in the time of each of the four ages, for Hari's forms beginning with Vāmana,
until at the end of the Kalkī the lifespan of people comes to a count of a hundred years.

Thus in all regions too, in the times of the four ages, the barbarians' teaching spreads;
it remains for exactly one hundred and eight years, and then the barbarians' teaching declines.
After that, for twenty thousand years less two hundred, the Buddha's Dharma will flourish.
The ages of perfection, of three, of two, and of strife move over the earth according to the measure of their power.

The generation of the world realms, the equal motion of the planets, the arising of the cakravartin,
the decline of the barbarians' teaching, and the Kalkī's granting of the path to the supreme abode of bliss:
all these I have explained to you, Sucandra, according to their true meaning, in terms of the three times.
Now again, for the benefit of all people and for the sake of liberation, what will you ask of me?

This was the first chapter, on the arrangement of the world realm, from the glorious Kālacakra, drawn forth from the glorious great Primordial Buddha.

Whatever the various measures of the unknown that you, begetter of the Victors, have taught, I too have received.
Now I wish to hear again: how are all the three existences present within the body?
Having heard the words of Sucandra, the Sugata said "Excellent!" and spoke:
For the liberation of sentient beings, out of supreme compassion, the various measures too are within the body.

Earth, water, fire, wind, the thunderbolt, the bow of the gods, and the winds, and the wheel of the houses; moon, sun, Rāhu, Meru, the hooded serpents, the abodes of humans and of gods, together with the crooked one and the stars; the transits, the months, the fortnights, days and nights, and all the lunar days, are within the body. They are to be known, each by its own letter, as the threefold, the course of the all-pervading lord, by the yogin through the divisions of emptiness.

In this body the whole collection of elements comes from the six tastes that are eaten and drunk; the six tastes too come from the elements, and the clearly arisen collection of elements comes from the element of space. Gnosis, mingled with emptiness, of one taste and unchanging, also becomes permanent. Thus the peaceful one, abiding in the elements and abiding in the threefold existence, is to be known in one's own body.

The seeds lying in the lotus are held by earth; then water gathers them; afterward
fire ripens them and makes them consume taste upon taste; their growth is through wind.
Space gives them room to grow. O lord of men, thus are they accomplished through time,
and at the time of birth, and in the periods of childhood and the rest, they take on the states of the gods.

For the seeds, earth clearly produces heaviness and solidity, and water likewise moistness;
fire ripens them, and the supreme ten kinds of winds are their growth.
For their growing, space in turn gives room; thus are they produced through time,
and through many various things they bring forth the embodied, with speech, mind and powers.

When the seed of awakening, together with the life force, falls into the blood within the secret lotus,
it becomes the fish, the tortoise, the boar, the man-lion, the dwarf, Rāma and Rāma,
Kṛṣṇa, the Buddha, and the lord of men: it becomes these ten, and these are also the ten states.
Three are in the womb, likewise one at the time of birth, and two in the periods of childhood.

Others arise in boyhood and youth: one in maturity, and likewise one in full development;
in old age there is one, the peaceful; and the last in the existence of womb-born beings is death itself.
In the fish there is the fish transformation for some days; in the tortoise, the tortoise state;
in the boar its twelve sense bases arise; in the man-lion comes birth.

In the dwarf there is the state of childhood; in Rāma, O lord of men, the teeth appear;
then in the other Rāma they fall out; in Kṛṣṇa clearly one practices deceits;
in the Buddha one practices peace and the discipline of vows; in the Kalkī comes death.
From semen arise marrow, bone and sinew; from the female substance, skin, blood and flesh.

In the womb, in one month, blood and seed turn into the taste of nectar and develop;
afterward the sprout of the seed, at its own heart, has ten kinds of channels, exceedingly subtle.

And at the navel too there are another eight times eight, which move upward toward the feet, hands and face.
When the two months are complete, there is here a slight indication of feet, hands and face.

When the third month is complete, the hands, feet, neck and head become evenly formed.
In the fourth month, the subtle channels form in the regions of the feet, hands, face and neck.
In the fifth, flesh, blood and bones form, and the three hundred and sixty joints as well.
In the sixth month flesh and blood increase, and there are feelings of pleasure and of pain.

In the seventh, the eyebrows, the hair of the head, the body hair and the orifices form, and the remaining channels;
at the month's end, day by day, they come into being two hundred at a time.
In the eighth, the vital points, bones and marrow form, along with the sense of taste, urine and excrement.
In the ninth, at the time of birth, there is fierce suffering from being squeezed by the birth passage.

In the womb there is the suffering of dwelling in the womb, and at the time of birth and in childhood there is suffering as well. In youth and in the prime of life, there is the great suffering of affliction over women and wealth, and over the loss of fortune. In old age there is the suffering of death, and then again, in the six destinies, the terrifying realms of wailing and the rest. This whole world, deluded by illusion, clings to suffering.

In saṃsāra one becomes human only now and then, and only sometimes does a mind turn toward the Dharma.
After that, by the power of virtue, one comes to devotion to the Buddha and enters the first vehicle.
After that, of all minds, only rarely does one enter meditation on the glorious Vajra Vehicle.
And beyond that, to enter buddhahood, the state of the supreme bliss of desire, ah, how very hard that is!

In the womb it is the perfectly pure body; from the time of birth, when the teeth have grown, it is the Dharma body.
When the teeth fall out, O lord of men, it is the Victor's enjoyment body.
Then the emanation body becomes fully manifest, up to the moment of death, for human beings.
So again, in the womb and outside it, by the division of gnosis and consciousness, this becomes fourfold.

Consciousness, moon and sun abiding in the lotus: that is the pure body in the womb.
The tenfold arising of the elements and aggregates is the Victor's Dharma body.
Then, by the power of the qualities, the ear, sound and the rest that arise become the enjoyment body.
Then the hair and so forth grow, and the time of birth too is here the emanation body.

When one is born, the breath that surely comes forth is itself the pure body.
Then the teeth that come forth, and the speech that is not yet clear, are likewise the Dharma body.
Then, when the twice-born teeth fall out, O lord of men, it is the Victor's enjoyment body.
From the teeth onward, up to the moment of death, outwardly it becomes the emanation body of the lords of the Victors.

Here in the womb, first, it becomes the glorious body vajra; when the nature of speech arises, the speech vajra. When the teeth have arisen, it is the mind vajra; and after they fall again, it becomes the gnosis vajra. Gnosis and consciousness mingled, together with the sun and the hare-marked moon, are all of these, the gnosis vajra and the rest. Those dwelling in the womb come to be endowed with strength through the body vajra and the rest, in sequence.

The core of earth becomes fire, and earth and wind are the core of space.
Wind and the core of earth become water; these are the natureless and the nature.
The support of earth is water, the blazing fire is the support of water, and wind is the support of the purifying fire.
Space becomes the support of wind, O lord of men, just as the elements are support and supported.

The elements become the support of the aggregates, and in the supreme body the channels support the mass of elements.
The tenfold vital wind supports the channels, and mind supports the vital winds.
That mind takes on a twofold nature, by the power of the qualities: having a nature and being natureless.
And their support is the lord, bowed to by the three worlds, indestructible and all-pervading.

Smell arises from earth, taste from fire, and the element of form from water.
From wind comes touch, from the peaceful one sound, and the dharma element from space.
Space apprehends sound; indeed the dharma element, which gives birth to the Victors, and wind apprehend smell;
fire apprehends form, water taste, and likewise earth apprehends the tangible.

Form is established in earth, and in the abode of the three worlds, perception is established in water. In fire it surely becomes feeling, and in wind only the aggregate of formations. Consciousness and gnosis, mingled, are established in space and in the vajra, here in this body. The elements, aggregates and all the rest come to be through the union of the female substance, the mind of awakening, and the rest.

The ear takes on the nature of vajra, O lord of men, and the mind is of the nature of space.
The nose is of the nature of earth, and likewise the tongue becomes of the nature of fire.
The eye is of the nature of water, and in the abode of the three worlds the body too is of the nature of wind.
Thus the ear and all the rest come about by the power of the qualities, through the union of gnosis and consciousness.

Miserliness arises in the gnosis element, and in the supreme body, in space, the vajra of aversion. In wind the vajra of envy, in fire the vajra of attachment, the illuminator, and likewise in water the glorious vajra of pride, and in earth the vajra of delusion: natures arising by the power of the qualities. Speech, hands, feet, the anus, all of these arise from wind, fire, water and earth.

The downward-clearing wind (apāna) arises in the gnosis element, in what is called the threefold one, and the vital wind (prāṇa) in space. The equally abiding wind (samāna) is in the midst of wind, the upward-moving wind (udāna) in fire, and the pervading wind (vyāna) in water. In earth is the nāga wind; then the winds called kūrma, kṛkara, devadatta and dhanaṃjaya: these four arise properly in wind, fire, water and earth, in sequence.
