The crown protuberance arises in the space element, and the secret place of gods and humans in the gnosis element.

The lotus of the heart is in the wind element; the throat wheel is clearly in the blazing pointed one, fire.
In water is the lotus within the brows; the navel lotus, with eight times eight petals, is in earth.
The six joints of the legs and arms are counted in threes in earth, water, fire and wind.

In the five elements beginning with earth, arising threefold through the force of the qualities, of the legs and arms
the ten fingers and toes beginning with the thumb all possess the form of joints multiplied by three;
they are to be grasped correctly as reaching to the ends of the nails, and again the teeth, by a division of the same.
Further, at the ends of the hairs it comes to the supreme body, and so too each of these in the elements.

In the secret place, the crown protuberance and the navel is the innate body of the Victor, whose nature is without intrinsic nature. In the water-born lotus of the heart it becomes the dharmakāya, and in the water-born lotus of the throat the sambhogakāya; in the drop it is the nirmāṇakāya; through the force of the qualities they become, in order, the exceptional deities. The heart wheel, the throat wheel and the lotus at the crown of the head are the Dharma, complete enjoyment, and the pure.

In the navel and throat, the secret place and crown of the head, the heart, and likewise the center of the crown protuberance, the Mother and the Lord, and the threefold vajra of body, speech and mind, arise in order. The wheel, the jewel, the lotus of space, the water-born, the supreme sword, and the vajra: these are the six families. They abide above and below in the three channels, the threefold path, divided as sun, moon and fire.

The thought that has entered the body is like space; in the midst of the thought of equal taste, the bodies too are to be known by the yogin as their nature, divided by the colors and so forth of the elements through the force of the qualities: yellow, black, the color called red, and white, in earth, wind, fire and water; in gnosis and space they become blue and green, through the division of body and thought.

Wisdom and method are bone and flesh, blood together with water, fire, and urine itself, wind and feces, emptiness and semen; in the supreme body they become the glorious menstrual substance and mind itself. The ear and the others, with sound and the rest, by the force of their own Victor family, and the nature of speech and so forth as well: thus the goddesses together with the buddhas, the objects and the subjects, are to be understood in the maṇḍalas.

The channels, six thousand multiplied by the sun (twelve), grow in the midst of the womb; when this is done and the year is complete it is released, and the hare-bearer (the moon) and the sun also cause growth. At twelve years the solar element gives forth growth in one's own body. Then the severing of the channels arises, and day after day, in order, it comes to be counted in pairs.

The glorious elements and the senses become ten, with the five faculties of action, and those called the five subtle elements together with the qualities, and mind, intellect and the supreme ego-grasping. These are the principles; in the body and in the abode of the three existences, these are enjoyment and the Principal. The self made of the five aggregates abides in the threefold existence, of those who have accumulated their own karma.

Earth is hardness, water wetness, fire heat, wind lightness, and the openings
are emptiness; the firm bones are the supreme immortal mountain, and here the rain becomes nectar.
The vajra sound and the bow are also explained; the ghaṭikās are the pairs of inhalation and exhalation.
By twice nine hundred pairs there is one transit, and the astrologers are the division of the openings.

The lunar mansions are the rows of teeth; the days and ghaṭikās become the divisions of the joints. The channels are rivers, the intestines clouds; the moon's other digits and the elements are of the nature of both. Hair, lice and semen, together with the kinds of worms, are the four modes of birth of beings. Joy and the others are the joints of the thumb; the lotus at the crown of the head and the navel lotus are also the powers.

The realm of Brahmā, the heavenly world, is the head; the feet are the human world and the world beneath the earth.
The oceans, continents and mountains are the elements whose nature is the three: wet, soft and hard.
The salt ocean is the urine; of these, the fire and wind maṇḍalas, O king, are the blood and the skin.
The hairs are all the perfected ones, established with many divisions in the abode of the three existences.

In the lotuses of the heart, throat and navel, the vajra, the sun and the pointed one blaze forth in order,
and day by day in the hearths shaped as a bow, round, and with three and with four corners as well.
Where in these the other fire, stainless, radiating light, the form of gnosis, is at the end of darkness,
that which sun and lightning do not burn, is where there is no moon, no planets, stars or the like.

The planets are in the lotus of the heart, made clear; the sixty ghaṭikās are in the navel lotus; in the middle of the brows are the day, the quarter and Manu; and again, O lord of men, in the wheel of enjoyment are the stars. Again, in the navel lotus the ghaṭikās of the lunar mansions are to be understood by yogins. The ghaṭikās and palas are clearly in the whole body, in the joints of the hands, feet and so forth.

Revolving on the twelve solar petals of the navel's water-born lotus, the supreme portions transit in order.
A transit takes place by twice nine hundred pairs of life-breaths, from the sign Cancer, O lord of men.
In whichever sign the sun abides, revolving through day and night, that is to be understood there.
The measure of the signs, the gain and the debt, and the equal too, are to be known in the right and left courses.

On each of the lotus petals they flow: in the west earth, and in the north water,
in the south fire, and in the east wind flows, and in the center of the petals, space.
Transit occurs by five divisions; when the flow is even, the breaths of the slow one are sixty.
Thus the divisions of transit come about at the time of each day, through the divisions of the signs.

The five principles beginning with space flow definitely to the north; those beginning with earth to the right.
Rāhu, Kālāgni, the moon and the sun, Mercury, the earth-born one, the minister of the asuras, the guru,
the long-tailed one and Saturn: these ten are the lords and field-holders of the maṇḍalas beginning with space.
For six months the moon becomes lord, in the north; O lord of men, in the south it is the sun.

Above that, in the region of the heart, on the petals of the eight-petaled lotus, the wheel of channels abides:
the host of winds beginning with the life-breath, with moon, sun and fire on the left, the right and the center.

On the left the channel flows as the hare-marked moon, surely white; on the right is red, the sun.
In the center the form of Kālāgni flows forth; and at the equal junctures the breaths of the slow one are sixty.

Prāṇa, apāna and samāna, in the petals of the jewel lotus, and the wind that moves upward,
vyāna, nāga and kūrma, the wind kṛkara, devadatta, and the one called dhanañjaya:
these ten kinds of wind abide in the navel wheel, distinguished by their functions.
The whole wheel of channels, from Iḍā and the rest up to Śaṅkhinī at the end, is in the lotus of one's own heart.

Prāṇa, abiding in the paths of sun and moon, is the life-giver; all food and drink
are drawn downward by apāna, and samāna, abiding in the body, takes them all as of equal taste.
The upward-moving wind makes the body move, and with mouth, hands and feet one sings and dances.
Vyāna purifies within; and it is just so that this body perishes by the power of nature and its qualities.

Nāga is belching; plainly, the drawing in of hands and feet is by the kūrma wind.
All anger and distraction come from the kṛkara winds, and yawning from devadatta.
When the body has gone to the five, one wind does not leave it, O lord of men: dhanañjaya.
Thus all the winds, prāṇa and the rest, abide by their own nature and do not give up their qualities.

The tawny one and the subtle one, with Iḍā, are the three kinds that travel the path; Rohiṇī and Hastijihvā; the sixth, the Victorious One, the Mountain-Victorious, the Wealthy One, the ninth, Alambuṣā, and glorious Kuhū. The one that completes the number of the directions is Śaṅkhinī, which, O lord of men, lets the seed of awakening flow at the end of bliss. This glorious wheel of channels becomes manifold through the many divisions of the joints.

Indra, Agni, Yama, the asura, with Varuṇa, Vāyu, the yakṣa, Raudra, and the center:
in these places its nature is supported, and its power circulates through day and night.
Above that, in the region of the throat, abides the wheel of the lunar mansions together with the host of planets.
In the glorious forehead, the realm of Brahmā, the white drop encircled by the vowels flows.

At the root is earth; on the left flows water, on the right the oblation-eater, fire, and above is wind. Between the two flows space, and the equal flow, from the nostrils in sequence. These pierce through and go to the place of the supreme power, to the end of twelve, and to the end of the digit. The fields and the other sacred places are in the hands and feet, and in the joined lotus-hands, all ending at the tips of the thumbs.

The wheel-holder in one's own body is the vajra-holder; the lords who are the supreme deities stop the twelve links.
The Kalkis are true wisdom; the elephants, horses, chariots and foot soldiers are the noble immeasurables.
The fierce ones become those called pratyekabuddhas; Hanumān is the śrāvaka; the lord of the wicked barbarians
is the sins of living beings, the mind that commits them, and the nonvirtuous path that gives suffering.

The one who rides the horse is ignorance; all the armies of the asuras are the four kinds of the māra side. Those who assemble for that battle, destroying the fear of existence, are the glorious Victor's path of liberation. Kailāsa is the giving of Dharma, which steals away the fear of existence; and the earth filled with wealth, the Thirty gods, the son of the guru of men, Brahmā and Indra, lord of the gods, are those who fill the rear and the front.

Mañjuśrī, Lokanātha, Trailokyavijaya, glorious Rajas, and Bodhicitta;
behind them, on the grounds, Brahmā and the others of many various families are the pure buddhas.
Thus the battle of the lord of the barbarians takes place, surely, in the midst of the bodies of embodied beings.
Outside, in the land of Mecca, it is an illusory form: the barbarians fight, and yet it is no battle.

The year of time, multiplied by the number of fire through the sets of days of a year, with the limbs that pass through the six classes; by the division of the months it is thirty-six; the tastes multiplied by the tastes are the families of the yoginīs. What is obtained by dividing by the planets is, clearly and surely, the deities of Kālacakra. It will be expressed through the gods and goddesses divided by the directions, O lord of men.

The three kinds of Māyājāla also arise from the limbs of the days, in the holes and in the Vedas; again they arise through the elements, the oceans, the fires and the treasuries of water; the divisions of time of the glorious Samāja are called "that very one"; others are reduced by six and divided by the division into halves; and again the mixed ones have two divisions, thirteen and nineteen, together with those very holes.

Of the sets of days fixed in the portions of the month, the six days obtained through the portion of the planets
are the six mudrās of the vajra-holder; the other sets of days are the seals of body, speech and mind.
In the wisdom body the three junctures are surely made clear, in the yoginī tantras and the yoga tantras.
The śaktis and the wrathful ones become ten in order, and five are the buddha goddesses.

Form and the rest, and the eye and the rest, are six; and again, O lord of men, the hosts of deities are twice six.
The yoginīs, the nāgas and the caṇḍālīs are called eight; all these existences are doubled.
These, in glorious Kālacakra, are a hundred and four, and half of these are in the three realms.
The hours that arise for each day from the portion of the sun, which in the middle of the month are sixty,

all of these are yoginīs, the mixed junctures in the abode of the three existences, doubled.
And the days divided from the portion of the sun, doubled, which here are the host of yoginīs,
all gathered together with their designations, become the solar days tripled.
The sun becomes the divisions of the year, and for the hare-holder, the moon, all are divisions of the fortnights.

Thus in the tantra of the Ādibuddha, the sky-goers and the lords of men, and the gods and goddesses:
wisdom and means become night and day, and the equal portion is at midnight and midday.
Whoever comes to know, in his own body, the divisions of day and night, of time, and of the monthly transits,
he is glorious Mañjuvajra, destroyer of the fear of existence, a buddha in this very birth.

By a thirtieth part, what is obtained from that, multiplied by three, is certainly the deities of Kālacakra
And the six outer mudrās; and again, from that, likewise the channels of the cakras:
At the uṣṇīṣa four, at the heart eight, at the crown sixteen, and at the throat thirty-two, the number of the teeth;
At the navel eight multiplied by eight, and twice sixteen are well known in the middle of the secret place.

Six channels are the stoppers of the cakras, and ten, which seize the sense objects, are of the nature of the gatherings. Again and again, the doublings and multiplications are the portions of phlegm, wind and bile. These certainly bestow death; by the power of the guru's certain instruction they also bestow life and freedom from illness. The channels of time in the six cakras: for yogis who rob the fear of death, there is nothing marvelous in this.

At the uṣṇīṣa four, at the forehead the times multiplied by four: the phlegm element is greatly disturbed.
At the throat thirty-two, and in the heart lotus the times multiplied by two: the bile element is greatly disturbed.
At the navel and the secret place, sixty-four and sixteen: likewise the wind element is greatly disturbed.
In the secret place the other ten and six are made clear: these are certainly the gatherings, the stoppers.

The six channels, the stoppers of the cakras, which abide upon the path in three ways, divided as moon, sun and fire,
Bestow death upon living beings; they are to be guarded in the body by the power of the guru's certain instruction.
If the life-breath enters into the six, then the death of human beings is certainly overcome.
When it has not entered the subtle one, where could yogis cut off the domain of death?

If the life-breath flows in a single channel day and night, life lasts for "time" years.
If it flows unbroken for one fortnight, the life of human beings is a year and a half.
If it flows unbroken for one month, O lord of men, life is three months.
Thus the yogi should know, in his own body, the monthly passage and the wheel of the time of death.

In the passage of days from five to twenty-five, the five arisings arise by way of increase;
Then, adding one each, ten multiplied by three, as long as three remain over.
When the time is complete: years three, two and one, and with those, six, three, two and one months;
And with those months, days fifteen, ten, five, three, two and one: this is the remainder of life.

From its own place, by a supreme portion, the wheel of the houses is abandoned; with decline and remainder equal,
By years, months and days as well, that is, three, two and one, and by months six, three, two and one,
After completion, with the equal remainder of fifteen, ten, five and three days, and entering the navel,
By thirty-six: at the end of the days, through the conjunction of the sun's signs of death, one dies.

First, the collection of days of three solar years abandons five, three and three.
Then, together with the movements, five, "time," seven, and zero, seven, one are in the third;
Two, eight, seven, five, eight, and another two: in the seventh these too are the movements.
Then all the collections of days, beginning with the lunar days, whatever they are, as far as the navel,

Until the time-year, certainly shift and move by the power of the passage of days,
On the path called moon and sun, by the increase of unequal and equal days one by one, in sequence.
Again, the divisions of shifting are made on the twelve spokes by unequal and equal days,
As long as a month and a half; the other, by thirty and by the lives, three nights.

Breaths, palas and ghaṭikās: these are the times of day and night, moving in their sequence.
From the limbs of the passage, the passage by seven years; by the power of the course, by sixty years;
From three months, by thirty years; by the power of the passage of days, by time-years and fortnights too,
On the wheels of time they move by the collections of days divided by thirty-two and a half.

Fortnight after fortnight, eight multiplied by nine channels are cut off at the navel cakras. From the joints called the wheel of action, by seven days one channel each is abandoned. From the throat, the twenty-seven channels of the lunar mansions are abandoned in sequence, day by day within a month; The quarters of the moon abiding in the drop are abandoned within a fortnight, and abide in the nine channels of the planets and in the secret place.

From the classes of letters together with the vowels, the sun moves in its passage by the divisions of the houses;
Afterwards, adding one each, day by day it turns back, for as long as the time-year.
When the year is cut off, within a fortnight, by the power of the vowel-days, there is movement and also exhaustion;
At the times of day and night, when the exhalation and inhalation of breath decline, one night of life remains.

In the lotuses of the navel, throat, forehead and one's own heart are the channels with their remainder;
Twelve, six and three abide; in one's own heart lotus, likewise, are the channels with a half.
In the joints of the hands and feet are the times multiplied by fifteen, for as long as six days;
At the end the wholly subtle one flows strongly, and the days completely abandon the lotus of the heart.

By collections of thirty-six thousand nine hundred days, the channels of time move. In the times of day and night, whatever the sun and moon cannot complete by their respective movements, The solar day becomes a debt even in the sun's movement, and in the half-month's movement of the moon; By the fine divisions of the days of the lunar mansions, death comes in the months empty of the sun's and moon's movement.

Whatever surplus of life-breath is in the body, at the most clear junction, is sixty diminished by four less a quarter;
Through the divisions of shifting, at the time of each and every day, pairs of channels are certainly destroyed.
Again and again, in the middle of the year, from the movements of sun and moon, the empty ones number two and seven.
Thus, by a hundred years each of its own measure, time passes in the lives of gods and humans.

If at times, through food, illness, or a blow, it reaches the channel of time in human beings,
then on that basis, at once and swiftly, time enters the wheels of channels.
Blocking these channels of the wheels and cutting off the course of sun and moon, by stages
it seizes what rests upon the subtle one in the central channel: O lord of men, the life of living beings.

In the glorious lotus of the heart and on the forehead move the moon's digits called "directions" and "lunar days";
in the wheels of enjoyment and of the navel, the "ten with nine," the "pair of Rudras," and the one called "Jina,"
and from the seven days called "Suchness," this rabbit-marked moon is once more cast off, out of sequence.
Having been filled in the drops, it enters the heart in the bright fortnight, for the sake of the "Vasus."

Again, in the dark fortnight it goes in the same way, by force of the digits, as far as the lotuses of the navel. At the heart and likewise at the head, the water-born lotuses with eight spokes and with sixteen spokes are named black and white; at the throat and the navel, the one with thirty-two spokes and the other with twice that are red and yellow. The two eighth days are at the navel and the throat, and at the head and the heart white and black are completed.

The thirty vowels beginning with a, joined with ha, ya, ra, va, and la, with the glorious bindu and the visarga, are divided to become sixty; they are the "seasons," O lord of men, the "Vasus," and the "teeth," on the filaments and the tips. Through the forms of the bindu and the visarga, the classes beginning with ka are distributed on the supreme lotus petals. And by force of the course of the watches, a hundred and twenty as well, the sun moves and brings about decline.

In the fortnight, through the moon's own movement, the digits called lunar days are turned into debts.
Again, through two months, the others too, the increase arising from the conjunctions of days and years,
amount to six days in a complete year; others likewise increase at the junctures through omissions.
O king, in this way this twofold debt of the sun is made to grow through the conjunctions of the years.

Six breaths make one pala, and sixty palas make one ghaṭikā; sixty ghaṭikās make a day, and days three times ten make one month; six months make a course of the sun, and the course doubled is renowned as one year. That, out of the hundred-year lifespan of persons, the debt reaches its course, is through the power of the channel of time.

In the three worlds, among asuras, gods, and humans, there is no one endowed with yoga
able to complete moon and sun in his own body, because the moon, through its own movement, moves through day and night,
and within the year, in worldly existence, the sun too, stage by stage, does not move through all the lunar mansions.
Year after year, through the debts, it devours existence: Kālacakra, the one hero.

If what is cut off is a single channel, O lord of men, the urine turns sour.
The subtle elements are cast off in their supreme portion five days before, at the time the body breaks apart:
sound from the ear, taste surely cast off from the mouth, and the pupil from the center of the eye,
smell from the nose, and from people's hands, feet, and body, in sequence, the heat.

The tip of the nose droops, and one perceives as though a hand were placed upon the crown.
Though both palms are thoroughly smeared with lac dye, they do not turn red.
One sees the sun as black in color and the moon, transformed, as yellow in color.
If urine and body too grow cold, O lord of men, death comes on that very day.

Beneath the tongue a black line appears, and in the eye a very fine line of Brahmā;
the breath streams in the paths of moon and sun, O lord of men, and the two cheeks likewise;
above the breasts it enters the armpits, and one sees all the hosts of planets with sparks.
The neck, along with the body, stiffening and spinning: these too are held to be the signs of death in the body.

That which makes the forms of birth, the time of delivery, infancy, and youth,
is the very one who gathers them back again in the worlds: the one with the wheel of time, not the sun.
The gods, moon, sun, and the rest, together with the elements, that Blessed One devours in the portion of night;
and again, in the time of day, he sends forth the infinite ones, by stages, as the cause of their production.

The six, sound and the rest, are the objects of action, and the six faculties are the instruments;
year, month, fortnight, day and night, lunar day, moon and sun are the agents.
The agent who gathers them up is here something other; the agent of production is no agent.
That space-pervading Vajra-holder of space is free of objects, without qualities, and without nature.

Smell, color, taste, and what is called touch, and earth, water, fire, and wind:
these eight possessing form are surely the aggregates of all objects, and the one becomes the principal.
This entire threefold world is emitted and gathered back through the power of time.
Sound, emptiness, the sphere of phenomena, and mind as well, O lord of men, become formless.

The self binds itself, abiding in nature (prakṛti), through the qualities and conceptual thoughts, just like a silkworm; and again the self surely releases itself, by itself, through minds free of conceptual thought. Therefore, O king, from oneself, abiding in the qualities of nature, one brings about suffering and happiness. Apart from nature, who is a maker, and what does he make? Unknowing deludes the world.

Earth, water, fire, wind, and space, and the mind of mentation, intellect, and ego-grasping,
gross and subtle, and further, abiding in the three qualities, through the divisions of letters, bindu, and the rest:
the gross abides by nature in the gross faculties, and the subtle in the subtle mind,
and in wisdom too is the wisdom body: the life free of nature and modification, both element and not element.

The five empties beginning with a are nature, not modification; the glorious kalā, the bindu, and the nāda,
and time, mind, and intellect are likewise natures; the other vowels are wind and the rest.
The gross letters of the classes are ten taken three times; the other sixteen are modifications.

In their midst, the Vajra-holder is neither nature nor modification; the pervader is without intrinsic nature.

In saṃsāra, pleasure and suffering abide in the qualities (guṇas) of nature, and what is born of karma is what is consumed. These actions of humans also become threefold: gross, subtle, and peaceful. From the claims that "the self is the agent," and that, by a distinction, "there is also another agent, the Lord of Creatures (Paśupati, a name of Śiva)": the agent is neither the self nor another; that knowing which is free of intrinsic nature is not karma.

Therefore no agent whatsoever gives or takes away the pleasure and suffering of living beings.
In saṃsāra, whatever former karma of the three kinds was done bestows its fruit.
"This creator and destroyer gives, and he takes away": that is the thinking of the deluded.
The openings in the body that steal away immeasurable virtue are not seen by one's own senses.

The power of desire, the power of action, and third, the power of wisdom are in beings' own minds;
manifesting things, engaging, and as the cause of equal taste, they apprehend objects.
And again, emission is the fourth, revered by the three worlds, and by the nondual as well, in definite sequence.
Among these, O lord of men, the Vajra-holder consumes nothing at all and emits nothing at all.

The self is not the agent, O lord of men, nor the giver of suffering and happiness.
No one at all is bound or freed, O lord of men, apart from the power of the mind.
Just as, when water is drawn from within a pot, the space does not go anywhere,
so too the space-pervading Vajra-holder of space, free of objects, is within the body.

Thus one becomes a proponent that karma exists; the Blessed One (the Buddha), the sole Teacher, is not an agent. The omniscient Ādibuddha, bowed to by the three worlds, is the holder of the Wheel of Time, not the holder of the wheel (Viṣṇu). At the lotus of his feet Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Rudra too especially take refuge; to that Kālacakra, the Victor, begetter of the supreme, without qualities and without conceptual thought, I pay homage.

With a peaceful disposition one becomes a god, O lord of men; with darkness, a hell-being;
with passion, an animal; and when these are mixed, a human upon the supreme earth.
Spirits (bhūtas) are of three kinds, when passion, darkness, and goodness are mixed with one another.
At the time of death, whatever thought of the mind is recalled, there the person is truly born.

By their divisions the gods are eightfold, in the supreme abode of the gods, while humans are just one; animals are of four kinds, and in the destiny of hell the hell-beings likewise are one. In these abodes beings wander in the course of flavors, firmly bound by the noose of karma; through many hundreds of thousands of births they do not reach the state of the supreme bliss of liberation, and are not freed.

The aggregates, the elements and the senses, the fearful threefold existence, and the five organs of action,
the subtle elements, the faults and virtues of karma, mind, intellect, the grasping at "I," and so on:
bound by these, living beings wander in the course of flavors, again through subtle things.
When things are abandoned, one goes to the supreme imperishable abode, where one is not again subject to birth.

The Vedas with their limbs, together with the texts of tradition (smṛti), and in the world, logic with its established conclusions, and the other treatises composed by poets, by Vyāsa, by Agni and the rest, are not knowledge. What is called knowledge is the immutable inner knowledge, proclaimed in this world by the sages, in which the three worlds come to be in their entirety, O lord of men, and into which they dissolve again.

O lord of yogis, if one who has not attained yoga dies with a wavering mind,
in the glorious world of humans he is born as a yogi into the supreme family of sages.
Through that former habituation he again takes up the vast yoga of wisdom completely;
having attained wisdom, he goes to the supreme imperishable abode, where there is no birth again.

Wisdom arises: for the victors, the sun is at daytime, at midnight, and at the end of night; for the asuras, the moon is at nighttime, at midday, and at the end of the day. For those whose true wisdom has declined, speech turns into Sanskrit and the vernacular; from the peaceful and the fierce arise, in the abodes of the three existences, eminent persons and ordinary ones as well.

At noon and from midnight, in the day and in the night, are their times for eating. Their food is porridge and beef; their drinks are of many kinds, nourishing, and egg broth. Their clothes are red and white; like the courses of the sun and moon, they dwell in heaven and the underworld. Their Dharma is non-harming and also harming; by the force of their gurus' rules, there is the vajra seat and also the asura seat.

The actions of the bhūtas become a guide, and their speech is mixed, that of the piśācas.
Their Dharma is non-harming and harmful; by the force of their gurus' rules, with regard to faults such as killing,
they engage in them for the sake of sacrifice and the like, O lord of men, and at times turn back from them.
Their food and drink too are mixed; from the courses of the sun and moon, their path is another guide.

The path of rays is the light of the sun, the white, the day, the northern course, and the halves of north and south as well; the light of the moon, the nights, the not-white, and this southern course: this is the path of smoke. Those of the rays go to the gods in heaven; the not-white likewise go to the world of the asuras and nāgas; the wholly mixed, O lord of men, in the human world, become hungry ghosts, animals and humans.

Whoever has entered into the midst of whatever, delighting in a fixed observance and firmly bound by the noose of karma, in its midst, by their own intentions, O lord of men, they are born into that family and become its holders. As long as the nature of the mind, by the power of threefold existence, feels pleasure and suffering, so long, O lord of men, it wanders in this terrifying saṃsāra, in heaven, in the human realm, and below as well.

The classes beginning with ka, together with the vowels, transformed by the qualities, and whatever vowels belong to the three sides, and the thirty consonants as well: from these, by the power of the threefold course, they move on through death. The feet of the five planets, the feet of the sun and of the hare-marked one (the moon), and the feet of Rāhu and the long-tailed one, thirty-two years and fortnights as well, transformed by the qualities, are divided by moon, sun and fire.

By three years come the fortnights and the qualities of the hare-marked one, and the two fortnights of Kālāgni; less than a hundred years, the remaining fortnights, in the equal and unequal courses of sun and moon, numbering "world and eye" and "fire and eye"; the periods of day and night, the principal ones, and the slow one, all of them: the classes of consonants and the rest, and the vowels, are devoured by death through the power of the courses of sun and moon.

The water-clock periods of the wheel together with its half, apart from the feet of the hare-marked one, and the lesser periods of the planets, these too, through the divisions of wisdom and means, are devoured by death through the times of day and night. Thus the classes with vowels, the consonants together with the assemblies of vowels, and the feet pertain to lesser and middling timely death, to the death of moon and sun, and to the deity of the surplus consonants.

Thirty-two, and one day as well, in the course of the sun together with the hare-marked one: this is the cutting of the channels. Again, with thirty, by three nights, it casts off the aggregates, elements, senses and the rest, together with the vajra. Having abandoned the channels of moon and sun, the vital wind enters the conch-bearer channel (śaṅkhinī) for one day, for as long as the elements of earth, water, fire, wind and space are being severed.

First, the entire body of the Victor, as the cause of accomplishment, should be well protected by mantra practitioners. Without the body there is no accomplishment, nor will supreme bliss be attained in this birth. Therefore, as the cause for the body's sake, at the time of each day and night, one should cultivate the yoga of the channels. If the body is accomplished, other accomplishments become servants in the abodes of the three realms of existence.

In emptiness, the path of smoke and the rest, by the power of the guru's certainty, one should cultivate the image at the juncture. Then, in the wheels of the channels, one makes the prāṇa and apāna winds stable; afterwards the yogin surely arrests the moon, the mind of awakening, as an eclipse does. Then whatever small thing one desires is attained in this birth within a few days.

When sun and moon are arrested in their equal and unequal courses, they enter the fire of the central channel.
Once the first day has passed, prāṇa and apāna both cheat untimely death.
Having filled with the life-wind the joints of the fingers and toes of hands and feet, up to the tips of the nails,
the yogin should meditate on the buddha goddesses in the six wheels, fearless, with lotus hands.

Contracting the apāna is desired for wind diseases in the upper body, all of them;
breath control is for phlegm, and releasing the winds again is for disorders of bile;
properly arresting above and below brings ease in combined disorders and in fevers;
the life-wind above the navel takes away all diseases, and at the root of the navel the others too.

Contracting the apāna wind that moves on the threefold path, and the life-wind from above as well,
for as long as they properly meet, the fire grows strong and pervades the whole body.
Dropsy, tumors, hemorrhoids and diseases that have reached the stomach are destroyed by the lunar yoga,
as are asthma, cough, the three kinds of poison, eye diseases and the rest.

From the right armpit and the one not right, pressing forcefully on the breasts and holding back the life-wind,
from the right it at once flows down into the left channel, and from the left armpit to the right.
Even within half a month, the yogin endowed with yoga removes the omens of death that would bring death.

Seated in the vajra posture, with the lotus hands grasping the feet, one destroys pain in the back.

With the feet above and the head below, it removes all diseases of phlegm from the body.
By restraining the life-breath for some days, one surely destroys diarrhea and diseases of urination.
At dawn, rubbing the rows of teeth well with the ring fingers, in a mouth free of water,
becomes a salve for the eyes; and sprinkling them with water also removes disease.

Lying on one's back and grasping the navel with the lotus hands destroys the pain of indigestion.
When tumors arise, fumigating them with salt together with ghee surely destroys their growth,
and so does smearing them with arka milk. If at some time there is toothache with tooth-worms,
smoke from the seeds of vyāghrī, held here with a tube and the like, destroys the worms and the rest.

In half a year it removes white leprosy from the body of the best of mantra practitioners; what need to speak of other ailments? And when one's own mind falls in union with the wisdom consort, by constantly restraining it with the life-wind, the old age of a seventy-year-old, with its white hair, is surely removed when two full years are completed. From the close union of the path and the mind for some days, thereupon the accomplishment of the mudrā arises.

One should practice breath control intensely, for as long as the heart and likewise the head are tormented by fire.
Then, above, in the excellent lotus, one swoons; and through not restraining it, or through force,
the crown is pierced, and having cast off the aggregates and elements, one goes to death.
For persons who have fallen away from union with the mudrā, it gives no fruit of bliss in this very birth.

It was taught for the sake of meditating with the mudrā at the times of day and night, and not at all for the sake of exhausting desire. It is for the sake of worshipping the vajra of speech; intoxicants were surely not taught to mantra practitioners for their own sake. All food is for the sake of well-being; nor was it taught as a cause of indigestion at the times of each day and night. The glorious conduct is a cause of accomplishment; wandering in charnel grounds at night is not for the sake of play.

Slightly warm akṣobhya, that is, urine, calms diseases of the mouth, and toothache as well.
Snuffing akṣobhya at dawn removes diseases of the head, and likewise snuffing water.
Urine poured warm and cold into the ears and eyes removes the diseases of both.
For one tormented by bhūtas, snuffing akṣobhya with the three pungents gives ease, and so does eating it.

Eating feces, urine, semen and blood together with human flesh bestows long life. Snuffing the flower while in meditative concentration removes old age with its white hair, and the growths on the body as well. Eating the five lamps mixed with honey removes all disease, and eating a woman's flower mixed with semen for one year surely dispels old age.

Salty and sour things destroy wind; sweet and astringent things are the enemies of bile.
All bitter and likewise pungent things destroy phlegm, whether as medicines or as decoctions.
Goat's milk with the three pungents destroys phlegm; buffalo's milk is the enemy of bile.
Camel's milk destroys wind; and so does the milk that removes all three kinds of disease, and likewise ghee.

Warm water in which jātī has been boiled calms diseases of the mouth, and toothache as well.
Sesame oil boiled with water, with the three pungents and salts, destroys diseases of the ears.
Ghee and milk boiled with nāga and the red substance always destroy diseases of the nose.
Smearing with karkoṭī, lāṅgalī and indī together with donkey's urine removes chains of tumors.

With the hands hanging down and the lotus feet placed evenly, one should restrain the life-wind,
until the body falls to the ground, and until the fevers are released.
Again and again in meditative absorption, the thorn that causes the fear of death is surely destroyed
by meditating on Viśvamātā in the heart lotus, having the form of the moon, like the stainless hare-bearer.

Drinking ground small lentils destroys the thorn of people struck by fever.
At the end of the fever, a skull, ground and mixed with akṣobhya, also removes the terrifying one;
and the mantras are the names of water, fire and wind, preceded by the syllable oṃ and by vajra.
With these very mantras one should protect the head, throat, heart, navel, secret place and so forth.

Equal parts of bhātrī and dhānya, and likewise powder of tintiri leaves, should be boiled in stainless water touched by the sun and moon, down to a quarter remaining. Its decoction, mixed with sugar and drunk for three days, starting on another day, removes the deadly fever of early summer burned by the rays of the sun, even when it has entered the seven bodily constituents.

With gold, the sun, magnetic iron, and iron born from paṭa, one should set it with mākṣika.
With half the amount of the seed, purified mercury free of impurities should be made into six measures.
Having killed it with cow's buttermilk and the fierce sharp-pointed one, one should take the essence here.
The glorious pellet is again boiled in a magnetic vessel until it is reduced to half.

Taking it out, one should mix with it eight or nine measures of pure sugar.
It should be eaten at all times, for as long as one goes on abstaining from food.
In six months one has a divine body, free of white hair and wrinkles; by this one becomes deathless.
Therefore it should be eaten at the time of each day, though in an amount less than a pellet.

The seed and the king spoken of before are in equal parts, and mercury is also poured in, half as much.
They are kneaded with salts and sour things until, with many kinds of medicines, they become one.
With sour bile, sulfur and the like, darada and śilā as well are kneaded in equal measure with the mercury.
Silver leaf smeared with it becomes gold, when it is cooked together with half the amount.

Sharp metal, the sky-born, āla, salt, yavakṣāra and sarja, in that order,
increasing, with the sun doubled; by making the tastes equal, the remainder of the sun is to be burned.
Purified again with salt, and likewise with what comes from the cow and the like, the base metal becomes soft.
Given a third part of silver, filled and beaten, it too becomes the very flower of the pure.

Cardamom, camphor, mālā, cala, ghana, the fruit-bearing, crow and mountain-born, kakkola, siddhamūtra, utpala, fruit, deer-born, red, daityapūti, nāga, the cool, raṇa, leaf, pala, water, creeper, sky and the crooked: these, with the five substances, form the treasury of scents, together with musk, camphor and the rest, and with incense, flowers, liquor and the like.

Eye, moon, fire, ocean, arrow, quality, water-treasure, taste, hand, moon, arrow and eye;
moon, fire, ocean, moon, time, time, arrow, eye, water-treasure, arrow, eye, moon and worlds:
these numbers of parts, for cardamom and the rest, are laid out in order in squares, five by five.
The substances placed in the city of the joined squares, their pure parts called days, become a perfume.

Jāti and the rest, cardamom, creeper and leaf are to be set in order in the city of Kailāsa.
Lac, sarja, milk, pura and sita are to be used as fumigants in the work of fumigation.
Camphor, sugar, flowers possessing taste, and nails, with a fire-portion:
these, free of honey and mixed with scented water, are to be made into incense sticks.

The navel first, siddhamūtra and camphor are to be set in the city of space for bathing preparations.
Granthi, tiger, hareṇu, hata, and likewise others, and portions of bark such as conch;
dhūnya, rbīmu, the hundred-marked, dama and madhuri, likewise also for rubbing unguents;
as stated before, not reversed, like the water-born, body, granthi and pamja, also for sesame oil.

For powders granthi likewise changes; body and hata for drinks, for anointing, and for the mouth as well.
For the bark kind, granthi and conch; fruit and leaf combined for ripening, and for clarifying and the rest.
Thus, well adorned with thirty divisions, one should make many kinds of preparations of scents, incense and the like.
The substances are purified, ripened and anointed by combining with fruit, and also by piercings.

First, an eighth part, by virtue of the leaf, becomes astringent; twice that is the fierce incense.
Afterwards molasses in the measure of the substances is to be burned, increasing by the spring preparations.
A quarter part of conch incense, and white honey, equal to the substances, must be burned.
The pellets are the measure of a conch; malaya, again, and cala with the moon, likewise.

The ripened scent is to be infused with fine flowers for some days, as long as one wishes.
Afterwards the piercing is made with a hundredth part, with the three fruits and the moon, together with mada liquor.
Water of śigru, chāga mūtra, and flower essence in equal measure, and at the end the flower-born, are to be boiled.
In one month it becomes ripened for the teeth: with deer in equal measure, liquor, and the navel, the piercing.

The secret: for scents, pūti, rasa, nails and cala; the secret in incense preparations.
Likewise the cool, turuka, guru and camphor too, and flowers for the work of anointing.
For piercing, camphor, navel, the three fruits and mada liquor, for proper bathing preparations.
The secret: bark, granthiparṇa, with the forest-roamer, and granthi also for rubbing unguents.

The water-born, pure, lesser in substances and free of sweet things, is ground with scented water.
The ripening of incense: astringent, fierce, with honey, and with nails, the morsels increasing in order.
Two morsels are mixed with sugar, with malaya, cala, iron and camphor also.
At the beginning and end of the morsels, honey and white must be burned; first from the incense.

For some days perfume it with fine flowers, and mix it with scented water.
Put it in a clay bowl that does not leak, bind it with wax and cloth, and seal its mouth firmly.
Place it in a very large vessel, or else underground, and fill around it with sand.
Through six months of this procedure it matures: kept in water, it becomes the foremost of navel-scents.

Purify sesame seeds with water in which leaves have been boiled, and with incense, unguents and the like.
Afterward, for some days, as long as one wishes, perfume them with jasmine and other flowers.
When the sesame oil has been pressed out in presses, the wise lord of men should store it in a glass vessel.
Used for bathing or for anointing the limbs, this intoxicating flower-scented sesame oil does not spoil.

Mountain, sun, moon, Manu, eye, desire, vasu, Rudra, king, fire,
directions, elements, apertures, six, lunar day and ocean should be placed in the squares in order.
The total for four squares is ocean and the crested one. Draw the whole glorious diagram
on the back of a measure, and show it to pregnant women when the time of giving birth has come.

The yoginīs who are renowned on the bright earth as the mothers of the eight eighths
are those called Womb and those called Day, three times nine, and ten, and eleven, and the others three fifths,
and those called Month and those called Year. All of them seize children upon the earth.
They cause stabbing pain in the womb, and at the time of birth they cause sharp pains in the birth passage as well.

In those who have been born they enter the child's being. According to the divisions of day, month and year,
the five fierce youths, by the power of the qualities of nature, abide at the junctures.
All of them become terrifying on their own lunar days and seize the child, and, O king, they do not let it go.
To pacify them, various maṇḍalas, baths and fire offerings are employed here.

Kuṣṭha, uśīra, kaseru, tagara, utpala and lotus filaments,
ground with cool water and consecrated with the vajra mantra, are given for pains of the womb.
If the womb is rigid, grind mongoose and eight peacock feathers and give them.
Milk, ghee, porridge and barley flour with curd and molasses are given to the Day Mothers.

The five kinds of cooked food, with curd and molasses, and polikā and modaka cakes,
scent, flowers, lamps and water for washing, together with leaves, should be offered for five nights.
An incense is made of cow's teeth, sheep's horn, deer's nails, hair and snakeskin.
For the growth of children born into their months, all the things described go into the tormas as well.

The five foods to eat and the five to chew, the flesh of creatures that live in water, scent, flowers, lamps,
liquor, the incense and washing water described before, and likewise directional tormas for each direction:
these are for children born into their years, O lord of men, that they may clearly flourish on the earth.
From the womb up to the fifteenth year, the yoginīs are certainly to be worshiped.

The limbs stretch, the eyes hurt, and mouth, hands and feet turn yellow;
the urine is yellow in color, there is what is called fever, and vomiting, dryness and fainting.
Once the signs have been recognized, O lord of men, a maṇḍala and fire offering should be performed for their sake.
If this is not offered, the mothers, born from the elements by the power of the qualities of nature, will not let go.

The child whose limbs all turn white, O lord of men, with very fine pustules,
with neck and body twisted, and blood flowing from mouth, genitals and anus:
for such a one worship should not be performed, for deluded mantra practitioners are only diminished by it.
That child will die; surely neither gods nor humans nor hand-goers are able to protect it.

Nāgas, yakṣas, whatever grahas, those born in the asura clan, rākṣasas and piśācas,
the Sīkaṃnyas, the Lāmās of fumigation, the ḍākinīs who delight in human blood, the Rūpiṇīs,
kumbhāṇḍas, field protectors (kṣetrapālas), Gaṇapati, pretas, vetālas, siddhas,
and the lords of the ḍākinīs together with the apasmāras: when worshiped in maṇḍalas, they bring supreme well-being.

For worshiping the fierce ones a maṇḍala is made, O lord of men, outside the town,
at a tree, in a charnel ground, at a temple of a high god, or where rivers meet.
It measures one cubit, or two, four, nine or thirteen, O lord of men, according to the number of deities.
In the center is an eight-spoked wheel, which, by the power of the family, is half the extent of the maṇḍala.

The doors are an eighth of the wheel; the friezes and the garlands are certainly half of that.
The walls are half the frieze, and the portals are three times the measure of the doors.
The round hearth is a third, filled with white colors in the nature of a white lotus.
With the five glorious colors, draw the deities' own emblems in their own directions, according to their families.

A vajra in the center and a sword in the direction of Indra, the east; by the power of the family, a red jewel in the south; on the left, white lotuses with a hundred petals; and in the west the emblem of the wheel; in the fire direction, the southeast, curved knives standing on lotus petals; in the asura corner, the southwest, hooks; in the wind direction, the northwest, a vajra noose, O lord of men; and on the petals of Rudra, the northeast, tridents.

At the eastern door, a sword like a black cloud; in the south, a vajra staff.

in the west, the direction of water, glorious clubs, and in the north, hammers as well.
Knowing what each mind inclines toward, make these Kālacakra wheels in their very many forms, as many as are needed,
for sons for barren women, and for the pacifying and increasing of people struck down by grahas. 152.

Eight vases with jewels, with petals and lotus mouths, and unused bowls, washed with the seven, water, the five nectars and the rest, and then sprinkled thoroughly again with white mustard seed and the like: with scents, incense, lamps of many kinds, fruits, flavors, white flowers and garments, make a splendid offering, and after that perform the fire offering with the pacifying substances to be burned. 153.

Milk, barley, sesame and the like, kindling sticks of the five milky trees, hundred-petaled lotuses,
water for welcome, invitation, water to refresh the mouth, and likewise honoring and worship:
these are to be done upon the supreme earth to pacify those tormented by the mātṛkās.
Then, O lord of men, the thirty-six yoginīs are worshipped at all times. 154.

Selflessness, the ripening of karma, the three realms of existence, the destinies of beings in time, arising from the twelve-linked dependent origination, the four truths, the nine doubled, which are the unshared qualities of the buddhas, the five aggregates, the three bodies, innate wisdom, and likewise suchness, and an emptiness that is not inert: wherever these are proclaimed, that is certainly the teaching of the Vajra Holder. 155.

Where the Veda is self-arisen, and the mouth, arms, feet and so on are the birthplaces of people,
and it is said there is no dharma higher than the horse sacrifice (aśvamedha), that is the teaching of Brahmā.
The agent, the self, karma, time, nature and the qualities (guṇas), and emptiness a corrupt doctrine,
the agent as the cause of results, taught clearly and decisively: that here belongs to the followers of Viṣṇu. 156.

The six paths, the five principles, the supreme state in its entirety, and further the body of mantra, the śiva-state of the supreme lord of knowledge, the reality of the joining and release of those who dwell in the threefold abode, the divisions of the bindu, the śiva-state, and the divisions of the twelve knots that reside throughout the body: wherever all these are found, that is certainly the teaching of the Śaivas. 157.

There is no Lord, nor any ripening of karma; enjoyment is that of the assemblage of elements, qualities and objects; when that is absent there is no result: know this as plainly taught by the guru of the gods (Bṛhaspati). The creator produces all that is born from causes and from what is not a cause, and he is the cause of the liberation of the Tājikas; by pleasing him, people certainly go to the higher realms: that is the teaching of Rahmāna. 158.

The three times, the six substances, established through nine categories, the soul with its parts in six kinds of body,
further the five existent bodies, and the divisions of vows, restraint, movement, knowledge and conduct;
the soul is the size of the body, and through countless existences liberation is by the permanent soul;
where the place of liberation is declared to be above, that is the teaching of the Jinas. 159.

This Veda is not self-arisen: within the three realms it is Vedic sound that expresses meaning.
Formerly, through the four faces of Brahmā, the meaning was made clear by Vedic sounds as well.
If sound and meaning were not different, why would saying the word "fire" not burn the mouth?
Therefore there certainly is a teacher of it, for objects of knowledge and for things future and past. 160.

The Veda is not like space: here it was uttered by the mouth through the divisions of the places of articulation. Since by caste it is recited by the mouths of the twice-born, it is limited; were it all-pervading, others too would recite it. Since the castes beginning with the śūdras neither recite nor inquire into these Vedas, which are not all-pervading, for people who are wise and learned the Vedas do not become valid cognition. 161.

If there is a Lord who makes all things, then he alone, and no other, is the one who experiences the karma of beings. If the Lord is not the agent of karma, then he cannot be the maker of absolutely everything. If he is impelled by other agents, then this supreme among agents is wholly dependent on others. Therefore, setting aside the karma of living beings, there is no Lord, no agent who bestows the fruits of virtue and nonvirtue. 162.

If here the atoms of earth, water, fire and wind did not exist in space before the creator, then where there are no substances, the all-maker, devoid of objects, could not make the variety of things. For this creator, devoid of objects, there is no valid cognition, neither direct perception nor knowledge of the hidden. All the forms of karma come about from conjunction alone, O lord of men, not by anyone's wish. 163.

From conjunction, even from a moonstone, water arises; from mirrors, the forms of things;
from a cause that is sour, the tongue waters; from one's own sound, its echo; from a pure seed, a sprout.
From a magnet, iron needles move: in this, wishing plays no part at all.
This power of things, within the three realms, was created by no one whatsoever. 164.

If the self were all-pervading, how would it experience the suffering of separation from loved ones? If it were permanent, why would it be changed in the state of being struck by the arrow of desire? If it were endowed with activity, how would it fall into stupor at the time of sleep? Thus this self is certainly not all-pervading, not a pervasive lord, nor endowed with the activities described before. 165.

Without a self there is arising, and there are fruits of virtue and nonvirtue, though devoid of an agent; without a goer there is going, for the sake of complete liberation; there is bondage, yet no one is bound; things and non-things too exist, and there is existence of what is free from momentariness and without intrinsic nature. These are my words of truth: they cannot be overturned by the reasoned words of gods and nāgas. 166.

Whoever says that the reality called "person" dwells within the body is defeated by the very nature of things.
The one who speaks of objects in conventional terms holds that ultimately there is no ignorance.
The one who declares the entire three realms to be consciousness alone asserts consciousness.
Whoever proclaims compassion and emptiness as nondual, where the other positions fail, remains unfailing. 167.

Beings experience their former deeds; what is done here is for another birth.

If that is so, the body does not perish; through the births and further births of humans,
through countless existences, one comes forth from saṃsāra, yet there is no entry into liberation.
The Tāyins too firmly hold these views; they are utterly inferior to the other lineage.

If, when the elements become one, mind together with its faculties fully arises, like the potency of liquor,
why then does it not become a person in trees, since earth, water, fire, wind and space combine in them?
If these have no potency to make a living being, then the potency of the combining of the elements is utterly false.
These words of the Cārvākas do not give people the fruit of happiness; theirs is a ruined path.

If the life-principle is the size of the body, then when hands and feet are cut off, is it diminished?
Do permanent things, through the power of the body, become subtle and also grow gross?
Who, freed from the karma of saṃsāra, goes to an abode of bliss located at the top of the world?
Whatever in the three worlds is arranged by atoms is never permanent, because of time.

With these and other words, for the sake of gnosis, Kālacakra made the teaching fully clear on earth,
following the minds of persons according to the force of their gentle, harsh and other habitual tendencies.
Since mind, like crystal, takes on the color of the colored things placed near it,
therefore the yogin refutes no dharma at all, whether of his own lineage or of others.

Dharma is benefit to sentient beings and freedom from objects; non-dharma is harm. Violence, with the Vedas as its authority, gives no fruit of happiness and at all times gives suffering. The holy ones endowed with love, far from the words of the deluded, bring about supreme bliss and delight in the happiness of beings. Therefore, O lord of men, with a mind set solely on the welfare of beings, meditate on the absence of intrinsic nature.

I, the guru of gods and humans, am Indra in the heavenly worlds and the wheel-turning monarch on earth;
in the underworlds I am the nāga king, bowed to by the hooded race; I am the all-pervading and the supreme;
I am gnosis, the Buddha, the lord of sages, the unchanging supreme, the all-pervading lord, the vajra yoga of yogins,
and the pure syllable auṃ of the Vedas. O king, all beings have gone for refuge to me.

You are mother, you are father, you are the guru of beings, you are kinsman and good friend;
you are protector, you are the maker, the benefactor and the remover of sin, you are the perfect state;
you alone are the abode, you are the abode of supreme qualities, you are the one who has destroyed all faults;
you are the protector of the lowly, the wish-fulfilling jewel; O lord of Victors, I go for refuge to you.

From the great glorious Kālacakra, extracted from the glorious primordial Buddha, this is the second chapter, called The Inner Determination.

The various measures in the body, the times of day and night, the divisions of the moon's transits,
the subtle number of the channels and their nature, the views of non-Buddhist persons,
and the divisions such as the Vedas and the creator: having heard these, I ask you, please teach the maṇḍalas.
Having heard the words of Sucandra, the Sugata fully proclaimed the maṇḍala of Kālacakra.

First, one should properly rely on gurus who keep the pledges and abide above all in the Vajrayāna,
who meditate on reality, are unattached, free of stains, of patient disposition, and have entered the path,
who give the path to disciples, remove the fear of hell, and through reality itself possess pure conduct,
who are a vajra staff against the māras; such a one is renowned on the earth as Vajrasattva.

One who is proud, overcome by anger, devoid of pledges, greedy for goods, and without learning,
who seeks to deceive disciples, whose mind has fallen from the abode of supreme bliss, who is not empowered,
who clings to enjoyments, is careless, speaks harsh words, and lusts after the objects of the senses:
such a one wise disciples should shun like hell, for the sake of perfect awakening.

One whose mind is set on the profound and vast, who delights in certainty about the guru, is generous by nature and knows good qualities, who seeks liberation, reveres the tantra, has an unwavering mind, and keeps what he has gained of reality deeply secret, who has given up the company of the wicked: such a disciple should be taken in by very learned gurus as a candidate for the wisdom empowerment and the rest; the other, the middling one, as a cause of merit.

At the end of the dark fortnight, on the juncture of the bright, the guru who benefits others, having erected the maṇḍala, should give to his sons, for the sake of merit, the seven empowerments that remove the stains of sin. Or else, in order to teach the treasury of all qualities, one should make offerings to the yoginīs. If there is no offering, the guru should not teach from the king of tantras for one year.

For the sake of the empowerment one should examine the ground: forests, cities, towns and the quarters of villages; the characteristics of the hearths, along with the vases, the colored powders, the fire offerings, the ritual daggers and so on; the gathering of disciples, and the drawing of the maṇḍala of the supreme lord of Victors: having learned these, the master should perform all the rites, pacifying and the rest, for the fruits of virtue and nonvirtue.

The classes of ground are fourfold by force of their qualities: śūdra, vaiśya, kṣatriya and brahmin; they are to be known by their colors, black, yellow, red, and white as the moon. Rotten, fishy, lotus-scented and of supreme fragrance, in that order, are the kinds of earth. Sour and salty belong to the śūdra, sweet and pungent to the vaiśya and the kṣatriya, and the remaining to the twice-born.

The white are for pacifying and increasing; those like a black cloud are for killing and expelling;
the red are for subjugating and summoning; those like fine gold are for immobilizing and stupefying.
The green serves as the active portion for all; it is the class of the fifth and the last.
The yogin should know it as possessing all tastes and smells, a treasury of all qualities.

The ground in the northeast, Īśāna's quarter, and in the north serves for pacifying and increasing;
in the southeast, Agni's quarter, and in the eastern part, clearly and certainly, for killing and expelling.

In Nairṛti's quarter and in the south, clearly and always, are subjugation and summoning.
In the quarter of the wind and in the west, surely, O supreme lord of men, are paralysis and stupefaction.

The fire pits in the eight directions of the village are surely of these kinds: round, and with as many corners as the oceans, a half-moon, five-cornered, and, according to their nature and qualities, seven-cornered, and three-cornered, six-cornered, and eight-cornered. According to the family, the emblems at their heart are a lotus, a wheel, a curved knife, a sword, what is called an arrow, a vajra, a hook, chains, and a snake.

One, two, a half, and one cubit; and "space and time," "space and eyes," "space and fire," "space and time" become finger-widths. By portions of half the six limbs, by the sun and by portions of the sun, come the depth, the rim, the lip, and the garland of emblems; on the rims, according to the family, all around. Outside the rim make lotus petals, O lord of men, for pacifying and increasing, and not for the others.

Or else, for all rites, a vajra is used. Of the pegs driven into the ground there are eight kinds:
of nyagrodha, of aśvattha, of bone, of iron, of khadira wood, of mango, of bilva, and of arka.
Likewise crystal vases, vases of the nature of fine silver, glorious skull cups, iron ones,
those called copper, and golden vases, clearly; and surely those made of wood and those of the nature of earth.

The round vase is said to be eight times two finger-widths; its height is ten multiplied by two, and its lip two finger-widths; its neck is six, its mouth eight finger-widths; white like the hare-bearer (the moon), it is for pacifying and increasing, not for the others. From morning on, the eight watches, at their respective times of day and night, serve for pacifying, increasing and the rest. Likewise, in their half-watches, during day and night, the eight rites should be performed.

Midday and midnight, in the day and in the night, are to be avoided for pacifying.
Conjunctions when malign planets are in place are well known to be likewise terrors of death.
Fuel sticks with milk, a hundred "arrows," are for pacifying and increasing; for killing, human bones.
For separating, crow tail feathers and khadira wood; kiṃśuka is for summoning and subjugating.

Bilva and datura, half a cubit, numbering a hundred "arrows," are for paralysis and stupefaction.
Milk, butter, blood, fat, dust, vajra water, saliva and liquor are to be burned;
dūrvā grass, grain, meat and poison, and likewise mustard, red flowers,
garlands of old bilva, and golden flowers: these are for pacifying and the rest.

Yama, Nairṛti's corner, with the water lord, the wind, the yakṣa, the Fierce One, Indra, and Agni:
these become the seats of the master, O lord of men, for the rites of pacifying and the rest.
Colors belong to the two kinds of rite; on the principal lotus too they are white, black, sun-red and yellow.
Outside, by the division of the buddhas, the colored grounds are in the east, the south, the west and the north.

In the fire pits or in the maṇḍala, according to the family, colors are also laid in the directions. For the placing and so on, the purifying tormas and so on, the deities are emanated from the vajra at one's own heart. On the arghya vessel of crystal and the rest, a lotus with jewel petals, twelve thumb-widths; at the tip of the handle a bowl the size of cupped hands, and the small ladle too, for pouring oblations in the fire offering.

They become peaceful, wrathful and passionate according to the family; the deity has the form for paralysis. Likewise for the two kinds of rite, clearly and surely, in the maṇḍala too it becomes the presiding deity. The lord of Victors is fivefold, because he abides in the threefold existence, by the division of aggregates, elements and the rest; and therefore in the maṇḍalas of the deities the colored powders are of five kinds.

The measuring cord is eight cubits, triple-twisted, as thick as one barley grain of the master's thumb, a single cord lying along three paths, and no other. For pacifying and the rest, the postures arranged in order are the vajra cross-legged posture, the asura posture, squatting, the half cross-legged posture, the two divided ones, and resting the foot at the anus: the seats according to the division of rites.

Crystal, pearl, the teeth or bones of humans or donkeys, bodhi seeds, those called lotus seeds, indrākṣa, putrañjīva, according to the Sugata families, become the rosaries for mantra recitation. Worship with fragrant white flowers, pungent black ones, and red and yellow ones. The incenses are cooling, of feces, of meat, with honey and with blood, fierce incense, and astringent.

The yantra is drawn on a nyagrodha leaf, a charnel-ground shroud, birch bark, or an arka leaf,
with ground sandalwood and śāli rice, charcoal from the charnel ground, blood and poison,
saffron, red sandalwood, the juice of the three fruits, turmeric and gorocana;
the pens are dūrvā, a cooling stem, bone, a tail feather, gold, khadira, bilva and arka.

In a clay bowl or a glorious skull cup, with the three sweet substances, blood, monkey fat, a lump, in the midst of bricks, in a moist place, clean places, a house of corpses, ground heated by fire, and the earth, the yantra is to be placed; according to virtuous or nonvirtuous rites, the mantra practitioner should understand this. The moon, an elephant, a preta, a camel, a deer, a horse, cattle and a tortoise are on the body in sequence.

With glorious vajras, the host of māras abiding in every direction is surely to be burned. Then, on a ten-spoked wheel, in the directions and intermediate directions, one should meditate on the host of wrathful ones. In the center of the wheel is Vajravega, lord of the wrathful ones, with arms numbering the Victors plus two, and faces numbering time. Then whatever small aim one endowed with certainty in the guru desires, the practitioner should accomplish.

With the three vajras, on the earth maṇḍala, the three fences stand out to the limit of far sight;
from dawn to the end of the shadow, from the place of the ground up into the sky, the vajras are piled up.
In the center is a lotus of a sun's number of cubits; its hub together with the seat measures time in cubits.

Upon that, the yogin, free of all faults, should meditate on himself.

Here, in the directions of the ground, one should first give torma to the field protectors (kṣetrapālas) with the three vajras; then, with the four vajras, one should utterly burn the hosts of māras dwelling in the directions and the intermediate directions. Inviting the earth goddess Pṛthivī, one should offer her the welcoming water with flowers and water. Having supplicated her, one says: "Then please carry out the purifications of the ground as you wish."

"When the Victor destroyed Māra together with the lord of the gods, O goddess, you were the witness.
Therefore you are to be worshipped, and the supreme gods bow to you. Accept, accept my offering of welcoming water!
Just as the Victor, for the sake of awakening, utterly destroyed even the powerful armies of Māra,
so I too, for the sake of empowering the disciples, shall bring the māras to ruin.

"May the buddhas and bodhisattvas, free of faults, with their consorts, who are in all the directions, out of their supreme compassion, please protect me for the sake of the empowerment in the maṇḍala." Having supplicated them all, one should drive into the ground pegs of khadira wood, hard by nature. The wrathful ones of the directions, bearing their weapons, should be set in the quarters of the directions for protection.

If thorns together with charcoal, horns, and the terrors of death have entered the core of the ground,
then in those places, through their properties, there will be ruin; for that reason the ground should be purified.
Jewels, conch shells and beads in the ground are for maṇḍalas of victory and of well-being,
for palaces and for houses, and here, clearly and certainly, for sacrifices and the like.

When this is complete, one purifies and takes possession of the ground, and likewise duly gathers the sons.
On the twelfth day one wishes to lay the lines; on the fourteenth days the glorious colored powders are spread.
On the full moon the supreme guru gives the empowerments and the rest, and also on the days of the destroyer of Māra.
On that night consecration takes place, on a day of the Victor's class; on other nights there is no consecration.

Through pure conjunctions of time, planets and the like, consecration for kings takes place on other nights.
When Jupiter and Venus have set, then certainly no wedding takes place.
When the masters have fully understood both the worldly and the transworldly at the site of the ground,
then whatever acts they perform, all of them bestow their results accordingly.

At the disciple's head and heart, likewise at the crown protrusion, the navel, the throat and the glorious secret lotus, protection is made by those of both families, the Victors and the rest, distinguished as body, speech and mind. Likewise images and the like, and books, are consecrated with the glorious vowels, clearly and certainly. "For the liberation of sentient beings, I, so-and-so, shall also construct the maṇḍala of the Principal."

A pure site, well filled, laid out perfectly level, raised like the back of a tortoise, measuring one cubit and so forth, six, eight, and sixteen, and likewise thousands: having set the measuring cord, the vajra and the colored powders in the east of the gods, the south of Yama and the west of the Lord of Waters (Varuṇa), and the vajra and bell in the north, if signs are found, one should lay the maṇḍala lines on the first side and on the last.

If the measuring cord breaks, the guru declines; if the cross-bar line goes awry, so do the sons.
If the colored powders are carried off by the wind, this signals terror in the land and also terror for the king.
On seeing these bad signs, one should again perform the recitation of the mantras of the principal deities.
When signs are found once more, one should accomplish the laying of the lines with even and uneven steps.

A cross-bar that falls in the middle of the limbs of wisdom and means is to be avoided in maṇḍalas. On the ground at the time of battle, like a hooded serpent in the midst of an army stationed in fire and in wind, the army is defeated in battle; on those grounds it is struck down in the combat of omens. Therefore, in battle and in empowerments as well, the mantra practitioner should know the cross-bar to be exceedingly powerful.

With A and the rest and KA and the rest, joined with the vajra, on the lotus at one's own heart, upon moon, sun and fire, one meditates on glorious Kālacakra, bearer of the hare-marked moon, his extended right leg trampling Kāma (the god of desire); and on the lotus at the heart of the Principal with his Wisdom consort, joined with sun and moon, with one's respective vajra hooks one summons the buddhas and goddesses, sets them as one taste in the colored powders, and meditates on them in the measuring cord as well.

These lines, six and ninety, are given out from the Brahmā line into the portions of the directions.
With the lines of eight and six, leaving out half a finger-width, the core maṇḍala arises in the center.
From the core outward, all the layouts: the great maṇḍala, the borders of the doors,
the walls, the portals and the rest; and outside, on the ground, the fire and the moving wind maṇḍalas are to be seen.

From the lotus-seat of the deities and the rest of the Principal's wheel, threefold, in the place of Brahmā,
it extends by twelve squares; and again with one it becomes the pillars and the vajra garland.
With four it becomes the lotus for the buddhas and the rest; with one, the outer vajra garland as well.
Between the goddesses and the buddhas, with three squares, it becomes the seats of the vases or the skull cups.

From that place here, up to the line, with twelve it becomes the ground of colors.
In the corners, with four squares, the moon, sun and lotus with scents and the rest.
With one and a half, three lines; and with six and six, the doors and the door recesses,
likewise the sides and the jambs; and with three, the great archways and the half-pillars.

Then with one and a half, two and six, the bands of jewel nature and the ground of the necklaces arise.
From the first at the base of the door, with ten multiplied by three, the portal with its pillars arises.
Casting half a line above, at the end of the half-necklaces it becomes the bakulī ornament as well.
With six squares, below the portal, are the bands of the yoginīs with eight lotuses.

Then with six multiplied by six, the glorious ground of colors, up to the five lines.

In the directions and corners, twelve lotuses with twice fourteen petals are made with twelve squares.
The doors of the core are doubled; by the power of the three qualities, here the outer doors as well,
the walls and the rest, and likewise the layouts of the three maṇḍalas, are all fixed by three eighths.

At the first and last parts of these, the sun and moon maṇḍalas, and the outer vajra garland as well, are made with half squares. Whatever the wheel may be, at the edge of the maṇḍala there is the wind maṇḍala, and below the pillars it also becomes maṇḍalas of the nāgas. The chariots of the deities are made with twelve, in the middle of the doors, in the sky and on the ground, on the eastern side and also on the west.

With the five precious substances, diamond and the rest, with gold, emerald, coral, pearl and the like, or with grains of five kinds, or with many kinds of gems, and likewise with dyed colors, on the colored ground in the directional parts, O lord of men, the colored powder is laid according to the divisions of the buddhas: with yellow, white, red and the rest for the earth and water maṇḍalas, and for fire and wind, in order.

In the east it becomes a glorious black ground, in the south it is like the sun, and in the west it is golden in color; in the north it is the other, white as the moon, according to the divisions of the faces of the Vajra-holder. White, red and black, set out in order, are the frieze and the grounds of the necklaces, in the colors of lotus, moon and sun; and there are three lines, like the stainless moon, red and black.

In the center is an eight-petaled lotus, green; the pillars and the vajra garland are like bees. In the northeast, the southwest, the southeast and the northwest, with bodies of moon and sun, and black and yellow, in order, are the conch, the gaṇḍī, the jewels and what is called the tree; likewise white vases in the eight intermediate directions. The moon comes to rest on a red lotus, and the sun upon a white lotus.

In the hubs of the red lotuses are the moon seats of the goddesses;
in the hubs of the white lotuses are the suns of the gods, in the directions.
Outside, by the power of the principal lotus, there is a vajra garland; the parapets are white in color,
the pillars yellow, the bākulī the color of snow, and the archways of many colors.

White in color, too, are the jewel-lotus friezes of the yoginīs in every direction.
In the directional parts there are red lotuses, and by the power of the Victor, white lotuses also in the corners,
