On the maṇḍalas of wind and the rest are Padma, Karkoṭa, and the others, the lotuses of the hands of time.

Dog-Faced, Sow-Faced, and Jackal-Faced surely are in the directions on the maṇḍala of the Mover;
Tiger-Faced dwells in the north; they dwell on the charnel-ground earth, with curved knife and skull cup in hand.
Crow-Faced, Vulture-Faced, Garuḍa-Faced, and Owl-Faced are at the corners.
Vajra-Eye and the Very Blue One dwell below the earth and in the sky, at the edge of the moving source of the bhūtas.

The red preta, the lord of birds, buffalo, peacock, elephant, swan, bull, and the five-faced lion become in order the lotus seats of Cāmuṇḍā and the others, in the directions and the intermediate directions. Likewise, of the asura and the rest, of the Lord of Wealth, Agni the Crested One, the Lord of the Waters, Vāyu, and Gaṇapati, the mounts are, in order, the lord of elephants, the ram, the one called makara, the deer, and the rat.

Bheruṇḍa, crane, the blue-eyed bird, and the anus-faced are at the corners, for Crow-Faced and the others;
rhinoceros, bear, lion, and yak come to be in the directions, for Dog-Faced and the others.
The eight-footed one of Vajra-Eye stands on the chariot of earth; in the sky, of the chariot of the Very Blue One,
the one who goes to the citadel, with wrath, five-colored, full-bodied, and three-eyed, is called Anila.

For the chariots of Marīcī, Cundā, the Lady of the Iron Chain, and the Frowning One are the boar, the horse, the elephant, and the lion, seven in number, in the east, at Yama, at the Waters, and in the north. In the east are the Lady of Scent and the Lady of Garlands; dwelling at Yama and the Waters are the Lady of Incense, the Lady of Lamps, the Graceful One, and the Laughing One; the Dancer and the Musician are on the ground of Kubera; in the sky, the Singer and the Desiring One.

The eight at the heart are on the coping; those dwelling in the sky and below are surely placed beneath the porticoes. The dhāraṇī goddesses are on the bands of cloth; because they are joined with the nāga families, the desiring goddesses are on the coping: there come to be the one who desires dividing and the one who desires driving away, O lord of men, and likewise the ones who desire increase and paralysis, through the pure ones, the goddess Tārā and the rest; the one who desires to slay the begetter of the three existences, and the one who desires begetting;

there come to be the one who desires music and the one who desires ornaments, O lord of men, the one who desires food, and likewise the one who desires scent and the one who desires garments; surely the ones who desire sexual union are the sixth.

She who desires to rub the body, she who spits out the saliva in the mouth, she who desires the dirt of the body,
She who desires dance, she who desires a seat, she who desires to swim in water and to lie on beds;

The activities of the eight beginning with Cāmuṇḍā, and likewise the desirous ones of those born of wrath upon the earth: She who desires to torment and to bind, she who speaks gently, she who desires to dry up and to expel, To touch and to summon, to bind, and, O lord of men, she who desires to drive in the kīla and to run; She who desires to shake all the limbs, very clearly, and she who desires to leak excrement and urine;

She who desires to deceive beings, she of many disputes, the fifth, who eats leftovers,
She who desires battle, she who binds snakes, and she who desires to anger children: these belong to the asura family.
These desirous ones, thirty-seven, are then placed outside again, on the parapet of the outer maṇḍala,
For whatever small activity of beings there is at each time of day and night, here in the activity of the yoginīs.

Thus the vajra-holder taught all the three existences, in their entirety, as maṇḍalas.
In the outer, in the body, and in the other as well, the arising of entities truly abides in emanation and withdrawal.
Therein the permanent sky-vajra abiding in the threefold existence does not become impermanent;
The host of elements becomes impermanent, O lord of men, just like a magical illusion.

Permanence and impermanence are seen; even so, for the sake of purifying the minds of those of foolish intellect,
Sādhanas are to be taught; here there is nothing at all of essence to be accomplished.
"What is to be accomplished and the accomplisher are delusion": this is the whole of the vajra-holder's sādhana.
Therefore, O king, one should make one's own mind, free of stains, the lord of the maṇḍala.

The glorious vajra-holder becomes the mind-vajra; O lord of men, the maṇḍala is the body-vajra;
The deities, the wheel born of the family of vowels and consonants (āli-kāli), are the speech-vajra, upon moon and sun.
The root and the stem are the syllable ā; the petals and likewise the anthers are the syllable u;
In the center, the glorious pericarp and the two, moon and sun, the seats for the feet, are the syllable ma.

The syllable ho, essence of first and last, granting the fruit of equal bliss, is the vajra of body, speech and mind. Beholding that all-pervading lord, the vajra-holder, melted through passion for wisdom, like the hare-bearer (the moon), The goddesses sang songs: "Blessed One, you are the benefactor of all beings! Protect us, guru of gods and men, possessor of the vajra! Grant their desires to those who seek desire!"

Hearing the songs, that vajra-holder, seeing all the three existences in their entirety as like a magical illusion,
Performed generation: having emanated his own emblem, radiating stainless rays of light,
Adorned with vajra ornaments, crowned with the Lord of Jinas, and embraced by wisdom,
Through wisdom and means, O king, he again emanates the entire maṇḍala.

With blue rays, a terrifying form, a mouth laughing loudly and fangs half bared, Roaring fierce sounds, with twelve eyes, the number of the suns, twenty-six arms, two more than the Jinas, four faces, the number of the Vedas, and two feet, With weapons arisen from wisdom and means, standing on chariots with eight feet, Vajravega, destroyer of the Jina's enemies, should be urged on for the sake of the wisdom wheel.

The lords of the wisdom wheel, radiating stainless rays, are struck at the navel with the hook, Their hands likewise bound tight by his own vajra-nāgas, and they are terrified with weapons; Having made all of them subject to accomplishment and having set the excellent wheel in motion, this one goes forth again. Causing to enter, binding, pleasing, and making of one taste are performed by Jambhaka and the others.

In the perfect yoga, the mind together with space comes to be upon fire in the lotus of the heart.
In the east, on the supreme lotus petal, is glorious Kṛṣṇadīptā; in the south, Raktadīptā;
In the north is glorious Śvetadīptā; and by the power of the family, in the west, Pītadīptā.
In Agni's quarter, Dhūmā; in the quarter of the son of Danu, Marīcī; in Īśāna's quarter, Khadyotā;

In Vāyu's quarter, glorious Pradīpā. The jewel, the tree, the Dharma gaṇḍī, and the conch
Are likewise in the quarters of Agni, Vāyu, the asuras and Hara, in the portions of the intermediate corners.
In the east, formations and earth, abiding on the lotus; in the south, feeling and water;
In the north, perception and fire; and in the west, form itself together with wind.

In Agni's quarter are wind and form; in the quarter of the lord of the asuras, fire and perception, the two;
In Īśāna's, water and feeling; in Vāyu's, the earth, joined with the aggregate of formations.
On the goddesses' and buddhas' platforms, in the eight places, are vases with the taste of nectar.
Again, on the outer ledge, in the east, nose and smell; in the south, eye and form;

In the north, tongue and taste; and in Varuṇa's quarter, likewise, body and the tangible.
Below the earth, ear and sound, by the power of the family, to the left of the southern gate;
In space, mind and the dharma-realm, to the left of the eastern gate.
First the six means, by the power of the Jina, become the presiding deities of the maṇḍala;

Afterwards the six beginning with wisdom, very clearly, on the lotus seats of their respective lords.
At the eastern gate is the very fierce sword-bearer Atibala, and his consort is Stambhakī;
On the right, Jambhaka and Mānakī; in Kubera's quarter, Mānaka and Jambhakī;
In Varuṇa's quarter, Stambhaka and Infinite Vigor, on lotuses in the middle of the gates.

Nīladaṇḍa with Marīcī, the one called Acala with Bhṛkuṭī, Anantavīrya with Śṛṅkhalā, Kāma with Cundā: these dwell on chariots in the quarter of the God (Indra) and of Kubera, and also at the center of the southern gate. Sumbharāja with Raudrākṣī are below; in space are Uṣṇīṣa and Atinīlā. They are set above the eastern gate and elsewhere as well. The chariots of the second circle, too,

in the maṇḍala born of speech, in the quarter of the Wealthy One, become the seats of those born of the bhūtas. In the east are Cāmuṇḍā and Īsā; in the quarter of the Crested One, Vaiṣṇavī and the Veda-Faced (Brahmā); in Yama's quarter, Vārāhī and Raudra; in the quarter of the lord of the asuras, Ṣaṇmukhī and the lord of obstructors (Gaṇapati); in the west, Aindrī and Nairṛti; in Vāyu's quarter, the goddess with the face of Time and Viṣṇu himself.

In the quarter of the yakṣa, Raudrī and Yama; in Paśupati's quarter, Lakṣmī and Ṣaṇmukha. Outside, to the right of Īsā's gate, are the lord of the asuras and his mudrā, who is the rākṣasī. In Agni's quarter, Vāyu and Pracaṇḍā; Agni and the water goddess Vāruṇī are to the right of the southern gate. In the quarter of the lord of the asuras are the glorious Ṣaṇmukha and Lakṣmī; in the west, Īsā and the wind goddess Vāyavī.

In Vāyu's quarter are Brahmā and the Lightning Goddess; in Kubera's, Sāgara and Vārāhī.
In Īśāna's quarter are Gaṇapati and Kaumārī, at the left side of the gates of Kubera and Yama.
Raudra, Kāla, Viṣṇu and the one called Kubera are to the left of the southern and western gates.
Their mudrās are the renowned Pārvatī, Yāmī, Lakṣmī and Kauberī.

Outside, at the portals of the God, of Yama, of Kubera and of the west, are all the nāgas:
Padma and Karkoṭa dwell in the wind maṇḍala; Vāsuki and Śaṅkhapāla
dwell in fire; above the water, Kulika and Ananta are renowned as nāgas;
and likewise, dwelling in the earth maṇḍala according to their families, are Takṣaka and Mahāpadma.

Their wisdom partners are the fierce goddesses who dwell in the charnel grounds, the Dog-Faced and the rest;
and their method partners, Padma and the others, through the power of the other family, serve the aim of supreme bliss.
In the eastern circle, in the charnel ground of impalement, dwelling in the wind maṇḍala, is the Dog-Faced;
in Yama's quarter, where corpses are burned, the Sow-Faced; in the north, where corpses rot,

the Tiger-Faced; in the west, amid the stench of pus, likewise the Jackal-Faced; in the quarters of Agni and the lord of the asuras, where remains are left and in terrible battle, the Crow-Faced and the Vulture-Faced; in Vāyu's quarter, where people are devoured by snakes, the one with the face of the lord of birds (Garuḍa); and in Īśāna's quarter, where children have died, dwelling on the wheel, the Owl-Faced. They dwell in the earth maṇḍala, and one should meditate on the moon and sun as well.

The left-extended stance belongs to the consort, and the even stance to the host of goddesses on the petals. The ten and the hosts of the Wealthy One take the left-extended and the viśākha stances, and the asura women the circular stance. The partners in union of those standing in the left-extended stance are certainly in the right-extended stance; for viśākha it is likewise called viśākha, and for the circular, certainly the circular.

In the maṇḍala the remaining deities, shining clearly, are seated in the vajra posture. The goddesses who dwell in the directions of their own families sit in the lotus posture, each on her own lotus and moon. The deities, together with the nāga families, who dwell on the mounts of their own directions, sit in the vajra posture. The goddesses of the deities on the petals stand in the playful stance, and so too those born of the bhūtas.

The right leg is bent and set firmly on the ground, and the left foot extended
two and a half cubits: that, O lord of men, is the left-extended stance.
With the left applied in this way, it becomes the right-extended; with both feet even, it is the even stance.
According to their qualities, from the two knees spread wide come the viśākha and the circular stances.

With the knee slightly bent by half, it is the playful stance; the rest are well known.
The yogin should further learn them from Bharata, for the sake of the vajra dance.
On the crowns, the vajra family bears the vajra in exchange; at times the five buddhas are found there.
On the crown of form is Akṣobhya, and clearly, for the lotus-holder, it is Vairocana.

For the lord of jewels, it is the lotus-holder; on the crown of Amoghasiddhi, the supreme Ratnapāṇi. The crown of the sixth is called the matted locks; and for the rest of the circle, too, it differs according to their qualities. From earth arises the wheel, from water the lotus, from fire the jewel, from wind the sword, from space the vajra, and from the immutable the curved knife.

They become the garment and the vessel of nectar, and certainly the mirror, the rosary and the lute,
and sixth, the source of phenomena: these come from the peaceful aspects of earth, water, fire, wind and space.
The shield, spear, arrow, axe and ḍamaru are five emblems; and likewise
the club, noose, hook, and likewise the hammer and trident.

The bow and the utpala, and again the severed head, the khaṭvāṅga and the bell,
likewise the chain and so on, the water creature, the skull cup, the tiger skin and the elephant hide:
here the remaining secondary emblems are born from earth, water, fire, wind and space.
Their nature is to be understood according to the qualities of the gods and goddesses.

The thirty vowels beginning with ḷ, born from earth, water, fire, wind and space,
are set out in order by the divisions of short and long, and dwell on lotus, moon
and sun seats; all of them are the own mantras of the emblems, the wheel and the rest.
That which alone has become the sixth emblem is there, according to its qualities, the indestructible.

Nāda becomes the glorious Vajrasattva; O lord of men, the mind vajra is the letter a.
The short i becomes the sword-bearer; and again the letters ṛ are Ratnapāṇi.
The short u becomes Amitābha; and again here the letter ḷ is Vairocana.
Through the distinctions of intention, the whole collection of long vowels follows the order of the six Jinas.

The glorious Mother becomes the one called the Indestructible; likewise, born from the syllable ā, is the element of space. In wind, fire, water and earth, ī ṝ ū lī all abide, each in order. The supreme lord of the Victorious Ones and Viśvamātā, for the sake of bliss, are each other's body and mind; Akṣobhya and the element of emptiness, and the one whose lotus hand holds the sword and Locanā, are body and mind.

The Lord of Jewels and Māmakī, and, clearly, the lotus-holder and Pāṇḍarā (the White-Clad Lady), are body and mind;
likewise the Cakravartin and Tārā, by way of nature and quality, through the short and long vowels.
The syllable aṃ becomes Samantabhadra; by way of quality, the syllable ā is Vajrapāṇi.
The short syllable e is Ākāśagarbha; the syllable ār likewise truly becomes Kṣitigarbha.

The syllable o is Lokanātha; āl likewise here becomes Viṣkambhin, in the body. The syllable ā becomes Dharmadhātu; and certainly, likewise, born from the syllable āḥ is Śabdavajrā, the Vajra Lady of Sound. The syllable ai is Sparśavajrā, the Vajra Lady of Touch; by the division of the body, born from the syllable ār is Rasavajrā, the Vajra Lady of Taste. The syllable au becomes Rūpavajrā, the Vajra Lady of Form; and certainly, likewise, born from the syllable āl is Gandhavajrā, the Vajra Lady of Scent.

The glorious Excellent One and Dharmadhātu, and Vajrapāṇi and Śabdavajrā, are pairs;
Ākāśagarbha and Gandhavajrā, and Lokanātha and Rasavajrā, are pairs;
Kṣitigarbha and Rūpavajrā, and Sparśavajrā and Viṣkambhin, become pairs.
Thus the six families should be rightly understood as pure, by way of nature and quality.

The syllable haṃ becomes Uṣṇīṣa Cakravartin; by way of the body, here the syllable haḥ is Atinīlā.
The short syllable hā becomes Sumbha; and certainly, likewise, born from the long one is Raudrākṣī.
yaṃ lāḥ, in order and quite clearly, are Atibala and Stambhakī; these two are also a pair.
raṃ vāṃḥ are Jambhaka and Mānakī; and vaṃ and rāḥ become Mānaka and Jambhakī.

laṃ yāḥ become Stambhaka and the Supremely Vigorous Lady; ya va ra la are Nīladaṇḍa, Acala,
Kāma and Infinite Vigor; by way of the body, the long ones belong to the goddesses:
yā vā rā lā; likewise they ride on chariots of elephant, horse, lion and boar.
Nīladaṇḍa and Marīcī, the one called Acala and Bhṛkuṭī, Infinite Vigor and Śṛṅkhalā,

and Kāma and Cundā become pairs, O lord of men, in the maṇḍala, for the sake of supreme bliss.
From the a syllables, oṃ, the visarga, haṃ, haḥ, and ha hā arise the eight śaktis.
Likewise, for the vases, the syllable hā is differentiated by the vowels of wind, fire, water and earth.
oṃ hūṃ ho āḥduṅ: the conch, the stainless jewel of qualities, the tree, and the gaṇḍī of the Dharma.

The syllable hā is Cāmuṇḍā; haṃ and haḥ here are Īśvarī and Vārāhī.
hā and kṣa kṣā kṣaṃ kṣaḥ are Aindrī, Vaiṣṇavī who rides the lord of birds, Brāhmī, Lakṣmī and Kaumārī.
hī hṝ hū hḹ are on the petals of the supreme lotus at the back; the short vowels are in front.
kṣī kṣḷ kṣū kṣḹ are just the same, quite clearly; and certainly the short vowels are likewise.

On the lotuses of the east, the right and the left, of Varuṇa, Agni, the asuras, Īśāna and Vāyu,
on six petals, ya and the rest, differentiated by the six vowels, short and long, are arranged in order.
hi, kṣi and the rest, all those ending in la, eight times eight, are on the petals of the goddesses;
by way of family, facing them, they become the method partners of Cāmuṇḍā and the others, by the divisions of mantra.

taṃ naḥ are Śakra and the Ocean-Faced One; baṃ maḥ are Ocean and glorious Gaṇapati; ṭaṃ ṇaḥ are Agni and Kumāra; caṃ and ñaḥ are the lord of the rākṣasas and Vāyu; kaṃ daḥ are Viṣṇu and Time; Hara and Kubera are saṃ and, here, dkaḥ. The classes beginning with ca, together with the vowels, on the petals of the gods' lotuses become those of the goddesses.

The days of the month of Caitra and the others, by way of the fixed measure of time, number three hundred and sixty. Mounted on these are the syllable ha in wind, fire, water and earth; of the nāgas of the east and the other directions, standing on the seven classes stacked one on another, kṣa joined with ya ra va la; in the charnel grounds, of the asura women, through the body and mind of wisdom and method, by way of family, whichever here is in the center comes to face them.

The glorious vajra-holder and Samantabhadra; by way of family, Vajradhara becomes Vajrapāṇi.
Amoghasiddhi and Ākāśagarbha; the one with the stainless jewel in his hand and Kṣitigarbha, truly.
The one with the wheel in his hand becomes Viṣkambhin; by way of family, Amitābha and Lokanātha.
The glorious Mother and Dharmadhātu; Śabdavajrā and Vajradhātvīśvarī.

Glorious Tārā is Sparśavajrā; by the division of family, Pāṇḍarā is Rasavajrā.
Māmakī becomes known as Rūpavajrā; by way of family, Locanā is Gandhavajrā.
Vajravega, lord of the wrathful ones, becomes the lord of the Jinas; that is Samantabhadra himself.
Uṣṇīṣa here is Akṣobhya himself; and again, Atibala is well known as Amoghasiddhi.

Jambhaka becomes Ratnapāṇi; by way of family, Mānaka and Amitābha.
Stambhaka becomes Vairocana; mighty Vajrapāṇi and Sumbha.
Ākāśagarbha and Nīladaṇḍa; by way of nature and quality, Kṣitigarbha and Kāma.

Viṣkambhin becomes the Supremely Diligent One; by the power of family, Lokanātha is Acala. 88 The Mother becomes the consort of the lord of the wrathful ones; by the power of family, the dharmadhātu is suchness. She who is known as emptiness is Atinīlā; wind, fire, water, earth and the rest, as infinite diligence, are, in their order, clearly and definitely renowned as Jambhī, Mānī and Stambhī. Raudrākṣī becomes Śabdavajrā; by the power of family, Śṛṅkhalā becomes Sparśavajrā. 89 Marīcī becomes Gandhavajrā, and Bhṛkuṭī and the renowned Cundā, by the division of family, are to be known, in their order, as Rasavajrā and Rūpavajrā. Here Stambhaka is the Ender of Time, Yamāntaka, and again he is renowned as Atibala and Vighnāntaka. Jambhaka becomes Prajñāntaka, and likewise Mānaka becomes Padmāntaka. 90 Vāyu, Agni and Varuṇa, with Cāmuṇḍā, Vārāhī, Maheśvarī and likewise Aindrī the fourth, called smell, form, taste and touch, and the Moving One, Hara, the asura, and the one dwelling in fire as well: Brahmā becomes Vairocana; by the power of family, the Ocean is Amitābha. Agni becomes glorious Ratnapāṇi; Vāyu and Amoghasiddhi are likewise. 91 Akṣobhya becomes the Enemy of the Asuras (Viṣṇu), and the Lord of the Jinas is renowned as Śaṅkara (Śiva). Viṣkambhin becomes Śakra (Indra), and Lokanātha likewise is Gaṇapati. Kṣitigarbha becomes Ṣaṇmukha, and glorious Khagarbha is renowned as the Lord of the Dānavas. Glorious Vajrapāṇi becomes Time, and the sixth, Samantabhadra, is the Lord of Wealth (Kubera). 92 The six knowledge consorts and the six mudrās, by the power of nature and quality, are each the mudrā of each. The mudrās, knowledge consorts and the rest, the sun, the supreme lords of the gods, are the white, the black and the complete. On their lotus petals are the lunar days designated Vasu and Hand, and those which are the months beginning with Māgha: the six months become the first six, and by the power of family, the latter ones belong to the goddesses. 93 The eight nāgas are the vases; on the lotus petals of the All-Pervading Lord, the śaktis are the fierce goddesses. Glorious Dhūmā becomes Crow-Faced; by the power of family, Marīci is Vulture-Faced. Khadyotā is Owl-Faced; glorious Pradīpā is renowned as having the face of the lord of sky-goers. Kṛṣṇadīptā is Dog-Faced; Raktadīptā, with a most distorted face, is Pig-Faced. 94 Śvetadīptā becomes Tiger-Faced; by the power of family, Pītadīptā is Jackal-Faced. Likewise Lāsyā and all the rest are clearly tenfold; Viśvamātā is the remainder. The goddesses, the buddhas and the knowledge consorts, together with the trees and together with the vases, are the pure body of the Victor. The lords of the wrathful ones, the bodhisattvas, and indeed Rasavajrā, are the Dharma body itself. 95 The yoginīs are the enjoyment body, as is the god dwelling on the supreme chariot, renowned as the Sun. The eight nāgas and the fierce goddesses with their retinues are the emanation body of the Buddha. Likewise, again, the body of the Victor becomes twofold in division, outer and inner: just as it is with one born from the womb, so exactly does it come to be in the maṇḍala. 96 The Teacher, the goddesses and the rest, and the vases are the innate body of the Victor, at the center of the heart of the maṇḍala. The buddhas and the rest are the Dharma body; Rasavajrā and the rest are the body of complete enjoyment. The wrathful ones become the emanation body; by the power of quality they properly dwell in the heart of the maṇḍala. Outside, Cāmuṇḍā and the other goddesses, the eight together with their retinues, are the pure body. 97 The gods and the rest are the Dharma body; all the families of nāgas, here, are the body of complete enjoyment. The caṇḍālīs become the emanation body, O lord of men, for the sake of all beings. The body vajra and the innate body form a pair, for the sake of completing form. The speech vajra and the Dharma body also form a pair, for the purpose of teaching the nature of reality. 98 The mind and the body of complete enjoyment also form a pair, the agent of the benefit of all beings. Gnosis and the emanation body form a pair, the very bestowal of liberation upon living creatures. The body of wisdom and method, intention, the equal and unequal families, sun and moon, ka and the rest, a and the rest, and what dwells in the threefold existence: by the divisions of gnosis and consciousness, the yogi should understand these. 99 With those ending in vajra svāhā, at the head, throat, heart, navel, secret place and crown, the supreme limbs are blessed by these; the Lord of the Victors is to be bathed by the goddesses. Having gone three times for refuge to emptiness, the dharmadhātu, the vajra of the three times, and gnosis, with offering and devotion, the practitioner should recite tata svabhāva ātmako'haṃ. 100 Through the vases and the eight beginning with Dhūmā, at the heart it becomes the wheel of Dharma. Through the knowledge consorts and the Buddha himself, at the head the sixteen-spoked one is renowned as the innate. Through the bodhisattvas and the rest, doubled, O lord of men, at the throat it becomes the wheel of complete enjoyment. Through Bhīmā and the rest, the eight multiplied by the nāgas, at the navel it becomes the wheel of emanation. 101 At the joints of the hands and feet are the action wheels of the gods, ninety multiplied by time; through the nāgas and caṇḍālīs, O lord of men, at the junctures of the finger joints, it comes to be called activity. Through the purity of time it becomes the glorious vajra-holder, O lord of men, by the divisions of years, months and so on.

The aspect of the mind is not the sun; through each day and night, Viśvamātā is purified.

Through the purification of the winds, Dhūmā and the others, who abide on the wheel of channels in the lotus at one's own heart, are purified. Through the afflictions, Raudra with his consort, and Māra of the hosts of māras, beneath the feet of the Pervading Lord, are purified. Likewise those called the conch, the gaṇḍī, the jewel and the tree are purified by the body vajra and the rest. The eight vases are purified by the limbs of nectar, and the Victorious and Fully Victorious vases by bodhicitta and the rest.

Formations are Amoghasiddhi, feeling is the one whose hand holds the stainless jewel, and perception is Amitābha. Form is the cakravartin; the hare-marked one (the moon), strength, blood, feces and urine are purified. The six goddesses are purified by the elements, and the bodhisattvas with their mudrās by the objects and the sense faculties. The five wrathful ones are purified by the strengths, and again the other five by the other faculties.

Cāmuṇḍā and the others, through the eight watches, who abide on the lotus petals, are purified through the sun's junctures and hours. The asuras and the others who abide on the lotus petals are purified through the solar months, through the hours and the count of breaths. In the secret place, O lord of men, through the doubled hours, the nāgas and the caṇḍālīs too are purified. Likewise the desire goddesses and the rest are purified by the activities of the body, by virtue of their nature and qualities.

Through the hairs of the head, all the siddhas; through the hairs of the body, the hosts of bhūtas dwelling on the grounds of the charnel grounds. Through the principles, the weapons of the Chief; through the elements, by virtue of nature and qualities, the outer mudrās. Through the vajra, the inner mudrā, rightly abiding upon the moon at the heart of Vajradhara. The glorious vajra-holder and Viśvamātā, through the distinction between the states of threefold existence and immutable gnosis,

From the glorious pure one arises the dharmakāya; from the Dharma, certainly, the excellent sambhogakāya;
From the sambhoga arises the nirmāṇakāya, lord of the Jinas, who accomplishes the aims of all sentient beings.
The fourth state, deep sleep, and again, through the distinction of the other and the body, waking:
Thus, through the division of the body, the mind abides in four modes in the bodies of living beings.

In the emanation body, the mind becomes the enjoyer, through the faculties of body and speech. In the complete enjoyment body, it becomes the thought of what is unseen; by virtue of the qualities, in the dharmakāya, it is sleep. In the pure body, it becomes bliss: here, at the end of the three releases of the drop in the times of day and night. Therefore the yogin should meditate on it in the times of day and night, for the sake of the immutable.

When prāṇa flows on the left, likewise whatever is the right leg bent, That is the left-extended stance; the even stance, and the other, the right-extended, arise from the flowing of fire and sun. The sāga, the circular one, the stance of supreme play, and the lotus and vajra seats: The five kinds of life-wind flow in space and the rest, in equal and unequal movements.

Caṇḍālī, struck by the nine wheels of the navel, with the hand-goers, Carcikā and the rest, together with the presiding deities: In the syllable ho, the essence of gnosis, the fire of gnosis, like stainless lightning, blazes greatly. Burning Vairocana and the rest at the navel, O lord of men, and Locanā, the eyes and the rest, Having burned them all, what drips in the form of a drop from the excellent moon at the head, that is the vajra-holder.

Again emanating all that abides in the sources of phenomena of wisdom, certainly by means of the drop,
The self-arisen wheel of gnosis, adorned with various ornaments, like images abiding in mirrors:
The three kinds of existence, desire, form and formless, are also purified in sequence,
And then, with the flames of the fire of gnosis, all three existences should be summoned at once.

By moon and sun, by A and the rest and KA and the rest, is the recitation of the body vajra and the rest, abiding in the threefold movement; Through the six, withdrawal and the rest, in the lotus of fine gold, is the yoga of body, speech and mind; Through joy and the rest, by becoming of equal taste in vajra and lotus, the meditation is the three vajras; In the lotus of wisdom, upon the drops of mind, by virtue of innate bliss, the meditation becomes indestructible.

Through the five nectars and the rest, the vajras of the Lord of the Waters, mantra recitation and the rest, there is approach; Through withdrawal and the rest, and the nectar born from vajra and lotus, there is near accomplishment; Through joy and the rest, the meditation in which the three vajras and the lotus have become of equal taste is accomplishment; Joined with wisdom, when it comes from the subtle yogas without emission, it certainly becomes great accomplishment.

First, emptiness and awakening; then again the moon and the drops are rightly taken up;
Then the forms are generated, and with the supreme six families comes the setting out of syllables.
This is the general approach; the accomplishments by the vajras of the Lord of the Waters are also the middling.
Here the method becomes fourfold, and just so in the limbs of accomplishment of the lesser and the steadfast.

Withdrawal becomes the lord of the Jinas and is tenfold; meditative concentration is Akṣobhya.
Breath control is the sword-bearer, again tenfold; retention is Ratnapāṇi.
In Caṇḍālī, recollection becomes the Lotus-holder; glorious samādhi is the cakravartin.
Each has five divisions, and again they are divided by A and the rest and KA and the rest.

Withdrawal is the non-engagement in the body of the ten, the objects and the subjects.
The meditative concentrations are wisdom, conception, analysis, joy and unmoving bliss, and one-pointed mind.
Breath control is driving the two paths and the prāṇa entering into the central channel.
The prāṇa fully entering the drop, the movement of both destroyed, one-pointed mind: that is retention.

Whoever has come to the visions of caṇḍālī will surely gain recollection in the body and in space as well; through the nature of wisdom and method, by the power of immutable bliss, there is samādhi on the gnosis-form. By the divisions of the slow one and the rest, these various principal means of accomplishment become threefold: the three mudrās and the three states are the actions of empowerment that move through the three, and the supreme conceptions.

Through withdrawal the yogin, free of objects, is blessed by all the mantras. He gains the five superknowledges, O lord of men; this is purified through the yoga of meditative concentration. Purified through breath control, free of the hare-marked one (the moon) and of the sun, he is worshipped by the bodhisattvas. Māras, afflictions and the rest decline, and he enters into the ten powers, through the power of retention.

When recollection is well purified, the stainless maṇḍalas of light arise from the gnosis-form. Then, purified by samādhi, within a few days the gnosis-body is accomplished. If through withdrawal and the rest the aims of the mantra practitioners are not accomplished, then, through habituation to nāda, by forceful means, abiding in the lotus, one should accomplish it, holding back the drop at the vajra-jewel.

For approach, the first yoga: the ten forms in the sky, through the wrathful gaze of the cakravartin. For near accomplishment, the six limbs, together with the gaze that destroys obstructors, which has reached the path of nectar. For accomplishment, the three immutables: the moon spread into wisdom, the drop abiding in the vajra-jewel. For the great accomplishment, here, the yoga of innate gnosis, peaceful and single, with bliss unimpaired.

Breath control, granting in every way the fruit of equal bliss, is desired up to the head; above that it is not desired, for it brings the terror of death and is the cause of the certain destruction of the aggregates. If one goes forth having pierced the door of the uṣṇīṣa, one should certainly join oneself to the state of supreme bliss. Though without aggregates, the yogin enters equal bliss; yet in the world he is not renowned.

Prāṇa fully entered into the center is the embrace of Viśvamātā, free of objects; likewise one's own vajra entering the lotus, and its emanations too, fully entering the center of moon and sun. Bliss with the seed not falling, abiding in the joy of supreme joy: this yoga of yogins, for the sake of the accomplishment of mudrā, is more supremely secret even than the glorious secret of the All-Pervading Lord.

At the uṣṇīṣa there arises a five-pronged vajra, and its outer prongs, doubled,
become the vajra wheel of Dharma; another, doubled, and its double, another again,
and its double once more another: in the wheels of the innate, of complete enjoyment, and of emanation.
In their heart, the one-pronged, granting the fruit of equal bliss, abides within the secret lotus.

That the yogin cuts off the life-breath day and night is what is called taking life;
whatever sound is lacking from the mouth yet has arisen in the heart, that is speaking falsehood.
To seize the ground of the gnosis of omniscience is what is called the yogin's stealing.
To grow blissful from the drop not falling, from not being free of passion, is resorting to another's woman.

Drinking the hare-marked one melted by the fire of breath control is drinking liquor. From the joint of the big toe up to the uṣṇīṣa one's own mind moves, until the full moon, by the power of the lunar days; from the uṣṇīṣa back down into the toes this same one moves again, until the end of the dark fortnight. The conduct of yogins, at each time of day and night, fully grants the siddhis that are desired.

Longing, desire, fever in the body, the supreme lotus of the mouth drying, food not going down, trembling, madness, becoming deeply deluded, the mind confused, and fainting away: these ten, as clear as the smoke and the other signs of the vajra-holder, appear in the bodies of living beings in the world. Clear and certain are these, the states of Manmatha; who then is the one who desires the Jina?

The white stream that flows from the drops day and night is Māmakī, not liquor.
Go, ku and the rest are the eyes and the others, emanated each day, not some other meat taken now and then.
By the deities abiding on the ground of their own family, the five nectars are relied upon within the purified body.
The union that brings equal taste, with the mind fully entered into emptiness, is not in the womb.

Giving away wealth is generosity; no emission even in mind or in consorting with women is moral discipline; patience is not engaging with sound and the rest; destroying the twofold movement of the wind is diligence itself. Meditative concentration and wisdom are the mind abiding in innate bliss and the speech of all beings everywhere. From that are gained the welfare of beings, miraculous power, the ending of existence, and the unborn: these are the other four.

The single body endowed with its power (śakti), with the nine places, the nature of twenty-five, and the rest:
if these are meditated upon, the accomplishments of mudrā and so forth surely come about within some days.
Paralysis, pacifying, subjugating and destroying existence are done through the different faces.
Abiding in the maṇḍalas of the elements, this family of asuras and hand-goers should be accomplished by meditation.

Through white, pacifying and increasing are done in one's own mind; through red, summoning and subjugation;
through yellow, paralysis and stupefaction; and through what is like a black cloud, killing and expulsion.

Likewise one applies meditation and recitation in one's own mind; and body, speech, mind and vajra, abiding in the maṇḍalas of the elements, all three realms of existence, should be accomplished through the divisions of the rites.

With the right and the other hands that arise in excess of the directions, colored like a bee and like gold, he has flayed the whole elephant hide from the Māra of the aggregates and holds it by a pair of its feet. In the palm of a hand is a supreme skull cup of the blood of the lord of the asuras, and a hand holds the face of the Māra Lord of Death. The Māra of afflictions is struck down beneath his feet. The one with twenty-six arms should be meditated upon for the sake of others.

There the consort has one face, and in the lotus hands of her pair of arms are a curved knife and a glorious skull cup. This is because the moon, relative to the sun, travels in its own course one transit more than twelve. Therefore, through the division of the body, he becomes the Lord of the Jinas, and Viśvamātā is suchness. The bodies and intentions of wisdom and method bestow the fruit of equal bliss through the movement of the moon and the sun.

With faces from one up to infinitely many, and many arms and legs, of many colors, at the end of darkness, the very nature of wisdom and method surely bestows equal bliss through the cessation of the channels, the moon and the sun. Piercing through earth and the other elements, like a stainless jewel, it is emptiness alone, without beginning, without end and without middle: the objectless object accomplished is Kālacakra.

The lord of the wrathful ones, Vajravega, has twenty-six arms, four faces and two legs,
reddish-yellow eyes and reddish-yellow hair, a crown bearing the Lord of the Jinas, and sharp fangs bared;
serpent ornaments, a lower garment of supreme tiger skin, and hands bearing weapons like those of the principal deity.
On his head a garland is firmly bound of skulls of five colors, of all the Jina families.

On a sun upon a variegated lotus radiating stainless light, upon a maṇḍala of many colors, he presses down the lord of the bhūtas with his two feet in surpassing strength and stands firm in the posture with the right leg extended. He terrifies the bhūtas, asuras, nāgas and gods, and has become one with the wisdom being. If one meditates on him, in just one month one can bring under control the host of bhūtas dwelling in charnel grounds.

The nāgas are on an eight-petaled lotus: having laid Jaya and Vijaya down upon the sun,
with his feet he presses all the tips of the tails of the pair of nāga lords and of those dwelling on the lotus petals,
while from his belly he emanates the families of clouds in every direction.
By meditating thus on the lord of the wrathful ones, in a few days one can bring under control the hosts of clouds.

Such are the separate sādhanas of the lords of men, the gods and the goddesses.
Here in this maṇḍala, according to one's own Jina family, all the divisions of rites come about.
For paralysis, pacification, subjugation, seizing the wealth of others, killing and expulsion as well,
the thirty-six yoginīs come about, and again recitation and fire offering through their own seed syllables.

For pacification the meditation is peaceful, and the deity, white like the hare-bearer (the moon), has a peaceful form. For fierce rites the meditation is fierce, and the deities, like black clouds, have fierce forms. For subjugation the meditation is with passion, and the deity, with a body like the day-maker (the sun), has a passionate form. For paralysis the meditation is with stupefaction, and the deities, like supreme gold, have paralyzing forms.

Pacification and increase are accomplished by the Jina families with their sons, and by the kings and the goddesses. Sowing discord and expulsion are accomplished by those born of the wrathful ones, according to the qualities of nature. Subjugation and the like by those born of the elements; and the striking of the radiant asura clan with stakes by the asura women. By the consorts all rites; and for the vajra family of both, killing and also reviving.

First, glorious Kālacakra and the mother of the three realms of existence should be accomplished with effort.
Then, in places on the ground, whatever rites, such as pacification, should also be accomplished.
It is known in the world at all times that there is no son lacking a father and a mother.
Therefore the two who bestow the fruit of equal bliss should be accomplished; otherwise the rites are not accomplished.

At the heart of the principal deity, in the center of a lotus, upon moon, sun and fire, one should set a vajra,
born from the wisdom syllable hūṃ, like the clouds at the time of destruction, five-pronged and radiating light.
In its center, with the rays of the hook jaḥ, having filled all three realms of existence,
and having summoned the wheel of wisdom dwelling in the threefold existence, one should bring it into the vajra path.

All of it, like melted moon, should be emitted from the tip of one's own vajra into the lotus of the consort.
Entering the sun, it becomes of equal taste, fully endowed with ā and the rest and kā and the rest.
In its center it becomes the wisdom seed of the rites, such as pacification, according to the family.
Through that, the complete goddess or god becomes the bestower of the fruit upon the yogin.

jaḥ hūṃ vaṃ hoḥrim are, in order, called the hook, the vajra, the vajra noose and the bell.
oṃ āḥ hūṃ hoḥde are likewise declared to be the moon, the sun, the vajra and the changeless.
ī ṝ ū ḹ likewise, and plainly ya ra va la, are wind, fire, water and earth.
haḥ hūṃ haṃ phreṃ are likewise declared to be the sun, the vajra, the moon and the curved knife.

In the center of moon and sun, together with the vowels and consonants, is a lotus whose nature is the seed of water. From that arises the complete goddess, moon-colored, one-faced, whose pair of arms have lotus hands, riding a white elephant, holding a white lotus and showing the gesture of fearlessness, in white garments, adorned with white ornaments, her face broadly smiling. Having meditated on her, one should dispatch her to the house of the one to be brought under control.

Then, having seized the one to be brought under control, one should once again fully bring that person into the maṇḍala of the all-pervading lord.

Having fully received the command of the Lord, Locanā and the others, greatly delighted, with vases of nectar
wash that person who is to be brought under power, and the supreme Śaktis of ten kinds worship him;
those of form and the rest nourish him, and the ten, radiant Lāsyā and the others, satisfy him.
Those known as bhūtas make him fearless, and the supreme wrathful ones of ten kinds protect him;
the nāga maidens kiss him, and the twelve immortal maidens embrace him;
the fierce goddesses guard him, for the sake of pacifying and increasing upon the entire earth.
In this way the yogin should meditate on everything that brings supreme bliss to the one to be brought under power.

In the center of moon and sun is hrīḥ, and from it a bow of sugarcane and arrows of unopened utpala flowers. From that arises a perfect figure, radiant like the sun, both hands drawing bow and arrow back to the ear, standing with left leg extended upon a lotus and the hare-bearer (the moon), riding a horse; send him to the house of the one to be brought under power. He strikes with his arrows the heart, navel, secret place, head and mouth of that person, binds him at the neck with the bow, and drives him on, leading him into the maṇḍala. The fierce goddesses strip him of his clothes, and the nāga maidens drag him along; the goddesses revile him, and the wrathful ones strike him with pestles and staffs; the bhūtas terrify him, and Lāsyā and the others dig in their sharp nails. The vajra goddesses ruin his mind, and earth, water, fire and wind bind him; the Śaktis strip him, crush all his pride, and make him fall at the feet of the Lord. Having thus brought him under power, have the all-pervading lords satisfy him once more, and there accomplish the rite. Likewise, with noose and hook, the act of summoning from the three realms takes many forms.

In the center of sun and moon, meditate on a sword born from a long hūṃ, like a black cloud. From it arises one the color of a bee, whose hands hold lotus and sword, the threatening forefinger and the noose; standing with left leg extended upon a camel, his face fiercely wrathful, send him to the house of the one to be acted upon. He binds that person with the noose and, wrathful of face, leads him to the door of the maṇḍala. Born from the syllable yaḥ is a swift camel that travels like the wind; on it, at the Lord's word, the person is set, riding, and by its very nature is to be driven away as far as the maṇḍalas of Agni the Crested One and of the moving wind. If in this way even the lords of the gods are surely expelled, what need is there to speak of humans? For dividing as well, those brought low by the camel and made full of strife are to be dragged to right and left.

In the center of sun and moon, meditate on laḥ transformed into an excellent wheel, yellow in color.
From it arises a one-faced figure like the finest gold, holding in his hands an iron chain and a wheel,
abiding on an asura seat upon a tortoise and moving very slowly; send him to the house of the one to be acted upon.
Having terrified that person with the wheel, he hurls him to the ground and binds his feet with the iron chain,
leads him all the way into the maṇḍala, and at the command of the Lord of the Jinas throws him down upon the earth.
For paralysis, upon the body of that person one should set Meru, whose nature is the finest gold;
for nailing, one should drive kīlas whose nature is vajra into his six joints.
For stupefying, too, one should meditate that snakes devour him all over and that he falls to the ground.

In the center of sun and moon, meditate on a curved knife, stainless like lightning, whose nature is phreṃ. From it arises a most fierce goddess, like the clouds at the time of destruction, holding curved knife and skull cup, standing with left leg extended upon the horse's enemy, naked; send her to the house of the one to be acted upon. She swiftly seizes that person by the hair and leads him, stripped of clothes, all the way into the maṇḍala; and in the glorious charnel grounds, at the command of the Lord of the Jinas, vultures, crows and jackals drink the blood from all his limbs, and eat his flesh and likewise all his constituents. All of this one should meditate upon the supreme ground of the earth, for killing. If by this concentration even Śakra goes to the city of Yama, what need is there to speak of the womb-born?

For pacifying and increasing, according to the family, the concentration arising from the seed of water is white.
For subjugating and summoning it is red, burning the body, its nature the seed of fire.
For dividing and expelling it is like the clouds at the time of destruction, its nature the seed of wind.
For paralysis, nailing with kīlas and the like, it is like molten gold, its nature the seed of earth.
From the seed of space it becomes blue or green, for killing and for reviving.

The earth totality is for going everywhere across the ocean; the wind totality is for dispersing the rain;
the fire totality is for melting the earth maṇḍala; and for quenching fire,
there is the water totality; the space totality is for going through the sky and for invisibility.

To vanquish the lord of elephants, meditation on the five-faced one, the lion, arises from the seed of fire itself. To vanquish the lord of nāgas, there is a white garuḍa whose nature is the seed of water. Against the rhinoceros and the lion, there is the eight-legged one, like a cloud at the time of the world's destruction, whose nature is the seed of wind. Against the enemy of horses, there is the one known as the rhinoceros, by the power of the earth family; and to vanquish the asuras, a wrathful one arises.

The yogi should first accomplish the glorious mantra of the All-Pervading Lord and the form of the buddha. Afterwards the rites, governed by the divisions of the days and of immeasurable qualities, will be accomplished. Without accomplishing the mantra and the image, nothing whatsoever will be accomplished anywhere in the three realms of existence. Therefore, O King, one should accomplish the mantra and the form within one's own mind, wholly free of stains.

A hero who has fallen in time of battle, or likewise a hanged thief, should be washed with eight vases at the cremation ground, O lord of men, on the night of the eighth or the night of the bhūtas. Having worshiped it well with scents, incense, lamps, many kinds of food, and red flowers, and having duly placed vajras on the head, the heart, the crown, the navel, and so on,

in a triangular fire pit one makes with red colored powder a heart lotus together with its emblems, on the petals in the cardinal directions the emblems of the Jinas, and in the intermediate directions likewise the goddesses' own emblems; on the three outer lines, in the maṇḍala of the ten directions, one likewise makes the emblems of the wrathful ones. To its left are the corpse's lotus hand and the sword, and to the right of the maṇḍala, its feet.

Having thoroughly protected oneself from the harms of the mātṛkās mentioned before with the vajras of the Lord of the Jinas, the mantrin performs the fire offering to the right of the hearth with flesh together with blood. Its mantra is oṃ hrīṃ phreṃ hūṃ ending in phaṭ; one should make the fire offering a hundred times multiplied by ten, seated in vajra posture, unmoving like the mountain of the immortals, at midnight.

When the fire offering is complete, the corpse-possessing spirit, flashing and blazing like a sword, with sharp fangs and three eyes, who roars, crushes the very earth beneath his feet, and terrifies the practitioner, stands at the edge of the hearth laughing "kaha kaha" and dances with a terrifying body. A mantrin who sees it, is afraid, and loses heart will go in an instant to the city of Yama.

In the cremation ground, for the sake of accomplishment, the mantrin should not fear even the lord of the asuras.
Seeing an unwavering mind, the lord of the bhūtas, now accomplished, will speak thus:
"Excellent hero, you have accomplished me. Tell me everything: what shall I do now?"
When it has said this, the practitioner should ask, for the benefit of others, for what his own mind desires:

"The sword, the touch, the lord of elixirs, the pill of the nectar fruit, rocana, eye ointment,
foot ointment, and wooden sandals: grant me these eight worldly accomplishments.
Dividing, expelling, destroying existence, paralyzing, summoning, and control:
accomplish all my accomplishments." Then it answers in turn, "I shall do all of these."

Having accomplished the lord of the bhūtas, O lord of men, the practitioner goes to the underworlds, to the atmosphere,
to the palace of the supreme god, to the summit of Meru, and to whatever lies beyond the ocean.
Riding upon it, holding a sword in his hand, he performs the works of the world in places upon the earth.
Therefore, for the sake of sentient beings, one should accomplish the lord of the asuras with supreme compassion.

The first letter of the name becomes the mind vajra, O lord of men, of the gods and goddesses. The speech vajra is all the letters of the name, and whatever goes beyond that is the body vajra. From that the mantras of the individual limbs become manifold; rarely are they accomplished merely by reading them. They are to be meditated upon, worshiped, and recited according to one's own Jina family, by the divisions of mind, speech, and body.

Each mantra family is, individually, certainly to be recited ten million times; this is well known. The fire offering is a tenth part of that, for as long as it takes to be accomplished by the power of the nature of its qualities. Afterwards it will give results in pacifying and the other rites; otherwise there will be no accomplishment. Those firmly keeping their vows should accomplish the source of mantra through recitation and fire offering together with meditation.

The accomplished pill of a bird's flesh, bound with the three metals, grants the power of moving through the sky.
The lamps of dog, horse, and so on remove from the body hunger, thirst, and other ailments.
With their eyes and bile, the eye ointment of excellent men opens up the earth,
and grants invisibility, control, and the captivating of young women, when accomplished in the glorious charnel ground.

On the night of the dark eighth, or else on the day of Manu, having erected a maṇḍala,
and having made protection all around, place in its center the flesh pills or the eye ointment;
then worship them properly with very fragrant flowers and perform mantra recitation
until they radiate light like the sun in the sky; then take them up and keep them.

If the messenger stands on the living side, the sick one lives; if he stands on the side of space, the sick one is seized by the teeth and meets death. A messenger's question of uneven count surely gives the fruit of well-being; an even one is said to give death. If the messenger utters names such as "snake," then the sick one, seized by the teeth, will meet death. If he asks while the prāṇa is entering, it will be auspicious; while it is going out, it will be inauspicious.

If the messenger's left foot is forward, it is declared a woman; if the right is forward, a man.

Wherever on his own limbs he touches with his hand, in that very place the person has been bitten.
If the eyes and mouth are gaping wide and there is black at the roots of the ears, death is declared.
If there is a faint sound in the white lotus of the heart, then he should be taken in hand.

First, the rite of protection brings well-being to the one who has been bitten and to oneself as well. Earth, water, fire, wind and likewise space should be assigned to the thumb and the other fingers. la and the rest, ending with ha, go in order on the thighs, the belly, the heart, the inside of the mouth and the forehead. hūṃ kṣūṃ hya hyāl and so forth, the ten nāgas, are divided into odd and even, short and long:

on the left side of the body the short seeds are placed at the ears, the throat, the armpits, the waist and the thighs;
on the right side of the body those of the nāgas become long, divided by emitting and withdrawing.
Having protected with the reed of the fingers held quite evenly, known as the Jina and bearing the serpent seed,
if one strikes hard at the heart with it, bearing huṃ kṣūṃ, the one who was bitten is quickly made to stir.

The seed of earth at the forehead, with the space-born placed at the feet, quickly brings paralysis.
If water is placed at the crown and Agni the Crested One in the belly, the poison becomes nonexistent.
The seed of wind at the forehead, with Agni the Crested One at the heart, surely makes the poison move on;
with emptiness entering the crown and earth placed at the feet, the poison is surely cut off.

A white drop at the forehead surely renders even poison of the three kinds harmless.
Red sets it in motion, one like a black cloud makes it penetrate, and yellow color paralyzes.
A wick prepared with the three hot spices, applied where the prāṇa flows down, renders it harmless;
or else, with a finger, apply borax to the little tongue of the wide-opened mouth.

Jātī, vajrī, kumārī, the three hot spices, salt, laṅgalī and devadāli,
brāhmī, kṣāra and aśvagandha, together with the day-maker, and bandhakarkoṭakī,
and excrement, flesh, semen and blood in equal measure, ground with Akṣobhya and made into pills:
placed in the nose, these surely destroy bhūtas or bhūta fever, and the poisons of stationary and moving things.

The seven planets, the sun and the rest, are the times of day and night; at the end of each seventh part,
in the middle between Saturn and the Sun, the void, the impure Kulika, a form of death, moves for half a ghaṭikā.
At that time one should not play with nāgas, nor should one eat poison of the three kinds.
At that very time, one bitten by a snake, struck by a bhūta or pierced by a weapon goes to the city of Yama.

At midday and at midnight, the divisions of what always moves through the times of day and night,
and likewise at dawn and at sunset, are the ghaṭikās of the time of death.
In the middle of the junctures, at the void of the oceans, the bite of time by Kulika prevails for one ghaṭikā.
These should be rightly known as the faces of Rāhu, at the times of each day and night.

On Sunday Ananta holds sway during the times of day and night, in the first portion of the day;
afterwards come the remaining ones; here the mahoragas rise according to the division of the seven planets.
Space-time, space and opening, space-arrow, space-time, space-wealth, space-mountain and space-fire are the ghaṭikās;
from Sunday onward, by the division of the reigns of the eight nāgas, these become poison.

Ananta and Kulika are brahmins, the color of snow; Vāsuki and Śaṅkhapāla are kṣatriyas, red;
Mahāpadma and likewise Takṣaka are vaiśyas, like the finest gold;
Karkoṭa and Padma are śūdras, like a black cloud; the outcastes are of many colors.
Their places of birth are water, Agni the Crested One, earth, wind and the element of space.

The garuḍa is yellow from the feet to the hips, likewise the color of snow up to the navel,
red in color up to the throat, and like a black cloud as far as the creeper of the brows;
therefore he is surely of many colors, sealed by the five principles together with the nāga families.
If one meditates on him, his mudrā surely takes away the poison of nāgas, the diseases of bhūtas and the like.

In the lotus of one's own heart, upon a sun, the letter kṣiṃ becomes the lord of birds;
one should meditate on him adorned with nāga ornaments and radiating the five colors according to all the families.
Then the mantra of the lord of birds: first the syllable oṃ, then pa kṣi, ending in svāhā.
Having recited it ten million times, one should accomplish the lord of birds together with the nāga families.

When the garuḍa is accomplished, the lords of the nāgas and the sons of the nāga lords become servants.
On the supreme ground, bhūtas, yakṣas, grahas, ḍākinīs and mātṛkās
are summoned by the mantra, and the whole host of grahas tells of those bitten by the fangs of time.
Therefore, for the sake of sentient beings, people should first accomplish the lord of garuḍas.

On the Vedas, on the eight petals, on the lord of men, the teeth, the six and the oceans, and the twice-born: in the core, the target in his own direction, first oṃ, the pair of a, then i and the rest on the eight petals; e, ya and the rest on the sixteen, the ten transformed by the three beginning with ka, and ha and kṣa on the twice-born; on the petals of the junctures, the target, transformed by the lunar day, at the time, la together with the final vowels.

Through each of the vowels a and the rest, by the power of the makara and the vase, long and short, five;
on the outer thirty-two petals, my heart essence, the letters vajra tīkṣṇa and the rest;
and outside these as well, for the rites of pacifying and the others, the maṇḍalas of va ya ra la.

For bringing under control and summoning, the letters have their heads toward the center, and their feet toward Agni the Crested One and toward the Moving One, the wind.

For pacifying and the rest, the mantrin should write the feet toward the center and the heads toward fire and wind. Pacifying, increasing, destroying existence, expelling, summoning, bringing under control, together with paralyzing and stupefying: this wheel accomplishes these in the abode of the three realms of existence. Here, by uttering the mantra, by mantra recitation and by fire offerings, one should first accomplish the great mantra vajratīkṣṇa and the rest.

On the eight spokes, in the directions and the intermediate directions; in the twelve places on the spokes; and on the sixteen spokes, in the spaces between as well, the target is placed in the corners; in the center there come ya ma rā ja sa do me ru ṇa, and from that yo. Then, from the middle of the spokes of the center, da ni ra ya kṣe ccha, and then at the end ni. Outside of that are oṃ hrīṃ ṣṭīṃ and vikṛtānana, and for the two kinds of action they become hūṃ and phaṭ.

It proceeds from square to square; O lord of men, this is the name of the target, and this the mantra.
For dividing, killing and bringing under control, ya ma rā is turned about and becomes kṣe da me do sa ca and so on.
For paralyzing, summoning and stupefying, for bestowing strength, for pacifying and for expelling,
there the syllable ya proceeds from the mouth of the center; according to the quality, everything outside is as before.

In front of the heads of the letters, those embraced by vowels become ma.
Of the letters, the final letter of each class, by virtue of the vajra family the fifth, is declared to be haṃ and saṃ.
When the supreme vowel is joined with the two dots, this too, by me, the wisdom letter, becomes u.
aṃ muḥ haṃ huḥ saṃ suḥ mañjuḥñid are set on the petals of the jewel lotus, that is, on the petals of the sun.

Outside, śrī vajra ghoṣaḥs, and manta bhadra joined with sa, become hūṃ and phaṭ. Here the target is at the navel, that is, "so-and-so," and with "Glorious one, please do it!" one urges toward pacifying and the rest. Likewise, in the wheels described earlier, the name of the target should always be written. All of these, through my kindness and by the command of the Lord of Victors, will be accomplished by people.

Whatever sound there is in the region of the heart, heard through the ear-holes of excellent men:
when a person joins the mind to it, made one-pointed, they become of equal taste,
and whatever sound is uttered throughout the world by those born in existence, each one of these is heard just so.
The yogin should cultivate the very consciousness of the all-pervading lord, and hearing from afar as well.

Having bound the cross-legged posture, with the mouth opened so that the teeth do not touch one another,
drawing in the outer winds and placing them, together with nectar, deep in the middle of the navel,
one removes fever, hunger and thirst, and fully stops poison in the supreme body;
this comes also by meditating on a white drop on the forehead, encircled by the vowels and emanating nectar.

Then, closing the mouth, drawing in all the outer wind without remainder through both nostrils
by the force of prāṇa, and joining it with the downward-clearing wind, apāna, which is like stainless lightning,
through practice made habitual over time, it becomes of equal taste in the middle of moon, sun and fire.
It frees the body from food and the like, removes hunger and thirst, and grants deathlessness itself.

Turned away from the sun, standing in the sunlight, one looks at one's own shadow with steady eyes. Afterwards, when one gazes intently into the sky, one sees a smoke-colored man of equal taste. Through practice made habitual for six months, one sees the treasures lying in the earth as through openings in the ground; or else this comes by entering the shade of a tree and meditating on a garland of drops in the middle of the sky.

The power, the śakti, which goes from the middle of the navel to the other place, to the end of the twelve and the end of the digits, is arrested at the navel; and when, like stainless lightning, she rises in the form of a staff, she moves through the central channel from wheel to wheel with a slow and playful gait, until, by sheer force, at the opening of the crown she touches the outer skin like a needle.

At that time, by the supreme forceful method, the downward-clearing wind should be urged onto the upper path.
When the paired winds have ceased, one pierces the crown and goes to the supreme cities.
Thus, by the vajra fully awakened in the mind, one becomes a sky-goer, together with one's objects.
One comes to have the nature of the five superknowledges; and again, this is Viśvamātā of the yogins.

The mudrā conforming to illusion, like forms in the mind, in the sky and in mirrors,
illuminates the three worlds and sends forth many rays like stainless lightning.
Indivisible from the outer and the body, free of objects, mere appearance, abiding in space,
she herself embraces mind and mental factors; through the forms of many beings, she is one alone.

In essence, the action mudrā, which is turbid, and the imagined wisdom mudrā are to be abandoned. For the sake of perfect, complete awakening there is the supreme mudrā, the supreme mother of the supreme Victors, unstained, unchanging, equal to space, destroyer of darkness, all-pervading, abiding in the tiered stages that yogins traverse. With the splendor of wisdom she steals away the stains of existence; she who is attuned to Kālacakra should be meditated upon.

"In the three realms of existence there are subtle particles; here in the formless there is consciousness, and likewise no consciousness at all; the wisdom of the buddha abides in none of these": these are the words the Buddhists teach. Since people lacking the unchanging grasp that emptiness and view things as empty, therefore the Chief taught in the Mantra Vehicle the bliss of the innate body, which is without emission.

At the time of the sacrifice that goes to the cow, the buffalo, the horse and the elephant, your senses
are held in bliss upon the objects of the body; that is the cessation of object and subject in the yoga of wisdom.
What becomes, for those who keep the discipline, the drink of the soma creeper in a cowhide together with blood,
that is the moon nectar at the crown, abiding in the rajas of the bhaga, the all-pervading form of joy.

Brahmā is the body of living beings, Hara is speech, and Viṣṇu is mind; these are the three Vedas. The syllable oṃ is these three letters; they are moon, sun and the eater of oblations, fire, and the qualities and the three channels.

That which has a lineage, distinct from the lineage of the body, abides in objects and qualities; it is the guardian of existence, having the form of nāda. In its center is the indestructible, which is free of objects and without qualities: the immutable.

These secrets from the end of the Vedas were formerly spoken by Brahmā to the yogins.
Sages whose knowledge of time had decayed have taught here even the killing of living creatures.
People eagerly engage in it for the sake of heaven, and it brings the evil fruit of hell.
Charcoal, though washed with a stream of milk, never takes on the color of the moon.

Those people deceived by the words of the Vedas, which lack union, by those devoid of sacred commitment,
guard their own wives by day and by night so as not to raise another's son,
and say that what is given by the son goes to the world of the hungry ghosts and becomes a gift to the father.
Therefore this gift of desire, which bestows the fruit of equal bliss, has been concealed by wicked brahmins.

If a pedigreed stallion gets a supreme horse on a fine mare when its owner is someone else,
the fortune that comes from its power returns again; why is it not also the gain of the mare's owner?
Why, then, do the lustful guard their own wives at every hour of the day and night?
When you have died, who are you, and who is your own wife? Alas, they are bound by nonvirtuous karma!

The gift of cattle and the gift of land, and likewise other gifts, bestow enjoyments in the human world. Gifts of medicine and food take away all disease, and take away hunger and thirst as well. In every gift of desire, the fruit of equal bliss is bestowed; what need is there to speak of it at the time of the circle? Charming wives, sisters, and fortunate daughters too should be fully given in the secret gift.

Excellent courtesans become the action seal; the secret woman, another's wife, is the commitment seal;
the independent woman, who delights in many objects, is the Dharma seal; and one's own wife is the wisdom seal.
The excellent courtesan, twelve years old, delights in supreme bliss; another's wife is sixteen;
the independent woman has reached twenty years; and one's own wife, together with the daughter, is thirty years of age.

In former times the buddhas gave the earth, elephants, horses, many chariots, and loads of gold
for the sake of buddhahood, and again they fully gave even head, blood, and flesh.
Through these they did not attain the buddhahood they sought; then they fully gave the gift of desire.
Through that secret gift, those beings were born as buddhas in the family that gives rise to the Victors.

Copper becomes the equal of gold, and a fragment of crystal the equal of the jewels in the crowns of the gods. Through the gift of desire, the excellent courtesan becomes a daughter of the family of immortals, as a scrap of hide becomes musk. If an ass can gain a pedigreed horse, and a goat the supreme lord of elephants, why should this not be gained through the gift of one's wife by that goddess in the family that gives rise to the Victors?

Gifts to an object of loving-kindness do not bestow the fruit of equal bliss, because of the power of beings' equality; nor likewise do gifts to a supreme object given with expectation, in the family of the Victors, because beings have the nature of Māra. But when followers of the Sugata fully give to the pair of objects endowed with compassion and joy, for the sake of others, then, because of their lowliness and their supremacy, the two accumulations are perfected and the inexhaustible fruit of bliss is bestowed.

Where caste becomes authoritative, there, O lord of men, the Vedas become authoritative.
Where the Vedas are authoritative, there, surely, in this place upon the earth, sacrifice is authoritative.
Where sacrifice is authoritative, there harm to many kinds of livestock and to humans becomes authoritative.
Where harm becomes authoritative, there the sin that brings the terrors of hell is authoritative.

For the sake of maintaining a dwelling, the Blessed One declared that he approves the consecration of a glorious monastery. There, it is said, the donor gives gifts of medicine and food, which pacify disease. Without giving, this monastery, a dwelling upon the earth, becomes a lair of animals. Where even a single member of the Saṅgha is maintained, there, O lord of men, are the Dharma and the Buddha too.

People go for refuge to the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha for the sake of liberation. Yet this buddha dwelling here in the monastery, the book with written letters that is the Dharma itself, and the Saṅgha wearing saffron robes do not bestow supreme, all-pervading bliss even in hundreds of thousands of births. The teachers are the Buddha himself upon the supreme ground of the earth, and the Dharma is their teaching.

The Saṅgha is whoever dwells there with a heart of great joy and with compassion for all beings.
Here it is taught as fourfold and as twofold; here, again, it is the śrāvaka and the unsurpassed.

Monks and nuns, and here again great laymen and laywomen;
yoginīs and yogins who delight in innate bliss, laywomen and laymen too.

For the sake of merit and gnosis, the many kinds of gifts that are always most desired;
feasts of offering and the like for the śrāvakas, and for the yogin the desired gift that brings supreme bliss.
Whatever donors give with a heart of great joy and at all times with a mind of attachment,
they complete merit and gnosis and go, in this very birth, to the abode of supreme bliss.

Those śrāvakas without learning who openly revile the master and the Jina
are slain by the lord of obstructors and swiftly go to Avīci, which causes supreme terror.
Upon one whom the supreme lord of the Jinas blesses as himself through the powers of mantra,
what monk among men, in the place of fixed vows, is his equal in pure conduct?

Monks who delight in supreme bliss, or wandering mendicants, naked ascetics, the shaven-headed and the matted-haired;
the learned who delight in hearing and reading, all who cause the path to decline, and those attached to a self;
those here always attached to their own and others' sons and wives, attached to what may and may not be eaten;
delighting in caste and in non-caste, attached to vessels and non-vessels, they perform austerities.

The buddha field together with the buddha, which bestows the fruit of equal bliss, and body, speech, mind and passion: having wholly cast these aside, with sinful minds they earnestly seek some other all-pervading lord, by hundreds of fixed vows at holy fields and fords in other lands, by fasting, by leaping from mountains, in battle, when the sun is seized by the eclipsing planet, and by leaping onto weapons and into fire, desiring the many pleasures of the senses.

All of these were laid down in former times by the māras so that they might drink blood:
that to die by fords and fasting, to be struck down in battle, and for cows and at the sun,
for the sake of release, for home, wealth and land, and in the rites of brahmins, to die is heaven.
Therefore one's own and others' bodies, this abode of equal bliss, are to be protected.

Whoever, having heard the conduct of the supreme Jina from the king of tantras and having received empowerment,
then again gives rise to jealousy, enters the eighth hell for as long as it endures,
where, by the force of the fruit of nonvirtue, the hell beings wander over ground of needle points.
Therefore, as long as there is jealousy in their minds, people should not take empowerment.

A wife of the family that gives birth to the Jinas, perfect in generosity and moral discipline, perfect in patience and diligence, perfect in meditative concentration, wisdom, skillful means, aspiration, power and gnosis: through this gift, swiftly, in this very birth, for the yogin whose mind is filled with great joy; and those deluded people who hinder him in this go straight to hell.

When a coward, his heart trembling greatly, for the sake of land and riches seizes sharp swords
and enters a battle of four divisions thronged on every side with fighters,
and, terrified at seeing a host of elephants, lets the swords fall from the palms of his hands,
that is not the fault of the sword. Just so it is with the family of mantra.

By mantra and by the sequence of the heroes, asuras, nāgas and gods are to be accomplished in a fierce place. Through self-blessing, the goddesses dwelling in the pledge family, through meditative concentration, mantra recitation and fire offering, are brought under control in the pure sequence by the mantrin, with an uninterrupted mind of great joy. And from whatever has fully become the wish-fulfilling jewel of gnosis comes this path of the Jina.

Fire is the very enemy of mercury; parted from fire, mercury is never bound.
If it is not bound, there is no making of gold, and without gold the alchemists have no enjoyment.
Likewise, parted from union with a woman, the mind of yogins is never bound.
If there is no binding, there is no piercing of the body, and an unpierced body does not here bestow innate bliss.

In gardens or on mountains, free of people and wild beasts, one should accomplish the peaceful mantras.
The fierce ones are done in a fierce charnel ground; in the abode of a supreme deity, paralysis and also stupefaction;
subjugation and summoning the mantrin performs on the supreme ground. But bliss in full union with all the mudrās,
in another place and in another womb, will not be accomplished for humans even in a hundred thousand births.

Having done mantra recitation with rosaries at home and in town, in the times of day and night,
they grow weary and bewildered, and, devoid of passion, they declare again: "In this there is no siddhi of mantra."
They do not truly know the place, emptiness, the time, or the abiding in the supreme night,
like one who, for a disease of the toes, smears medicines on the crown of the head.

Even without meditative concentration, without those who perform mantra recitation, the great maṇḍalas and the seats,
the fire offering, consecration by mantra, the colored powders, and the inviting and urging of the Jina families,
having abandoned union with a woman, the supreme, all-pervading lord bestows the siddhi of the mudrā of bliss.
Therefore the yogin should meditate on it at each time of day and night for the sake of liberation.

When one's own mind has entered into fainting and sleep, O lord of men, it becomes devoid of intrinsic nature;
when awake, it makes things appear as having intrinsic nature. These are not the path of liberation for living beings.
The mind divided by entity and non-entity does not bestow equal bliss on vajra yogins.
That very mind of one's own which embraces the wisdom of space and abides in innate bliss bestows liberation.

Abiding in order with oṃ āḥ hūṃ ho, first here one should always purify the liquor, and then realize it. Having made it blaze, and having meditated in stages on it as the melting of the hare-marked one (the moon), then from that vessel, with drops, using the tip of the ring finger of the lotus hand of the hare-marked one, upon the ground, one makes a triangle on the outside, and in its center a circle like the day-maker, the sun.

In its center the mantrin meditates on his wheel of gnosis abiding in the three worlds, and then, with thumb and ring finger, at each time of day and night, performs the satisfying and the rest. Here, first of all, to the lords of the land and of the village goes the first torma, with the names of center and periphery; to Hārītī, two balls of dough; and again the mantrin gives a torma to the king of the wrathful ones.

To the female messengers the vajra lords give the first portion of the food upon the ground. To the bhūtas they give again the leftovers of what was eaten, and for the sake of virtue this verse of giving is recited. At the head, throat, heart, navel, secret place and crown, one places the limbs with one's own vajras. With the limbs and doors abiding in both families, at the time of sleep, of worship and of intercourse,

the visualization of the deity, whatever it may be, is of two kinds, direct perception and inference.
