Here the Makara is consciousness and the element of space; the Water Jar is formations and wind;
The Fish, the Ram, the Bull and the Twins: feeling becomes fire, and perception water,
And form and earth, the immutable and the innate are the houses beginning with Karkaṭa.
These are the emblems, vajra and the rest, ka ca ṭa pa ta sa, long and short, and the deity of the surplus.

In the Ram, the Twins and Karkaṭa are the Moon, Venus and the Sun; the Lion, the Maiden and the Balance;
Mars, Jupiter and Rāhu; Mercury, Saturn and the Hooded One; the Scorpion, and the beginning and end of the Bow.
Thus the elements, earth and the rest, combine with one another everywhere, O lord of men.
This world becomes the nature of the karma of sentient beings, filled with every jewel.

The wish-fulfilling trees on earth, the tastes and the supreme taste, the medicines and the other elixirs,
Produced by particles that remove all disease, these sentient beings do not see;
What they see is ordinary grass, wood, water, dust, stones and iron.
For the pretas, water is a great fire; for people gone to hell, it cuts and pierces them all over.

To liberate these, the Lord of the Jinas, together with his sons, fully entered the land of karma,
took up a womb, and through supreme compassion brought forth awakening;
having vanquished the afflictions and the māras, he turned the wheel of Dharma upon the surface of the earth;
having displayed his illusory emanation, the Blessed One is again none other than that very one whose body is pure.

Therefore the body of the Lord of the Jinas, though born, is undiminished, and abides even in the three realms of existence. At all times that Blessed One does not forsake the aims of sentient beings, and he is not bound by karma. Thus, O Sun, I, the Lord of the World, abide in the land of karma within the three realms of existence, giving the path to sentient beings and taking away the fear of hell; other gods never do this.

Those who assail the Dharma cling to their own bodies, to the bodies of others, and to their own women. They hold that when one dies, heaven comes from a son's fire offering and from the merit of one's kin; that by harming animals in sacrifice, and by dying in battle, one enters the heavenly worlds; and that by the body's falling at a sacred ford one goes to the supreme state. These are not teachings that grant liberation.

When you have abandoned that very path, which mother of yours, which father, takes away the fear of death?
What excellent son or daughter, what brothers, wife or sisters, what lord or host of kinsmen?
So that these sages whom I have blessed may go to liberation here in the center of this wheel,
therefore, O Sun, at my word, have the sages enter a single caste.

If suffering comes when a stick strikes the body, then the stick is at fault in that. Yet it is not the stick; it is driven by the hand, so here the fault lies wholly with the hand. Yet it is not the hand; it is driven by the mind, and the mind in turn here by the fire of anger, the great venomous one. Therefore this fire of anger, enemy of beings, is slain by the bodhisattvas.

From attachment come hatred and the other faults, and for even the most excellent of gods and humans, attachment comes from their beloved wives and from consorting with others. To uproot it, all the sons of the Jina gave even that away as a gift to whoever desired it. Therefore this accumulation of merit, devoted to giving and bestowing the fruit of equal bliss, has become the friend of the three worlds, and at all times takes away the fear of hell from all people.

The very attachment by which sentient beings are ruined is that by which the protectors are freed.
What sentient beings guard day after day, that the protectors give away.
The bliss abiding in their own hearts that sentient beings let go, that the Victorious Ones guard.
Therefore this conduct of the supreme Jinas is hard for gods, nāgas and asuras to perform.

Earth, water, fire, wind and space, the qualities, mind, intellect, ego-sense and prāṇa,
forms and the rest, eyes and the rest, objects and their subjects, and the five faculties of action:
among these, which pervade the bodies of humans and of animals alike, which caste is elder and which younger?
You gurus of men, together with the lords of men, proud and arrogant in your caste, by what is your caste the elder?

The combustible one is earth, vimala becomes water, and tutthaka is fire; the element where wind and space are mingled is the moving one; the hare-marked one and vermilion make the seventh as well. Kākṣī, kāśīsa and sulfur likewise, with realgar and orpiment, are space, wind, fire and water; red ochre is earth; and the salts are zetsa salt, sea-born salt, black rock salt and hearth salt.

These are the elements, earth and the rest. Again, here, navasāra, yavakṣāra and sarja,
borax, and that born from kṣaca: these have the nature of space, wind, fire, water and earth.
The water of stone becomes earth, the water of the hare-marked one is water, the water of fire is fire;
the water of poison, with the nature of wind and space, and the other, are of the nature of the three curved knives.

Yellow nutgrass is the earth element; śaktuka, the horned one and the black one are water, fire and wind;
the black heap is called space; the others, the secondary poisons, are to be known as fivefold.
Stones and living substances come in many kinds, like fruits, with the shapes of heads, conches and the like.
Their iron is of six kinds: the thorn-born, likewise the divine, and the magnet in four kinds.

Mercury here is of two kinds, fixed and unfixed: the transmuting and the non-transmuting.
The fixed transmutes metals, and moreover, when eaten, it transmutes the body too.
Whatever mercury is non-transmuting, that mercury too, by being boiled and transformed,
transmutes metals and transmutes them further, taking away all diseases, leaving aside the iron of the bones.

At first it goes off in the form of smoke; set on the fire and boiled, it gradually
comes to have sound and leaping, and then becomes trembling and untrembling.
That with sound and leaping does not transmute; the trembling one transmutes leaf foil;
the untrembling transmutes by the spear. These three kinds, here too, are transformed by the transforming agents.

Through the power of supreme medicines it becomes potent and melts all metals.

If those were lacking, who on the face of the earth would accomplish its binding?
Once it is bound, one becomes a sky-goer, and likewise, when it is eaten, even death is overcome.
Lacking gnosis, kings should strive hard to prepare this lord of elixirs, mercury.

Their great wealth is laid out on performances of song and music as well.
O lord of men, whatever is squandered was not burned in the work of elixirs, nor given to the work of the Dharma.
Whoever exhausts his wealth for the sake of evil is bound by his evil deeds.
Therefore bodhisattvas spend it all, without remainder, for the sake of sentient beings.

That which, when smeared on, at once removes the stain from leaves of copper is the medicine.
That which removes every disease and digests the metals is seen in the land of the fortunate.
The binding of it that others achieve, surely over the span of a year,
By the methods of fumigation and placement, does not go beyond piercing a thousand parts.

All the herbs of very hot taste, the group of transformers and the group of sours,
And fermented gruels are used again and again for kneading and fumigating.
For turning mercury into silver as well, the seed is purified by those to which flowers have been added.
With the double salt, lapis, mouse, horse's hoof and poisons, smeared with sesame oil, it becomes soft.

With elephant and human urine poured on seven times, the ash of plantain, citraka and the like,
All the transformers, the hot substances, and again the secondary elixirs, purified, should be rubbed in.
Again and again, with the heat of fire, it should be rubbed a hundred times more and dried.
Likewise this, combined with a hundredfold conch powder and mixed with sulphur,

For melting the metals, this biḍa becomes one eighth part of the mercury. It is fumigated in the swing apparatus for eight nights, until what lies in the heart of the elixir has melted. Boiled fiercely, this stainless one, kneaded and melted, becomes powerful. When a single metal has melted, by the body of the Sun and the hare-marked one (the moon), that is, gold and silver, it changes the color of all metals.

That pervading transformer, together with the great one, enters by means of lead, realgar and monkey fat.
Thus, when those who expound the work of mercury perform it day by day,
At other times it binds, becoming clearly heavy and hard, and entirely free of all faults.
It removes from his supreme body all the suffering of disease, death and poverty.

On a golden lotus half a cubit wide, with petals of jewels, at the hub, in the center of the heart, a diamond;
In the directions of the god (Indra), Yama and Kubera, pure sapphire, ruby and crystal;
In the west the jewel karketana; in the directions of Fire, the asura and Hara, steel, copper and silver;
On the petal of the wind, gold; and at the hub, a seat born of gold and pyrite.

At the heart, upon that, Jambhala born of gold, with two hands holding a mongoose and granting boons; On the petals, eight goddesses born of gold or born of silver, their hands holding lotuses and granting boons; He is crowned by the buddha Ratneśa, his hands holding a jewel and granting boons, and so too the yakṣiṇīs. On the petals of the directions and intermediate directions are the eight letters in order, ha and the rest, short and long.

With perfumes and flowers, at the three sessions of each day, having made offerings in a vessel of the moon, that is, silver, With this mantra, auṃ jaṃ, ending in svāhā, with bhala and jalenjrāya in the middle, The alchemists, with the mind of a delighted king, should offer water for protection. By the power of such protection, the lord of bhūtas and the lord of nāgas will not steal the elixir.

With brick, the transformers and the group of sours, the elixir should be kneaded and fumigated for ten days.
Then again, what remains in the dregs should be separated and mixed in, at every stage.
If again it does not separate from the dregs, the elixir dies, and that too should be melted into it.
When mercury eats its own womb, it then eats every other womb as well.

Lead, steel, brass, copper, and what is born of pyrite, or other pure metals born of the field,
Are mixed together in equal parts; for the work of silver, what is born of white pyrite and realgar, in equal parts.
At first a sixtieth part of food is digested, and then it should be increased by adding one part at a time,
Until it comes to a quarter part; more than that should not be given.

In one fortnight it digests twofold, and again this, likewise, is the purified seed.
Thus it comes to digest two, three and eightfold; and again this melting is by the supreme biḍas.
If it does not melt, the elixir is placed in the heat of the wheel for up to eight nights.
By the medicines of the supreme touch, pyrite and metal set in the fire are digested without an opening.

From pyrite the metal comes to eightfold strength, and when it is melted, again eightfold.
By that the seed too is eightfold, and again from that the diamond jewel is eightfold.
Thus metals, jewels, secondary elixirs and elixirs should be boiled in the fire until they have come to the boil,
And then it comes to pierce many hundreds of thousands.

If the touchstone medicines are lacking, it is digested also by kneadings with the supreme biḍa and by poisons,
Until it no longer trembles; then the transforming gold becomes equal in measure.
And again others, twofold and threefold, by pouring the liquid of molten gold and sesame oil into the mercury.

For the sake of the substance, one should carefully pour one eighth part of lead into the molten gold.

For works of gold and silver, sharp gold, nāga, copper, lead, vermilion, realgar and sulfur,
gśa' tshe, the moon, likewise lhang tsher skyes, and orpiment are killed with the things to be calcined.
Then the transmuting poison, salt and the subsidiary flavors in equal parts, and mercury equal to the iron,
are ground with sour bile for ten days and nights, and the mixture is poured into the middle of a stone.

It is placed inside the earth for six months with fire above, and fumigated at all times.
That mercury, drawn out by the vowels, with a sixteenth part becomes able to pierce, and furthermore
copper and the moon become equal to gold. This auspicious gold destroys the sufferings of the alchemists.
If silver is given in an eighth part, pure copper, once pierced, also turns into silver.

Those stones on the earth that resemble the body of a fruit, with the forms of conches, heads and the like,
are the ores of it. To them add ghee and honey, and melt them with a fierce wind.
Then, having ground them again and purified them with borax and the groups of transmuting agents,
the alchemist, recognizing that ore of the metal, should melt it into twice its amount of mercury.

In seven days and nights the vessel becomes pure. The water of the three fruits boiled in equal measure,
iron powder killed with herbs, together with ghee and honey, and together with mercury;
fresh cow's milk and old śāli rice as food, with few sour leaves and little salt,
free of fish, and abstaining from sesame oil and the like: by those who eat these, of the people within the region,

knowing how far its piercing reaches, a thousandfold piercing, transmuted by a hundred, a hundred and ten million piercings, it should be eaten at the proper time each day, starting from a portion the size of a mustard seed and growing up to the final portion. The mansion or the earthen house should be kept wholly free of cold wind and of heat. This middling elixir in six months makes a godlike body, free of white hair and wrinkles.

Karkoṭī, devadālī, the three pungents, bṛhatī, nimba and kirātatikta
should be boiled and drunk; then, while it is still undigested, one should remain fasting for three days and nights.
Likewise moon water and the like, the waters of fire and of poison, of stone and of bhallātaka,
foul smells, earth, stone, poisonous flesh, and other tested things are taken by the mantra practitioner as well.

Whatever things are like poison, which when eaten overpower violently, all of them are medicines.
Having powdered them, one should eat a little with ghee and honey, just so.
Clearly, what is eaten by cattle-like people is not a medicine that grants accomplishment.
What gives them death and is supremely terrifying becomes nectar for the yogis.

With the juice of the leaves and very bodies of daṇḍotpala, śāliparṇī, the water-born and sārī,
mercury and sulfur in equal parts are ground with iron in a magnetite vessel for three days and nights.
Whoever eats a measure of a quarter ṭaṅka at the proper time each day for six months
is freed from leprosy and other diseases, is free of white hair and wrinkles, and has the form of a sixteen-year-old.

The quivering mercury should also be killed with pokkāṇa, the sky, śaktu and kīra's beak.
For killing it with other medicines, dmar ring, lhang tsher skyes, gold and magnetite in equal parts
are combined into one with living, stainless mercury and made into a pill of six srang.
Boiled with sour gruel in the magnetite vessel described before, it carries away wrinkles and white hair.

In six months, two, one, a month together with the lord of men, ten days and nights, seven days, three days, a single day and a single instant, by the power of this, accomplishment arises in the body. The common and the middling are freedom from fears; the supreme is the accomplishment of gnosis. The supreme medicine comes from the power of a person's own virtue, not from the piercings that follow upon a single birth.

Where creepers grow upon other trees, and where animals fight at the proper time each day,
treasure is revealed upon swords by lamps of human fat with wicks of lotus fiber.
There, at the proper time each day, one should recite the mantras of the lords of the bhūtas
until the signs for digging appear; then the mantra practitioners gain the accomplishment of substances.

First one should accomplish the mantras of the six families, blessed by the vajra of the Lord of the Jinas.
Having accomplished the yakṣiṇīs, one should then accomplish also those called fields upon the earth.
Having accomplished the nāginīs, one should accomplish the taste of the nectar fruits and those medicines.
Having accomplished the ḍākinīs, one should accomplish also the supremely terrifying asuras.

Having accomplished the nāgas and the rest, the yogi should accomplish the most excellent gods and humans.
Having accomplished Dhūmā and the rest, one should accomplish the central channel, which strikes terror into death.
Having accomplished prāṇa and the rest, one should accomplish the drops of the melting hare-bearer (the moon).
Having accomplished supreme bliss, one should accomplish the all-pervading, innate body of the Jina.

Whatever gives death to sentient beings, when blessed by accomplished mantras, is entirely nectar. Whatever desired accomplishment cannot be reached, that too, in the three realms of existence, is to be reached through meditative concentration.

The wisdom that is hard to find becomes easy to find, blessed by bliss in the vajra and the lotus.
Thus, in just this way, may the vajra-holder grant living beings equal bliss at all times!

Rāhu, fire, moon and the Sun are declared to be four; earth, water, fire, wind and space
are five; the remaining host of the planets is six; in the directions of this Meru
the continents become four; the sense objects are five, and these are taught as outer.
The qualities, sattva and the rest, are three; this is the other set, and it is just so in the middle of the body.

In the lotus, consciousness, joy, blood and what is called nectar are the first four; from these,
the bones and all the rest are five; then the eyes and the others are six;
the hands and feet are four; the digits on the hands and feet are five;
their joints are three. Here this whole sequence should be understood in this way.

The mountains, the divisions of the principles, the tastes multiplied by taste, and what are called the twenty suns,
the sixty divisions, the six apertures in the whole human body, and the number of the months:
all these gathered together become three times nine; fifty-four is the movement of prāṇa;
and the movement of the planets is declared again, by the others, to be the movement of the yoginīs.

Wisdom and space are two; and again this other set: the vehicles and the Three Jewels too,
the afflictions, the māras and the abodes, and again, assuredly, the bases of miraculous power and the defilements,
the fearlessnesses and the truths, and again likewise the applications of mindfulness, up to
the four means of gathering: by the vajras of the Lord of the Jinas, the yogin should meditate on these.

The five clairvoyances, the powers, the views, and the five eyes of the most supreme Lords of the Jinas; likewise the aggregates, the faculties and what are called the recollections, six each; the seven limbs of awakening; the offerings, which are seven; the eight limbs of the path, each rightly engaged; the eight liberations of the embodied; the nine branches of the supreme scripture, numbered as the apertures; and the grounds are the measure of the directions.

The powers of the buddhas, numbered as the directions, and clearly the ten masteries, and the twelve limbs of dependent origination, in arising and in cessation; likewise the twice nine unshared qualities of a buddha; the thirty-two major marks of the most supreme Lord of the Jinas, and the eighty minor marks as well: all these states of the supreme All-Pervading Lord the mantrin should meditate on in the body.

The moon by its nature is one; the pair, the hands, the eyes, sexual union, the fortnights and the solar courses are two; the worlds, the times, the qualities and fire are three; the oceans, the ages and the Vedas are declared to be four; the arrows, the elements and the senses assuredly become five; the tastes and the seasons are six; the planets, the mountains and the sages are the number seven; likewise the nāgas and the vasus are eight.

The channels, the apertures, the planets and the treasures are nine; the directions are ten; the Īśas are eleven; the suns, the Bodiless One, the Manus, the existences, the lunar days and the lords of men increase one by one from twelve; the faults are eighteen; the supreme Jinas, all of them, are twenty-four; what is known as the principles is twenty-five; what is called the twice-born becomes thirty-two.

In all the kings of tantras the vajra words have assuredly been concealed by the vajra-holder;
in the Ādibuddha, for the liberation of living beings, all of it has been taught openly.
Therefore, the cause lacking nothing at all of the worldly and the transcendent is the supreme:
the glorious tantra, the Ādibuddha, the complete pronouncement of the Lord of the Jinas, O Sucandra.

To that in which there is no beginning, end or middle, no abiding, death or becoming, no sound, smell or taste,
no touch, no form, no mind, no primal nature and no person, no bondage, liberation or agent,
no seed and no time of manifestation, no nature of suffering or of bliss in all existence;
to that which is nirvāṇa, signless, free of activity, without qualities, I pay homage.

Time, the manifold and the rest, the vajra person without equal, all-pervading, free of elaboration,
abiding immutable, with ears, noses, mouths, eyes and heads on every side, and hands and feet on every side,
the end of the elements, protector of beings, supreme holder of the three realms of existence, the cause of causes,
the goal to be reached by the yogas of knowledge and the rest, the state of supreme bliss: homage to Kālacakra.

Of the nature of emanating power, like the fire of lightning, with the splendor of twelve suns, the light of vajra wisdom, which travels to the supreme state: to that visarga I pay homage. White, protector of the three worlds, dripping from the hare-bearer (the moon), abiding perfectly at the crown of the world, nectar that dispels death and destroys the terrors of existence: to the form of the bindu I pay homage.

Mind alone, of the nature of mantra, surrounded by deities, of the nature of suffering and of bliss,
peaceful in form toward the holy, terrible toward those whose experience of their own deeds is terrible,
who produces in one's own mind, according to rule, the fruit of whatever karma one has done,
Lord of the World, of manifold forms, creator of the three realms: homage also to Vajrasattva.

One and many, yet one; equal and unequal, equal; right and left, front and back,
above and below, everywhere; white, green, varied, the form of one great color; short and long
and very long; called without qualities, yet with qualities; woman and man, yet neither man nor woman:
you who are the one support of all, the excellent bhaga, the supreme bhaga, to you I bow, to you I bow.

Whoever in the world of humans is the glorious guru, Vajradhara free of evil, he is I myself; by harming him, living beings assuredly go to hell, and in this there is nothing to wonder at. By pleasing him I am delighted; to rage at him with the fire of wrath is what is called the great wrath. He himself grants beings the fruit of liberation and equal bliss; he is worthy of homage and worthy of worship by his sons.

Who is a sinner? The one who does not bow three times a day at the excellent lotus feet of the glorious guru.
Who is ignorant? The one who does not offer a maṇḍala of many kinds of flowers three times a day.
Who goes swiftly to Avīci? The one who brings weariness to the guru, the giver of equal bliss.
Who gains wisdom and gnosis and does not fall? The one who never forsakes the feet of the supreme guru.

Who has fallen? The one who kills the wind that dwells in the three channels at the time of Ananta.
Who is a hero? The one who kills the prāṇa and apāna winds on the even and the uneven paths.
Who is a patron? The one who gives everything to the glorious guru: his own body, his son, his wife and the rest.
Who is base? The one who deceives, whose mind is led by the evil of his own heart, full of guile and pretense.

On the multicolored seat of the glorious wheel of the glorious Dharma, to which the foremost gods bow,
There the Buddha, the guru of gods and humans, was seated, and when Candra's request was concluded,
The lord of men, for the sake of beings, taught the king of tantras, the Ādibuddha.
That I have now set down as a tantra of few vowels and consonants, O Sun!

From what the Buddha taught, I have spoken the Kālacakra, composed in the sragdharā meter.
Puṇḍarīka, Vajradhara, make this a teaching for the mantra practitioners.
Just as the family of sages, together with the Sun, has come to gain wisdom and gnosis from this,
So may the beings who dwell in the threefold existence become the same through the kindness of Kālacakra.

O Sun, you will be a lord of men in a later existence, through the one yoga of Kālacakra.
Born of this Brahmā family, I, the lord of men Yaśas, am Mañjughoṣa, and my beloved
Is glorious Tārā. The one with the white lotus, giver of every virtue, is Lokanātha, who bears the lotus as his mark.
With the utpala stalk, behold all existence, dwelling in self and others, its meaning just as it is.

Having seen with the utpala stalk all existence, dwelling in self and others, its meaning just as it is,
The Sun, together with the chief sages, with heads bearing diadems and with their hands
Bowed to the lotus feet of Kulika and said: "Sole Teacher, with your supreme compassion
Draw the family of sages out of the sufferings of fearsome saṃsāra and set them on the path of gnosis.

"Though you are old, you are a youth; though you are the son of all the Jinas, you are first of all the Ādibuddha. You unite with women yet keep pure conduct; with supreme compassion you are the world's friend, the foe of Yama. Though you are peaceful, you are the excellent vajra that steals away the fear of death; you are forever the Māra of Māras. Though you are liberated, you, Yaśas, fully enter this existence to ripen beings.

"From the Brahmā family, I, the Sun, bowed to by the family of sages, fall at your feet.
For the liberation of beings you have made the whole of the Kālacakra shine clearly on earth.
To you, Kulika of the Kulika line, bowed to by the Sun and the lords of the earth, glorious Yaśas in glorious Kalāpa,
At your lotus feet those terrified by fearsome saṃsāra go above all for refuge."

On the full moon of the month of Vaiśākha, when the night had passed and the day had not yet fully begun, The Sun, together with the people of the sages, blessed by the Universal Lord, attained the accomplishment of the mudrā. "Just as my vajra mind abides throughout the earth for the liberation of beings, So through the power of Kālacakra may it abide in the threefold existence of all beings."

He who works for the good of all beings, having taught the meaning of the tantra with supreme compassion,
Gave his firm consent to his son Puṇḍarīka, supreme treasury of virtues:
"First you must set the entire king of tantras, complete and lacking nothing, in books,
And then, having composed a commentary as you wish, you should go on to another abode, friend of the world."

The bodhisattvas who above are the supreme terror of the asuras on the side of Māra, The kings of the wrathful ones with their queens, who dwell in the directions and intermediate directions in the world of humans, The lords of the nāgas below the earth, who at all times bind every unvirtuous host of grahas: May all of them, at every time of day and night, protect the whole of the ignorant world.

The tantra, glorious Kālacakra, condensed entirely and without remainder, Vajrasattva, the presiding deity,
The sixteenfold reality through the vajras of the Lord of the Jinas, the single union of method and wisdom:
What the Lord of the Jinas taught for the liberation of beings, and what I too have taught,
By that merit may all beings come to supreme immutable bliss, and may they bow to it.

This concludes the fifth chapter, the chapter on gnosis, of the glorious Kālacakra, drawn from the Ādibuddha in twelve thousand verses.

The king of tantras called the glorious Kālacakra, drawn from the supreme Ādibuddha, is complete.

It was translated, edited and finalized by the Kashmiri paṇḍita Somanātha and the Tibetan translator, the monk Dro Sherab Drak. Later, by the command of the holy lama, the Dharma king, adorned with immeasurable excellent qualities, and at the word of the great official Śākya Zangpo, with the great scholar Shangtön Dodé Pal and the monk Tsultrim Dar, who understood the system of the Kālacakra, carefully examining its meaning and urging the work, the monk Shongtön, learned in the treatises on the grammar of the Sanskrit language, compared it against two Sanskrit manuscripts from Madhyadeśa at the great temple of glorious Sakya, corrected it well, and finalized it.

By the merit of the one who with pure intention
Urged this work and provided favorable conditions,
And whatever merit I have gained through my own effort,
May all realize this and abide on the ground of buddhahood.

Translator's notes

"Kālāgni" renders དུས་མ, taken as the eleventh "planet" paired with Rāhu in the Kālacakra system. The other planets are given by their Tibetan epithets: the son of the hare-marked one is Mercury, the earth-born is Mars, the white one is Venus, the guru is Jupiter, the long-tailed one is Ketu, and the slow one is Saturn. "Six thousand fires" is rendered literally; it probably encodes the 21,600 breaths of a day and night. The phrase "half of four, two hundred thousand" in the cosmological measurements is rendered literally and is ambiguous. "Devoid of the principles" renders དེ་ཉིད་ཀྱིས་དམན་པ (probably Skt. tattva). The hell names Gravel Water and Sand Water are translated from the Tibetan, not identified Sanskrit forms. The heaven names in the fourteenth verse (Good Dharma, Beyond the World, Glorious Eon, White Eon) are likewise translated literally. This passage uses the Indian system of word-numerals, in which words stand for digits. "Corners numbering the oceans" means four corners. "Time and arrow" gives 54, so each age is 5,400 years. "Taste hundreds" in the Tibetan stands for six hundred. "Moon, moon and one," and "mountains, nāgas, planets and directions," are also coded numbers, rendered literally because their intended values are not certain from this text alone.

Several names are translated from their meanings because their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. These are the mountains Blue Light, Jewel Light, Cool Mountain and Vajra; the continents Moon, White Light and Fierce; and the "Word-Arrows" on Meru.

In the last verse the Tibetan reads "gods and gods." This is taken as a slip for "gods and asuras," which is what the parallel list in the preceding verse has.

"Dhru" is left as it stands; it is a unit of time. This passage is astronomical calculation, and the Tibetan states its numbers by symbolic number words: fire 3, sky 0, ocean 4, nāga or serpent 8, hand or eye 2, moon or hare-bearer 1, sun 12, six seasons or tastes 6, mountain or sage 7, element or arrow 5, Veda 4, guṇa 3, Īśvara, Rudra or Hara 11, directions 10, hole 9, Jina 24, tithi 15, lunar mansions 27. Compound number words are read units first (fire-sky-ocean is 403, mountain-nāga-time is 687). I have converted these to numerals. Where a grouping was not certain, as in the table of addends for the signs in the verses on Aries and the Fish, I give the digits one by one in the order of the text. "Hand-crested" was read as thirty-two, taking "crested one" (śikhin) as fire, three; this is uncertain. Technical terms: ghaṭikā (a sixtieth of a day), pala (a sixtieth of a ghaṭikā), bhoga (traversed portion), karaṇa (half lunar day), yoga. "Credit" and "debt" render the additive and subtractive quantities (dhana and ṛṇa). "The short calculation" renders the Tibetan for laghukaraṇa. The repeated "O lord of men" is the Buddha's address to the king. This passage of the astronomical portion of the first chapter expresses numbers with symbolic word-numerals (for example "mountain" for seven, "nāga" for eight), read from right to left. They are given here as plain figures. The resulting periods (687, 87.97, 4,332, 224.7 and 10,766 days for Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus and Saturn) confirm the decoding.

The word-numeral "time, season" is read as six in most places but as three in Venus's slow-equation steps ("five, three and one"), which fits the pattern of the other planets' tables. The figure "two hundred thirty-seven" for the long-tailed ones (Ketu), and "ten, four" for Jupiter's fast-equation steps, are less certain readings of the word-numerals.

"Steps" renders the technical term for the tabulated quarter-divisions (pada). "Slow" and "fast" equations are the manda and śīghra corrections. "Left" and "right" courses correspond to north and south. This section is Kālacakra astronomy written in word-numerals (bhūtasaṃkhyā: arrows 5, nāgas 8, eyes or hands 2, Rudra 11, fire 3, teeth 32, emptiness or sky 0, seasons 6, mountains 7, lunar days 15, wealth 8, Manu 14, intoxicator 13, Victors 24, faults and qualities 3, and so on). For listeners these are resolved into figures, reading compound numbers from right to left as the system requires. Several resolutions were checked arithmetically: 6,250 yojanas times 60 equals 375,000; the ascendant times add up to 1,800 palas, which is 30 ghaṭikās; 21,600 divided by 32 equals 675 surplus breaths; and the planetary periods add up to 48 years and three and a half months. The tabular verses on planetary corrections (stations, roots, debts and wealth, that is, the minus and plus equations) are highly compressed, and their exact grouping is uncertain. In "the long-tailed slow one" the final word could also mean "less." The Tibetan writes "gza' lag"; this is taken as gza' lhag, Mercury. This passage is the astronomical section of the Kālacakra Tantra's first chapter and expresses numbers by the Indian word-numeral system (bhūtasaṃkhyā), which has been kept as in the Tibetan. Common values: void and sky are 0; moon and one are 1; eyes, pair and hand are 2; fire, fire-eater, qualities, worlds and fault are 3; Vedas and ocean are 4; elements and arrows are 5; tastes, seasons and limbs are 6; mountains are 7; Vasus, nāgas and serpents are 8; holes are 9; directions are 10; lords and Rudras are 11; sun is 12; Victors are 24; teeth are 32; asterisms are 27. Digits are usually read from the right. "Nāḍī" renders the time unit ghaṭikā (one-sixtieth of a day) and "pala" is one-sixtieth of a nāḍī. "Motion" renders the technical term bhoga, the portion of the zodiac traversed. "Debt" renders ṛṇa, a subtractive correction. "Quarter" renders pāda. "Maṇḍala" here means a planetary revolution or a disc, not a ritual diagram. The text uses two spellings for Mercury: "lag" in "gza' lag" is taken as a variant of "lhag pa." The bare "lag" in verse 81 is also read as Mercury, although elsewhere it is the number word "hand." "The Auspicious one" probably stands for Maṅgala, which is Mars. This section of the Kālacakra's first chapter is technical astronomy and astrology. Many words are number symbols (bhūtasaṃkhyā) read from the lowest place upward, and they are translated as words, as the text gives them. Empty, sky and space mean 0, the hare-marked one 1, hand and eye 2, quality and fire 3, veda and ocean 4, arrow 5, taste 6, sage 7, nāga and arm-goer (bhujaga, a serpent) 8, and the sun 12. Read this way, the four ages come to 1,728,000, 1,296,000, 864,000 and 432,000 years. These agree with the stated total of twice 2,160,000. Several computational lines are very terse and are translated literally: the "two places," the "goddesses," "pādas," and "serpent and lord" at the end of the eleventh stanza, which are probably number words. Their exact arithmetic sense depends on the Vimalaprabhā commentary. The five classes of lunar day are Nandā, Bhadrā, Jayā, Riktā and Pūrṇā. "The slow one" in the stanza on risings is taken as Saturn (manda), following the earlier rendering, though it could also mean "the weak." This passage belongs to the Kālacakra system of svarodaya and calendrical astrology. Tibetan dus sbyor is rendered "ascendant (lagna)". In the first verse the Tibetan has simply "fire" in the list of planets; following the pairing of left and right planets with the five elements, and the later phrase "Kālāgni" in this same passage, it is taken as Kālāgni. In the verse on the good years, the Tibetan uses code words for numbers: "lunar day" for fifteen, "mountain-moon" for seventeen, and "sun" for twelve. These have been converted to numerals, a reading consistent with the standard 108-year planetary periods. In the list of planets in that verse, the obscure epithet lag 'gro has been taken as Rāhu, by position in the sequence. The phrase "fire, ocean, and arrow" near the end, a numerical code for three, four, and five, is left as it stands, since its exact intended number is uncertain. The phrase "add the people nearby" in the messenger calculation is a literal rendering of an obscure expression. Several terms are astrological or numerical code words. In verse 123, "the sages multiplied by the times" likely encodes the number of lunar mansions (seven times four, twenty-eight). In verse 127, "oceans" usually stands for four, but the value of "times" here is uncertain, so the strut numbers are rendered literally. "Kāla" and "the Dark One" appear to name portions of Rāhu's figure. "The one who goes on his chest" is a serpent epithet, apparently for Rāhu.

In verse 119, the syllable read as "also" after "yakṣa" (Tibetan nga yang) is unclear. It has been taken as a connective, since the four cardinal quarters are already complete.

The catapult terms translated as "cross-pieces" (Tibetan o log) and "screens and frames" (gur, cog) are uncertain technical words. These verses describe war machines and mechanical devices. The text, following the Kālacakra convention, often expresses numbers with symbolic words (seasons for six, faults for three, ocean for four, fire for three, sun for twelve, directions for ten, hands for two). Where the value is clear these have been given as numbers. In verses 141, 142 and 145 the symbolic words and the technical construction details are highly compressed and their exact values and arrangement are uncertain, so some have been kept literal. "The Joy type" and "the lotus type" render what appear to be names of chariot types. "The son of the gods" may refer to the divine craftsman (Viśvakarman), but the text does not name him. Throughout this section the Tibetan uses Sanskrit-style number words. "The sages" stands for seven, "the sun" for twelve, "wealth" (vasu) for eight, "arrows" for five, "qualities" (guṇa) for three, "Veda" for four, "elements" for five, "tastes" for six, and "hand" for two. These have been rendered as numbers where the meaning is reasonably clear: the seventh earth disc, twelve sections, eight petals, twelve lands, five hundred yojanas, the troop figures, and ninety-six kings. In the first line of the Shambhala divisions verse (one, two, three, the oceans and so on) the intended arithmetic is unclear, so the number words were kept literally. The same applies to "seven rays, mountains, planets, sages" in the following verse.

"O Sūrya" (literally "O Sun") is taken as the name of the person addressed, traditionally the sage Sūryaratha. "Ādibuddha" here refers to the root tantra.

The eight avatars listed are those of Viṣṇu; Jāmadagnya is Paraśurāma.

The eight teachers of the barbarian line are traditionally identified as Adam (Ādra), Noah (Anogha), Abraham (Varāhī), Moses (Mūsā), Jesus (Īsā), Mani (Śvetavastrī), Muhammad (Madhumati) and the Mahdī (Mathanī). The forms used follow the Tibetan spellings and the standard Sanskrit equivalents. Makha is Mecca and Bāgada is Baghdad.

"Quicksilver" translates the Tibetan literally, though it is odd in a list of foods. The list of forms in the wisdom-body verse is obscure and has been translated literally; "the man-giver" could be the goddess Naradattā. "Twenty thousand years less two hundred": the Tibetan has "less a hundred hands"; "hand" is read here as the standard code word for the number two, giving two hundred. "Twelve sense bases" renders "sense bases ... sun," taking "sun" as the code number twelve. "The teeth appear" renders "the twice-born" (Sanskrit dvija), a standard word for "tooth". The ten states listed are the ten incarnations of Viṣṇu, from the fish to the Kalkī, here mapped onto the stages of human life. "The lord of the gods" (Sureśa or Surendra) and Brahmā are named as the Kalkī's two sons. "The crooked one" in verse 2 is a celestial body whose identification is uncertain, possibly Ketu. The text uses Sanskrit-style number words, which I have converted: "space, taste, fire" read in reverse order gives 360 joints; "supreme sage" means seventh; "empty, empty, eye" is read as 200; "orifice" means ninth. The figure of 200 channels per day follows the usual right-to-left reading of such number words. The verse on the "core of earth" describes how the elements are layered and support one another. The phrase "the threefold one" for the place of the downward-clearing wind is obscure, and I have rendered it literally. Here "female substance" (rajas) and "mind of awakening" (bodhicitta) are the two generative substances, the female and the male. The text uses Sanskrit number-words throughout: "sun" stands for twelve (so the channels number 6,000 × 12, the traditional seventy-two thousand), "vasu" (Tibetan nor, "wealth") for eight, "fire" for three and "ocean" for four. I have rendered these as numbers where the context is clear: the eight-times-eight-petaled navel lotus, the eight-petaled heart lotus, and hearths of three and four corners. "Manu" in verse 37 is likely also a number-word (fourteen), but I left it as written. "The lotus of space" in the list of six families and "the hairs are all the perfected ones" in verse 35 are literal renderings of obscure phrases. "Gain and debt" render the astronomical terms dhana and ṛṇa, the additive and subtractive corrections. In the list of the ten channels (verse 45), several names are given only in Tibetan renderings that could not be confidently identified with Sanskrit forms. They have been translated literally: "the tawny one" (probably Piṅgalā), "the subtle one," "the Victorious One," "the Mountain-Victorious," and "the Wealthy One." Likewise "glorious Rajas" in verse 50 and "the son of the guru of men" in verse 49 are rendered literally. Verses 51 to 55 use number-words in the calendrical code typical of Kālacakra: fire for three, tastes for six, planets for seven, directions for ten, holes for nine, Vedas and oceans for four, elements for five. These have been kept as words, as the text gives them. "Gone to the five" means death, the dissolution into the five elements. This section of the Kālacakra Tantra uses Indian word-numerals (ocean for four, directions for ten, lord of men for sixteen, teeth for thirty-two, eye or hand for two, moon or hare-bearer for one, mountain or peak-bearer for seven, arrow for five, qualities, fire or world for three, lunar day for fifteen, snake for eight, sky or drop for zero, planet for nine, lunar mansion for twenty-seven, Veda for four, sun for twelve, taste for six). These have been given as numbers where the value is secure; strings of such numerals are given in the order the Tibetan presents them, which in Sanskrit notation are read as digits from right to left. The word "time" used as a numeral (verses 60 and 63) is kept literally because its value is uncertain here; "time-year" renders a recurring technical term. In verse 69 the syllable "dhu" before "hundred" makes no sense as written; it has been read as "nine" (dgu), though the intended figure may simply be thirty-six thousand days (a hundred years of 360 days). In verse 66, "hand-goer" has been taken as a rendering of bhujaga, "snake," that is, eight. The verses on the movement of the moon's digits through the bodily wheels use number-words and lunar-day names ("directions," "ten with nine," "pair of Rudras," "Jina," "Suchness," "Vasus," "seasons," "teeth") whose exact numerical referents are obscure without the Vimalaprabhā; they are rendered literally. Here དབུ་མ refers to the central channel of the subtle body, not the Madhyamaka school. In verses on the agent and the self, རང་བཞིན, ཡོན་ཏན and རྣམ་འགྱུར are used in the Sāṃkhya sense of nature (prakṛti), the qualities (guṇas) and modification (vikṛti), and are rendered accordingly; དེ་ཙམ is rendered "subtle elements" (tanmātra). The final verse is completed in the next piece. "Nature" and "modification" render rang bzhin and rnam 'gyur in their Sāṃkhya sense (prakṛti and vikṛti), which this passage is critiquing. "Four kinds" of animals renders "ocean" (chu gter), read as the numerical symbol for four. "The course of flavors" renders ro yi 'gro ba literally; the exact sense is uncertain. The term 'dren ma, rendered "guide," is also uncertain in this context, as is the "vajra seat and asura seat" in verse 99. The astrological and phonological stanzas here (on the classes of letters, the "feet" of the planets, and the numbers given as "world and eye" and "fire and eye," which are Sanskrit word-numerals) are extremely terse and depend on the Vimalaprabhā commentary; the translation keeps the wording literal. The rendering of "the slow one" (probably Saturn) and "the upper body" for the Tibetan "supreme body" are tentative. Akṣobhya here is a coded term for urine, as the text itself shows by alternating it with "urine" in the same verse. "Flower" is most likely the code for menstrual blood, and the "five lamps" are the five ambrosias. "The three pungents" renders trikaṭu. Vyāghrī is my identification of the Tibetan "the one with the tiger," most likely kaṇṭakārī, whose seed-smoke is a classical remedy for tooth-worms. "Nāga" and "the red substance" in the nose remedy, "the growths on the body," the "thorn" associated with fever, "the fierce sharp-pointed one," and the alchemical "seed," "king" and "sun" (perhaps copper) are rendered literally because their exact referents are uncertain. "Sulfur" renders the Tibetan mu zi. This passage is a highly technical alchemical and perfumery section in which many substances are named by code words or bare transliterations (for example sky-born, deer-born, the cool, the crooked, the navel, the moon, rabbit-bearer for camphor, hand-born for nails). Where the Tibetan transliterates or the code is clear, the substance name is given; otherwise the code word is kept literally, since identifications are often uncertain. Stanza 129 gives the proportions in the Indian word-numeral system (eye 2, moon 1, fire 3, ocean or water-treasure 4, arrow 5, quality 3, taste 6, hand 2, worlds 3, time a numeral of uncertain value), twenty-five values for a five-by-five grid. In stanza 127 the word rendered "base metal" is literally "servant," apparently a code term. "City of the joined squares" for an obscure phrase and "ripened for the teeth" in stanza 134 are tentative. The verse on the diagram for childbirth uses the Kālacakra code-numbers: mountain 7, sun 12, moon 1, Manu 14, eye 2, desire 13, vasu 8, Rudra 11, king 16, fire 3, directions 10, elements 5, apertures 9, six 6, lunar day 15, ocean 4. These form a four-by-four magic square, each row adding to 34, written as "ocean (4) and the crested one, that is fire (3)", read in the Indian order with units first. "On the back of a measure" is a literal rendering of an unclear phrase, perhaps a plate or measuring vessel. "Navel-scent" is musk, here an artificial musk. How the numbers in the verse on the child-seizing mothers ("three times nine, and ten, and eleven, and the others three fifths") map onto the Womb, Day, Month and Year Mothers is not clear from the verse; it is translated as it stands. "Hand-goers" is literal for an uncertain class of beings. "Sīkaṃnya" and "Lāmās of fumigation" could not be identified with confidence and are kept close to the text. "In the water" is taken as the western direction, the quarter of water (Varuṇa). "Washed with the seven" is read as seven washing substances, water and the five nectars among them; the construction is terse. The "nine doubled" in verse 155 is a numerical idiom for the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha, and "the four truths" renders a number word (Veda, meaning four) used before "truths." "Tājikas" and "Rahmāna" refer to Islam as known to the Kālacakra. "The Jinas" in verse 159 refers to the Jain tradition, not the Buddha. The doxographic lines of verse 167 are compressed, and the reading of the last line, "where the other positions fail, remains unfailing," is somewhat uncertain. Verse 168 targets the views of the Tāyins (Tibetan "stag gzig," the Persian or Muslim "barbarians" of the Kālacakra), verse 169 the Cārvāka materialists ("those beautiful on this side"), and verse 170 the Jain doctrines of a body-sized soul, liberation at the top of the world, and atoms. In verse 172 the clause about the holy ones and "the words of the deluded" is compressed, and its exact construal is uncertain. In verse 7 of chapter three, the Tibetan "nor gyi blo gros" ("wealth-mind") most likely renders Sanskrit vasumatī, "earth," and is translated that way. The four smells are taken as rotten, fishy ("water-born"), lotus-scented and supremely fragrant, matching the four classes. The pairing of the tastes with the classes in that line is compressed in the Tibetan. The text uses Indian number-symbol words (for example "ocean" for four, "eyes" for two, "fire" for three, "sun" for twelve, "Victors" for twenty-four, "arrows" for five, with "space" for zero). Where the value was not secure, as with "time" and "arrows," they are kept as given. Vajravega's arms thus number twenty-six. In verse 9 the Tibetan reads "lord of fire" (me bdag). This is taken as a slip for "lord of men" (mi bdag), the address to the king used elsewhere in this passage. Verse 18 writes lda for lnga; it is read as "five." In verse 16 the directions are named by their guardians: Yama for south, Varuṇa (the water lord) for west, Vāyu (the wind) for northwest, Kubera (the yakṣa) for north, Rudra (the Fierce One) for northeast, Indra for east, and Agni for southeast. The identifications of indrākṣa and putrañjīva (dbang mig, lung tang) among the rosary materials are tentative, as is "cooling stem" (bsil) among the pens. The Kālacakra uses symbolic number words, here rendered as numbers: "seasons" (six), "tastes" (six), "Vasus" or "wealth" (eight), "lord of men" (sixteen), "sun" (twelve), "Manu" (fourteen), "ocean" (four), "hare-marked one" or moon (one). The grammar of the measurements in verses 36 to 39 is compressed, and some figures, such as "six and ninety" lines and "six and six," are uncertain in their exact application. "Cross-bar" renders ཡོངས་འཇོམས, taken as Skt. parigha, an omen and a line-crossing in the diagram; the battle simile in verse 34 is obscure. In verse 26, ཁྱོན is read as ཁྱོད "you." "The terrors of death" in verse 28 is literal and probably denotes bones or similar remains. "A day of the Victor's class" refers to the jayā group of lunar days. This passage uses the Indian system of encoded numerals. Following standard values, "sun" is twelve, "Manu" fourteen, "hooded ones" (nāgas) eight, "lord of victors" twenty-four, "ocean" four, "taste" six, "time" three, and "fault" (doṣa) three; "peak-bearer" was taken as seven (mountain). The numeric sequence in the final verses is very compressed, and its exact distribution among the listed components is uncertain. "Moon" renders the epithet "rabbit-bearer." In verse 47, "together with Kubera" renders ནོར་སྦྱིན (Dhanada), and "at the water" (ཆུར) may indicate a side or direction; both readings are uncertain. "Bākulī" and "carcikā" are architectural terms kept as transliterations. Direction names follow the tantra's convention: "the god" is Indra in the east, Agni the southeast, Yama the south, the asura the southwest, the waters or Lord of the Waters Varuṇa in the west, Vāyu the northwest, Kubera or the yakṣa the north, Hara or Rudra or Īśāna the northeast. "The empty" (śūnya) here seems to denote the anusvāra or bindu. "Three times twelve" renders the Tibetan "three-fold suns", the sun standing for the number twelve, which gives the thirty-six desire goddesses. Several identifications are uncertain: "the corpse-faced one" in the verse on the ṭa class is a literal rendering; "Gaṇapati" renders the Tibetan "of the assembly"; "the Enemy of the Asuras" may refer to Indra or Viṣṇu; "those at the fords" in the list of rite components is a literal rendering of an obscure phrase; and "northern" for the hearth shape may stand for some other shape term. "Twice that again is the portion of the rims": the Tibetan has "the hundredth part of the rims," which does not fit the measurements; "hundred" (brgya) may be a slip for "breadth" (rgya). "Four lotus hands" takes "time" (dus) in its Kālacakra numerical sense of four, which matches the four implements listed. "Pacification" reads ཞིབ as a slip for ཞི་བ. "With the six endings" interprets the obscure རོ་རྐང, taking "taste" (ro) as the numerical code for six. "Camphor" renders ཟླ ("moon"), which may instead mean silver. The opening line of verse 79 ("it is to be spoken before their names") is terse in the Tibetan; it presumably refers to a syllable such as oṃ placed before each deity's name. "Bringing down the fragrance" in the last line is a literal rendering, and its sense is uncertain. The final verses count the root downfalls with Kālacakra code words for numbers: the hare-bearer (moon) is one, "other" two, the arrow five, the mountains seven, the nāga eight, the directions ten, and Raudra eleven. I have added the plain ordinal beside each so the sequence is clear when heard. The opening of the vase-bathing verse ("with golden vases set with jewels, eight and four") is uncertain: "nāga and four" may be a numeric code (nāga = 8), and the exact count or reading intended is unclear. "Tasting the buddhas" is kept literally; in this tantra's code it most likely refers to the sacramental nectars. This passage makes heavy use of the Kālacakra system of number words, in which ordinary words stand for numbers: the sun for twelve, Manu for fourteen, nāga and wealth (vasu) for eight, the directions for ten, Jina for twenty-four, space for zero, taste for six, qualities for three, and so on. Where the value was clear I have given it ("the twelfth," "eighty," "eight-petaled," "the eight nāga kings," "the seven oceans"). Several are left close to the wording because their exact value and reading are uncertain: "eight and four vases" in verse 104 (possibly twelve, or forty-eight by right-to-left reading), "the nāgas" binding the crown there, "the Jina measure" in verse 108, "space, taste and qualities" in verse 113 (probably 360), and the coded ages and numbers in verse 118 ("the moving ocean together with taste," "six and four," "the wrathful ones, the bhūtas and the asuras"). The Vimalaprabhā commentary should be consulted for these. "What is held for worship" translates a term for a ritual implement whose identity is not certain here. In verse 130 the bare word "Earth" most likely stands for Locanā, the goddess of the earth element, here assigned to the vaiśya caste; this identification is interpretive.

In verse 126, the crested one is read as fire, in line with its use elsewhere in the text as a name of Agni.

The names of the ten wrathful goddesses in verses 132 to 133 are reconstructed from their standard Tibetan renderings: Jambhanī, Stambhanī, Mānī, Atibalā, Bhṛkuṭī, Śṛṅkhalā, Raudrākṣī and Atinīlā. "The very blue one" is understood as Atinīlā.

Oḍḍa and nāda are kept as technical terms of the yogic process. The caste rendered "trickster woman" is a literal reading of an unidentified term; its Sanskrit equivalent is uncertain. In the numbering of the thirty-six, the word for "wealth" stands for the number eight (vasu): four, six, eight, ten and eight make thirty-six, with the one sovereign beyond them. The word rendered "secret region" in the descriptions of the women and of the deer literally means "region" or "domain" and is taken as referring to the genital area; for the lion, "without a homeland" renders the same word in "free of region." The "Water-Goer" in verse 140 appears to correspond to Śaṅkhinī in the following descriptions. In the first verse the line on the mudrā and samaya is compressed; I take it to mean that any consort whatsoever, even one lacking the commitments, may be taken for the drinking, in contrast to the preceding warning about drinking without a mudrā. In the second verse the phrases "the vajra-staff of the afflictions," "the folk of the beasts," and "the giver of fear" appear to be cryptic designations whose referents are uncertain. Several words in this passage are numerical code words of Indian verse: ནོར (Vasu) stands for eight, and "the ocean" stands for four, giving the counts of the groups listed. Many of the animal and bird names are transliterated or rare Tibetan terms whose exact species are uncertain (for example the dhāraṇī creature, iṣu, the horpa, gudmu, khabuk-ki, bhetrāghāra, the enemy of deer, the enemy of trees); these have been given literally or as they stand. "The Stupefier" renders རྨུགས་བྱེད, a goddess name not securely identified (possibly Stambhī or Jambhī). "Counted as the oceans": the Tibetan says "the goddesses, the oceans," using "ocean" as a code-word for the number four (likewise "ocean" twice for the four right abandonments and four bases of power, making eight). Mars, Mercury and Jupiter render "earth-born," "moon-born" and "the mantra-man"; the last is the usual Kālacakra epithet of Jupiter (Bṛhaspati, the gods' counselor) but is somewhat uncertain. "The one called the Fortunate" may refer to a Bhāgavata devotee; uncertain. "The outcaste woman and the outcaste woman" is repeated as in the Tibetan, probably naming two grades of low-born consort. Samāja here denotes the pīṭha category "assembly sites" (melāpaka). The verb rendered "Go!" is uncertain; from the gesture with the back of the hand it is taken as a dismissal. The "water creature" is probably the makara emblem. Verses 189 to 197 list the secret signs (chommā) by which a yogin and a female messenger (dūtī) communicate through gestures and glances; the quoted words give the meaning of each sign. In verse 200 the phrase rendered "the paths beginning with the cow" (Tibetan ba la sogs) is obscure, and its intended sense is uncertain. In verse 203, dbang po is taken here as Indra, i.e. harm from the sky such as lightning, rather than as a name used elsewhere; this reading is uncertain. The verses describing the deity use the Kālacakra numerical code (moon 1, pair 2, fire 3, ocean or Veda 4, sun 12, Victor 24, wealth 8, and "empty, six, fire" read in reverse as 360); these have been rendered as plain numbers. "Faces of time" is read as the code word yuga for four. In verse five, the syllable written "rnam mag" is taken as a slip for "rnam dag" ("pure"). The line about the hare-marked one on the lotus hands is obscure; it is translated literally. "Moving in water" in the list of left-hand implements is taken as the conch, in keeping with the standard Kālacakra iconography. The verses use Sanskrit numerical code words: "Vasus" for eight, "Vedas" for four, "suns" for twelve, "oceans" for four, "fires" for three, "seasons" for six. The numbers are made explicit in the translation. In verses 25, 28 and 29, ནོར་འདབ is read as "eight-petaled" (vasu, eight). In verse 25, ཆུ་གཏེར་གདོང་མ is read as "Four-Faced," that is, Brahmāṇī, rather than as "Lord of the Waters." Likewise "Six-Faced Goddess" is Kaumārī, "she who rides the lord of birds" is Vaiṣṇavī, and the "Vajra-Handed Goddess" is Aindrī.

In verse 16, "the hare-bearer" in a list of scents is taken as camphor, and "intoxicant" as musk; this is an interpretation. In verse 20, the phrase rendered "the lotus is the lesser" (ཆུ་སྐྱེས་དམན) is uncertain; it may instead mean that the lotus is the seal of the female partner. The closing phrase ལྷག་པའི་ལྷ་ཅན ("who has the presiding deity") is also uncertain.

The names of the sixty-four yoginīs are given as English translations wherever the Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. Urvaśī, Rambhā and Citralekhā are kept as known apsaras names. Many of the goddesses' names here are Tibetan translations of Sanskrit names that cannot be identified with confidence (for example Lotus-Eyed, the Water-Born One, the Indicating One, Star-Eye, Vajra-Eye, the Very Blue One); these are translated by meaning. "The Faced One" (verse 32) renders a single word, "face," in a list of twelve gods; it may stand for Brahmā, but this is uncertain. The phrase rendered "the lunar days of Caitra and the other months" contains an obscure element (literally "wealth-hand"), left unrendered as its sense is unclear. "The one who goes to the citadel, with wrath" (verse 40) is a literal rendering of an unclear phrase. "Pāñcajanya" interprets "the conch born of the fifth," Viṣṇu's conch. In the description of Vajravega, the text uses the Indian convention of number symbols: "sun" stands for twelve, "Jina" for twenty-four, and "Veda" for four. I have rendered the resulting numbers explicitly, giving him twelve eyes, twenty-six arms and four faces.

The phrase "le tshe brgyad" is unclear. I have rendered it simply as "the eight places".

I identified the consort "mtha' yas brtson 'grus ma" only by meaning, so I have translated her name as "Infinite Vigor".

The figures named for the gates (Atibala, Stambhakī, Jambhaka, Mānakī, Mānaka, Jambhakī) are reconstructed from their standard Tibetan equivalents.

"The son of Danu" denotes the southwestern, asura quarter. Here རིགས་ལྡན names one of the eight great nāgas, so it is rendered Kulika rather than Kalkī, its sense elsewhere in this text. The Tibetan names for Anantavīrya, the "goddess with the face of Time" (dus kyi gdong ma) and the "Lightning Goddess" (glog ma) could not be matched to their Sanskrit forms with confidence, so the latter two are translated by meaning. The directional names (the God, the Crested One, the lord of the asuras, the yakṣa, Paśupati) refer to the quarters of the corresponding world guardians. "Lotus-holder" in the crown verses refers to the buddha of the lotus family (Amitābha), and "lord of jewels" to Ratnasambhava. Two numbers are given in the Tibetan as symbolic number words, following the Kālacakra convention: "Vasus transformed by nāgas" (eight by eight, sixty-four) and "emptiness, six and fire" (read right to left as three hundred and sixty, the days of the year). They are translated as numbers.

In the line on Atibala and Stambhakī, the Tibetan reads "me sta". This is taken as a copyist's slip for "ma ste".

"The Supremely Vigorous Lady" and "Infinite Vigor" translate names whose Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. "The Ocean-Faced One" is likewise a literal rendering.

The vocative "O lord of men" addresses the king, Sucandra, to whom the tantra is taught. The figure rendered "the Supremely Diligent One" could not be confidently identified with a Sanskrit name and is translated literally. The phrase rendered "the lunar days designated Vasu and Hand" is obscure; it may be a numerical code (Vasu standing for eight, hand for two) rather than names. In verse 101, "doubled" and "the eight multiplied by the nāgas" appear to give petal counts for the throat and navel wheels (thirty-two and sixty-four), and "ninety multiplied by time" likewise appears to be a numerical expression for the joints. "Lord of men" is taken as the usual vocative address to King Sucandra. The opening line (the end of verse 101) is obscure; "is not the sun" renders the Tibetan as it stands. "Sāga" in verse 108 is an unidentified posture name and is kept as written. "Six families" in verse 113 takes "taste" as the conventional number-word for six. In verse 115 the "central channel" renders the term usually meaning Madhyamaka, which here denotes the central nāḍī. The fourth line of verse 125 is difficult; "the states of Manmatha" takes yid srubs as the standard rendering of Manmatha (Kāma), and the closing rhetorical question is rendered literally. In verse 126, "go, ku and the rest" are the coded abbreviations of the five meats, here reinterpreted as the senses. In verse 124 the "big toe" and "toes" render terms that can also mean thumb and fingers; the body-wide movement of the moon from foot to crown favors toes. Numbers are given here in the code-word system of the Kālacakra. "Jina plus two" is rendered as twenty-six, taking Jina as twenty-four, and "Veda faces" as four faces. The opening phrase of verse 131, "arising in excess of the directions," is translated literally, and its exact reference among the deity's arms is uncertain. "The horse's enemy" is taken as the buffalo, the traditional enemy of the horse in Indian lore and Yama's mount. The "fierce goddesses" render a term that may designate the class of caṇḍālīs in the maṇḍala retinue. "Totality" renders zad par (Skt. kṛtsna), the meditative totalities of earth, wind, fire, water and space. The "night of the bhūtas" and the "day of Manu" are calendrical code terms; in Kālacakra number symbolism Manu stands for fourteen, so both very likely mean the fourteenth lunar day. "Preta" in verse 161 refers to the corpse used in the rite; the phrasing of the hand, lotus and sword there is terse, and its arrangement is uncertain. "The touch" among the eight worldly accomplishments is literal; its precise referent (possibly a transmuting touch) is uncertain. "The lord of elixirs" is mercury. "The corpse-possessing spirit" translates ro 'dzin literally. Here རིགས་ལྡན clearly names the nāga Kulika (one of the eight great nāgas, listed with Ananta), not Kalkī, the Shambhala king, so it is rendered Kulika. "Akṣobhya" in the pill recipe is very likely a code for a bodily substance, probably urine, in the tantric correspondence of the five buddhas with the five ambrosias. Several words in the astrological and maṇḍala verses are Sanskrit-style number words, such as space for zero, Vedas for four, oceans for four, arrow for five, mountain for seven, wealth (vasu) for eight, opening for nine, and teeth for thirty-two. They are translated literally, and their exact numerical values are uncertain. "The day-maker" is probably the arka plant, and kumārī (literally "young") is probably aloe. "Borax" renders ṭaṃgaṇa (ṭaṅkaṇa). The yantra instructions in this passage are terse and technical; the placements of syllables (for example "from that yo," "becomes ma") follow the Tibetan closely, but their exact layout depends on the full ritual diagram. In the sacrifice verse, the Tibetan animal terms rendered "buffalo" and the phrase "that goes to" are uncertain; the verse reinterprets the Vedic animal sacrifices and the soma drink in terms of inner yoga. "The end of the twelve" refers to the yogic point twelve finger-widths beyond the body (dvādaśānta). The opening lines, completing verse 202, are terse and their syntax is uncertain. "Guardian of existence" renders the Tibetan literally; it may be an epithet of Viṣṇu as sustainer, but this cannot be confirmed from the passage. "Equal bliss" renders a term apparently meaning bliss of sameness or evenness. In verse 210, the phrase "because of their lowliness and their supremacy" is literal, and its precise sense is uncertain. The "gift of desire" and "secret gift" refer to the tantric offering of a consort, and "the circle" refers to the gaṇacakra or tantric feast. Verse 207 assigns the four seals (mudrās) to classes of women, and this translation follows the text exactly as written. In the verse on the gift of the consort, the Tibetan does not state the result explicitly ("swiftly, in this very birth, for the yogin..."); the implied sense is that the yogin attains awakening in this life, but the translation keeps the elliptical wording. "The lord of obstructors" renders the Tibetan literally; it likely refers to Vināyaka or Gaṇeśa as lord of the vighnas. The eighth hell is Avīci. In the verse beginning "Even without meditative concentration," the Tibetan literally says "having abandoned union with a woman"; read in the light of the Kālacakra's preference for the mahāmudrā over an external consort, the all-pervading lord, the mahāmudrā, grants the siddhi without such union. This sits uneasily with the preceding verse on the necessity of union, and the Sanskrit may intend "apart from." "The lotus hand of the hare-marked one" is the lunar, that is the left, hand. "The sun's doors" is rendered "twelve doors," following the usual Kālacakra number code (sun = 12). The verses use the Kālacakra system of numerical code words. "Flavor" (rasa) stands for six, so "the six multiplied by six" renders "flavor transformed by flavor" (thirty-six). Likewise "teeth" stands for thirty-two, "ocean" for four, "sun" for twelve, and "moon" for one: Heruka has four faces and twelve arms (presumably twelve on each side, giving the twenty-four arms of Kālacakra), and the gate figures are two-armed and single-faced. The vowel-number calculation in verse 8 is translated as written, without attempting to reconcile its arithmetic.

Several deity identifications follow the usual Kālacakra maṇḍala pattern rather than an explicit Sanskrit form. These are Mānaka (Tibetan "Pride"), Jambhaka, Stambhaka, Atibala, Viṣkambhin, Bhṛkuṭī, Dhātvīśvarī, Raudrī and Śṛṅkhalā. Some are uncertain, especially Mānaka and the "supreme lord of men."

The epithets "the goddess who travels by horse chariot" and "the time-faced one" are translated literally, because no secure Sanskrit name could be identified for them. This passage uses the Kālacakra system of numerical code words. The code words have been rendered as plain numbers. Veda stands for four, suns for twelve, seasons and tastes for six, Agni for three, oceans for four, and apertures for nine. Where the code is ambiguous, the most natural reading was chosen. "Twice four" renders "ocean pair," which could also be read as twenty-four. "Six multiplied by four" renders "seasons struck by oceans." The line on laying out the maṇḍala, "six lines and nine portions, by those dwelling in the maṇḍala of wind," is especially uncertain.

The object in Jambhala's hand (Tibetan so ba) is uncertain; "grain" is a tentative rendering.

"Tantra of yogic understanding" is a literal rendering of a title whose Sanskrit original could not be identified with confidence.

The figure trampled with Māra, Tibetan dbang ldan, is taken here as Īśvara (Rudra/Śiva), in line with Kālacakra iconography, rather than as the directional guardian Īśāna. This passage is dense with the Kālacakra numerical code, in which words such as "taste" (six), "mountain" (seven), "flames" (three) and "Lord of the Waters" (ocean, four) stand for numbers; they are translated literally to keep consistency with earlier pieces. "Six realms of beings" in verse 38 renders a phrase literally reading "the goers of taste," taken as "six (rasa) courses of beings"; this is uncertain. The name of the goddess "Subduing Dissolution" in verse 36 is translated from the Tibetan and not securely identified. "Anuyoga" renders a term literally meaning "subsequent knowledge of yoga"; its exact Sanskrit equivalent is uncertain. The term rendered "central channel" here is the same word as "Middle Way" later in the passage. "O kings" (ས་བོན་རྒྱལ་པོ་དག་གི་སྐྱེད་བྱེད) takes རྒྱལ་པོ་དག as a vocative, as the Kālacakra frequently addresses the king; it could also be read as "the producer of the kings of seeds." "Taste and supreme taste" (རོ་དང་མཆོག་གི་རོ) is rendered literally; in the six-element scheme these correspond to space and gnosis. "Source of Bliss" (བདེ་འབྱུང) is rendered as an English epithet because its Sanskrit form is uncertain. Several of the attributions to faces, such as "the supremely white face," are kept as the Tibetan gives them. "The image of the sun" (ཉི་མའི་གཟུགས) in the stanza on the seals is rendered literally; it likely refers to the visualized gnosis consort, but the exact sense is uncertain. "Engaging both the proper and the improper" renders རིགས་དང་རིགས་མིན་མངོན་སྤྱོད, which could also mean conduct regardless of family or caste. "The flows of rasa" renders རོ་ཡི་འགྲོ་བ, which may refer to the rasanā channel. "The six classes" (སྡེ་པ་དྲུག) is kept literal. The numbers in the last line of the first verse and in the verse on the twenty-year periods are given in the symbolic numeral words of the Tibetan: sky and void stand for zero, season for six, and the Vedas for four; read in the usual reverse order, "void and the Vedas" is forty and "sky and season" sixty. The exact ages intended in the first verse are not certain. "Lord of Jewels" (Ratneśa) in the second verse refers to the onset of the menses, associated with Ratnasambhava; in the later verse it is the deity himself. The line "This is for one endowed with a wisdom consort; then for one who is alone; and again the Buddha's body proclaimed the word" is obscure, and the rendering is tentative. "Victor of the Pledge" translates a name I could not identify with confidence; it corresponds to the deluded, the family usually associated with Vairocana. In the first verse the text reads "battle" (gyul); since the line pairs it with "subjects" (yul can), the reading "objects" (yul) has been followed as an evident slip. In verse 100 the line naming "Desire," Hara and "the host of vajras, the trees and the rest" is obscure; it has been rendered literally. In verse 97, "Āryas" (Tibetan 'phags pa) is taken as a people, listed beside Tibetans, rather than as "noble ones." The wrathful deities in verses 107 and 108 are identified from their standard Tibetan renderings (Atibala, Jambhaka, Stambhaka, Mānaka, Nīladaṇḍa, Acala, Sumbha, Uṣṇīṣa). In verse 108 "endless vigor" may instead name a further wrathful deity; it is rendered here as describing Acala. "The Great Dog-Faced Goddess" in verse 109 is a literal translation; the Sanskrit name could not be identified with confidence. In verse 114 "sky-light" corresponds to the third of the ten signs, usually given as fireflies. The line in verse 116 about the bliss produced by touch is obscure, and the rendering is tentative. "Oṭḍā" in verse 117 is a transliterated technical term whose exact sense is uncertain. Verses 127 to 129 use Indian symbolic number-words ("foot", "tastes", "arrows", "mountains", "sky", "age", "moon", "eyes"), read from the units upward. They are given here as numerals: 56¼ (foot, tastes, arrows), 675 (arrows, mountains, tastes), 5,400 (sky, sky, age, arrows) and 21,600 (sky, sky, six, moon, eyes), the classical Kālacakra daily breath count. The 5,400 reading takes "age" (yuga) as four. "Lords of men" and "twice eight" in verse 131, and "four times" (literally "Veda times") in verse 132, may also be number-words; they are rendered literally. In verse 128 ཕྱི་རྣམས is read as ཕྱེ་རྣམས "divided", following the parallel line in verse 129. "The blazing one" and "the purifying one" in verse 121 are fire. "Empty ones" may also mean zeros. In verse 121, མི་བདག is taken as a vocative to the king, as is common in this tantra. Much of this passage uses the Indian system of numerical code words (bhūtasaṃkhyā), in which words stand for numbers: eyes and hand for 2, fire for 3, Vedas and oceans for 4, arrows and objects for 5, tastes, seasons and wheels for 6, mountains for 7, serpents for 8, apertures and planets for 9, sky and emptiness for 0, and so on. These words are kept as words in the translation because the text uses them that way. Where planets appear in such lists, their exact numerical values are not decoded here. In the ritual-watch stanza, the "hand," "fire," "oceans," "mountain" and "serpent" indicate the second, third, fourth, seventh and eighth watches. "Smoke and dust" likely refer to the qualities tamas and rajas, alongside sattva. "The Auspicious One," heading the vajra ḍākas, renders the Tibetan literally, since the deity intended could not be identified with confidence. The motions "slow and six-footed" are rendered literally. The calendrical verses use word-numerals, kept literally here and read in reverse order: sky, space and void are zero, eyes two, fire three, oceans and lakes four, arrows five, tastes and seasons six, mountains seven, serpents and vasus eight, moon one. Thus "sky, sky, space, sky, eyes, fire and the oceans" is 4,320,000. Saturn's figure is 144,000, Rāhu's 240,000, Maṅgala's 28,800,000 and the Moon's 51,840,000. The value of "seasons" in the line on the minister of the god of time is assumed to be six. That figure and the identity of that minister are uncertain. Maṅgala here is the planet Mars, and "the kings" are the planets. In the navel-wheel line, "hand-goers" (serpents) presumably stands for eight and "lords of men" for sixteen. Verses 169 to 175 use Indian symbolic number words (bhūtasaṃkhyā), kept here as the text gives them. Their usual values are: arrows five, seasons six, Vedas four, sun twelve, lords of men (kings) sixteen, Manu fourteen, principles twenty-five, the Lord of the Waters (ocean) four, space zero, eyes two, sages seven, digits of the moon sixteen. So "a sun and a half" is twelve and a half finger-widths, "the arrows multiplied by ten" is fifty thousand yojanas, "a hundred thousand times the Vedas" is four hundred thousand yojanas for the wind maṇḍala, and "the seasons and nine" read in reverse order gives ninety-six finger-widths, the human body, against the hundred and twenty of the buddha body. Some of these sequences of measurements are compressed, and the way each value maps to a body part is not certain.

"Katana" probably stands for karketana, a yellow gem. "Acala" renders མི་གཡོ; it could also be understood as Akṣobhya. "Vajra goddesses of taste" renders རོ་ཡི་རྡོ་རྗེ, probably Rasavajrā and her companions. Many numbers in these verses are given by the Indian symbolic number-words: "ocean" for four, "arrow" for five, "nāga lords" for eight, "sun" for twelve, "Vedas" for four, "season" for six. The reading of "season" as six (rather than three) is not certain. In the measurements of the stūpa, "side portion" renders a term that may instead be a numerical fraction, since "direction" can also stand for ten. In verse 182 the text reads "very degenerate like the ground"; this is evidently a slip for "very level like the ground," which is the reading of the parallel line in verse 183, and I have translated it that way. In verse 191, "the Hooded One" in the list of planets probably stands for Ketu or Kālāgni, but the identification is not certain. In verse 188, the phrase rendered "here where the moon and Meru are complete" is obscure; "moon" could also mean "month." In verse 179, the sense of "the things borne aloft for worship" is uncertain. The alchemical verses (from "The combustible one is earth" onward) use specialized materia-medica terms whose exact identifications are uncertain. "The combustible one," "the moving one" (likely mercury), "zetsa salt," "the three curved knives," "the thorn-born" iron, "the black heap" (likely kālakūṭa poison) and "transmutes by the spear" are rendered literally. The correlation of minerals with the elements in the line on kākṣī, kāśīsa, sulfur, realgar and orpiment is an interpretation of compressed syntax. "Single caste" refers to the Kalki's unification of the sages into one vajra family. "The Lord of the World" is the speaker's self-designation. This passage belongs to the Kālacakra's treatment of alchemy (rasāyana), where "elixir" (rasa) refers chiefly to mercury. Several mineral and technical terms are uncertain. "Pyrite" renders lhang tsher, taken as mākṣika. "Realgar" for gsha' tshe is a tentative identification. "Monkey fat" for spra tshil is literal. "Swing apparatus" for khyogs is taken as the dolāyantra. "Placement" for dgod pa, "the double salt," "the great one," and "without an opening" (kha med) are literal renderings of technical terms whose precise referents are unclear. The "vessel of the moon" and "the Sun and the hare-marked one" are taken as silver and gold, following alchemical usage. This passage is alchemical (rasāyana and metal transmutation), and many mineral and plant terms are uncertain. "Sharp gold," "nāga," "the moon" (probably silver or camphor), "the sky" (probably mica, abhra), gśa' tshe, lhang tsher skyes, dmar ring and pokkāṇa are given literally or in transliteration because their exact identity is unclear. "Drawn out by the vowels" is literal; the vowels may stand for the number sixteen. "A month together with the lord of men" may be a numerical code, since "lord of men" (nṛpa) can stand for sixteen. "Those called fields upon the earth" may refer to the guardians of the sacred fields (kṣetrapālas). The phrase translated "overpower violently" literally reads "give fierce victory." Here དབུ་མ is the central channel (madhyamā), not the Madhyamaka school. Verses 235 to 242 use the Indian convention of number words, where objects stand for numbers (directions for ten, apertures for nine, the twice-born, meaning teeth, for thirty-two); these have been translated literally. In the verse beginning "Whoever in the world of humans," the clause rendered "to rage at him with the fire of wrath is what is called the great wrath" is syntactically uncertain in the Tibetan. "O Sucandra" takes ཟླ་བ་བཟང as an address to the king to whom the tantra was taught. "At the time of Ananta" renders མཐའ་ཡས་དུས; it may instead mean "at the endless time." Its exact technical sense in the Kālacakra yoga of the winds is uncertain.

"Candra's request" refers to the request of King Sucandra, who received the root tantra.

The "tantra of few vowels and consonants" is the condensed tantra, the Laghutantra. Its speaker here is Kulika Yaśas, addressing the sage Sūrya ("the Sun") and his son Puṇḍarīka.

In the verse beginning "O Sun, you will be a lord of men," the syntax is compressed, and the reading of the first line, and of "the one with the white lotus" as Puṇḍarīka identified with Lokanātha (Avalokiteśvara), is somewhat uncertain.

ས་ག is rendered as the month of Vaiśākha.

The commentary Puṇḍarīka is told to compose is traditionally the Vimalaprabhā.

The Dharma king in the colophon is not named in the text.
